# Chapter 1: Using This Book

{{Epigraph
*"Know your tools. Half the fools I've buried died because they trusted a blade they'd never sharpened."*
}}
{{EpigraphAuthor
— Master Armorer Aelric of the Eastern Guilds
}}

## About This Book

{{CoreBody
The Encyclopedia Practitioner: Provisioning is a working reference for everyone at the table who has ever had to pause a game and ask "how much does that cost?" or "what happens if I hit it with a sledgehammer?" It catalogues the arms, armor, mounts, vehicles, alchemy, food, drink, and everyday gear of a pre-industrial fantasy setting, with enough mechanical detail to use mid-session and enough flavor to read in an armchair.
}}

{{CoreBody
The book is built to be system-agnostic. Each item carries generic stats that translate cleanly into three major families of rules: those using armor-as-a-health-buffer mechanics, those using armor-class targeting, and those using armor-as-damage-reduction. Conversions are consolidated in Appendix A so the main text stays uncluttered.
}}

## What You Will Find

{{CoreBulleted
- Weapons and armor, historical and fantastical, treated as equals
- A full crafting and repair system with material costs, labor time, and hard economic limits
- Mounts, land vehicles, ships, and siege engines
- Early firearms and artillery
- Alchemy, brewing, food, poisons, and every other consumable worth tracking
- The adventuring sundries that keep a dungeoneer alive — rope, spikes, lanterns, ten-foot poles, and the rest
- Tournament rules, including the economics of ransom
- A framework for enchanted and legendary craft, so the GM can build named items rather than pick them from a catalog
- Appendices of conversion tables, price lists, scavenging results, and a settlement-based market system
}}

## What You Will Not Find

{{CoreBulleted
- A catalog of named magic items. Chapter 19 provides a process, not a price list.
- Stat blocks for creatures or NPCs. This is a book about the things they carry and the things they fight with.
- Spells, classes, or character options tied to any particular system. The book names generic abilities and lets your system supply the mechanics.
}}

\column

## Reading an Item Entry

{{CoreBody
Most equippable items in this book follow a consistent short-form entry. Once you have read three of them, you have read them all.
}}

{{note
##### Sample Entry

**Longsword** — *Heavy one-handed blade, 36" to 44" length*

{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 4 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 90 gp
Traits: Versatile, Balanced
Variants: Arming sword, bastard sword
}}

A balanced cut-and-thrust blade suitable for foot or mounted combat. Its length rewards reach without sacrificing control; its cross-guard gives the Balanced trait and a small parry advantage when used two-handed.
}}

{{CoreBody
Each entry has five fixed parts.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Name and Brief.** A one-line descriptor giving rough size, form, and purpose.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Stat Block.** Damage tier (for weapons), protection tier (for armor), weight, bulk, cost, and any item-specific traits.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Prose.** One or two paragraphs of practical description — how the item is used, who tends to carry it, and any quirks a buyer should know about.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Variant Forms.** When a single entry covers several close cousins, the variants are named inline with any stat adjustments.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Sidebar Notes.** Where relevant: fantasy variants, scaling notes for divergent systems, curiosities, or optional rules.
}}

\page

## The Tier System

{{CoreBody
Rather than commit to a single set of damage dice or armor values, the body text of this book uses tiers. Each tier is a bucket; your game system's entry in Appendix A tells you what that bucket translates to in dice, armor class, or armor points. Full conversions live there; what follows is enough to read the book.
}}

{{TableTitle
Damage Tiers at a Glance
}}

| Tier | Representative Weapons |
|:--|:--|
| Glancing | Dagger, club, sling, brass knuckles |
| Solid | Shortsword, mace, hand axe, light crossbow |
| Heavy | Longsword, warhammer, battleaxe, heavy bow |
| Crushing | Greatsword, halberd, maul, heavy crossbow |
| Devastating | Great-maul, braced pike vs. charge, bec-de-corbin on a downswing |

{{TableTitle
Protection Tiers at a Glance
}}

| Tier | Representative Armor |
|:--|:--|
| Minimal | Padded gambeson, thick wool, heavy cloth |
| Light | Leather, studded leather, hide |
| Medium | Chain shirt, scale, ring mail |
| Heavy | Plated mail, brigandine, partial plate |
| Extreme | Full plate, transitional harness, magical inlay |

{{CoreBody
Tiers are not rigid. A well-made longsword swung by an ogre is still a Heavy weapon in this book's vocabulary; the ogre's size bonuses are your game system's business, not ours.
}}

## Currency and Pricing

{{CoreBody
All prices are quoted in gold pieces (gp), with a default economy calibrated so that a longsword costs 90 gp, a plate cuirass costs roughly 800 gp, and a pint of ale costs 1 sp — the same ballpark as most old-school fantasy economies.
}}

{{CoreBody
Systems with very different pricing conventions are handled in two ways. Where the divergence is modest, the default price is simply a reasonable middle ground. Where a system's price is wildly different — more than three times higher or lower — a Scaling Note sidebar appears next to the affected item.
}}

{{note
##### Scaling Note — Example
Low-coin economies commonly price a longsword at 15 gp rather than 90 gp. If your table uses a low-coin baseline, divide all prices in this book by six.
}}

{{CoreBody
Appendix B includes a one-page rescaling formula for GMs running a custom economy. You will never need to apply it mid-session; the price on each entry is meant to be legal-at-the-table for the book's default setting.
}}

\column

## Weight and Bulk

{{CoreBody
Every item has a weight in pounds and a bulk rating from 0 (trivial) to 5 (unmanageable in close quarters). Weight governs how much a character can carry; bulk governs how much they can use effectively.
}}

{{CoreBody
A ten-foot pole weighs little but has a bulk of 3 — try swinging it in a five-foot-wide corridor and see. A heavy winter cloak weighs a great deal but has a bulk of 1, because it folds into a pack. Appendix D provides the full rules for converting bulk into movement, weapon, and maneuver penalties; most players will only need the bulk number to know whether a pike fits through a dungeon door.
}}

## Ability and Skill Names

{{CoreBody
The body text refers to abilities and skills by generic names. Translate them to your system's closest equivalent.
}}

{{TableTitle
Generic Ability Names
}}

| Name | Domain |
|:--|:--|
| Strength | Raw physical power, carrying, striking force |
| Agility | Dexterity, coordination, balance, reflex |
| Endurance | Toughness, stamina, resistance to poison and fatigue |
| Intelligence | Reasoning, memory, formal knowledge |
| Willpower | Mental resilience, faith, force of self |
| Presence | Charisma, bearing, leadership, social weight |

{{CoreBody
Where a check is called for, it is named by skill rather than by difficulty number. An entry might say "a successful Crafting check" or "a Perception check against a hidden compartment." Your game system supplies the target number. When the book needs to indicate difficulty, it does so in plain English — trivial, routine, challenging, demanding, or near-impossible — rather than in digits.
}}

\page

## Fantasy Alongside History

{{CoreBody
This book does not segregate fantasy gear into a separate chapter. Holy water sits next to lamp oil. A rune-bound longsword is described inside the longsword entry as a variant form. A dragon-bone cuirass is in the armor chapter, not exiled to an appendix.
}}

{{CoreBody
Where a piece of gear is purely fantastical — everburning torches, weightless plate, deep-salt blades — the entry carries a small fantasy marker in the margin. Where an item is grounded in history but has a common fantasy variant, the variant appears as a sidebar inside the main entry. The book assumes your setting has magic; the book also assumes not every sword is magical.
}}

## Sidebars, Curiosities, and Scaling Notes

{{CoreBody
Five kinds of boxed content appear throughout the book. Each signals something different at a glance.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Scaling Notes** flag items whose price or stats diverge significantly across rules families. Consult these when adapting the book to a low-coin or high-lethality system.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Curiosities** describe experimental, failed, or novelty items — spring-loaded gauntlets, multi-bolt organ crossbows, gun-shields, fire-pigs. These are rare in any realistic setting but make excellent plot hooks.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Fantasy Variants** present the magical or culture-specific version of a grounded item. A single longsword entry may include a dwarven-forged variant, an elven moon-silver variant, and a rune-bound variant, each with its own brief.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**GM Notes** offer optional rules, balance advice, or alternative interpretations. Ignore these at your pleasure.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Historical Asides** explain why an item existed, what problem it solved, and why it fell out of use. These never contain rules.
}}

\column

## Crafting, Repair, and the Economy

{{CoreBody
Chapter 3 is the longest single chapter in this book, and for good reason. Most gear books skip the economic layer entirely; this one treats it as the engine that makes equipment meaningful. A sword is not a thousand-gold object that appears when a character hands over a thousand gold. It is fifty pounds of iron, a week of a master's time, a guild's cut, a shop's rent, and a buyer who actually wants to buy a sword.
}}

{{CoreBody
Every crafted item in this book has enough information to be built from scratch: a materials cost, a labor-days estimate, and a saturation note on how many potential buyers a settlement of a given size can reasonably support. If the campaign involves player-character crafting and sales, read Chapter 3 first. If it does not, those fields can be ignored — the prose remains self-contained.
}}

## Using the Appendices

{{CoreBody
Six appendices sit at the back of the book.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Appendix A — System Conversion Guide.** The one-page cheat sheet that turns every tier in the book into real dice and armor numbers for your system. Read once; reference forever.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Appendix B — Master Price List.** Every item in the book, alphabetized, with cost and tier. Ideal for players outfitting a character and for GMs pricing a hoard.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Appendix C — Scavenging and Battlefield Loot.** Random tables by encounter type, so a dead skirmish on the road produces something more specific than "3d6 gold."
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Appendix D — Encumbrance and Bulk.** Full weight and bulk rules, including the tight-quarters modifier.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Appendix E — The Market.** Settlement-size tables showing what a thorpe, a village, and a capital city can reasonably be expected to stock, and how long it takes to find a buyer for high-value goods.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Appendix F — Curiosities Index.** A gathered index of every experimental and never-used item scattered through the book, sorted by chapter and item type for easy GM plundering.
}}

\page

## A Note on the Tone of the Tables

{{CoreBody
This book contains roughly eighty tables. Most exist to be consulted quickly during play. A few exist to be read like menus in an expensive restaurant. The book makes no attempt to hide which is which: any table that matters during combat is also repeated in Appendix A, B, or C in a more compact form, so the reader never has to flip back to Chapter 7 mid-arrow-volley.
}}

{{CoreBody
Where a table offers interpretive choices — which rune to bind to a blade, which breed of horse to buy, which regional cuisine to serve at a wedding — it is meant to be read aloud and argued over. Those are the tables the book wants you to linger on.
}}

## A Final Word Before the Forge

{{CoreBody
The chapters that follow are arranged in the order of use. Foundations first — materials, crafting, economy — then arms, then the logistics of moving arms and their bearers across a battlefield, then the provisions that keep them alive between battles, and finally the contests and enchantments that turn all of it into something worth telling stories about.
}}

{{CoreBody
A reader in a hurry can skip to the chapter that matches the question in hand. A reader with time to spare will find that every chapter refers back to Chapter 3, and that Chapter 3 refers back to every chapter, because in a functioning world the smith and the soldier are the same argument told from two ends.
}}

{{pageNumber,auto}}
{{footnote
The Ultimate Provisioner's Handbook — Chapter 1: Using This Book

v0.1 working draft
}}

\page

# Chapter 2: Materials & Construction

{{Epigraph
*"A good smith can ruin the finest ore. A great smith can coax a blade from scrap. But no smith, however cursed or blessed, can make bronze hold an edge against steel."*
}}
{{EpigraphAuthor
— attributed to the Second Guild of Ashenford
}}

## Why Material Matters

{{CoreBody
Every item in this book is described with a baseline set of statistics, quietly assuming a baseline set of materials. A longsword is steel. A bow is yew. A shield is oak faced in iron. A breastplate is forge-steel plate. The book names those defaults so the numbers in the entries mean something specific and comparable.
}}

{{CoreBody
When an item is made of something other than its default — a bronze sword in an iron age, a spear of dragon-bone, a hauberk faced with silver — the material's modifiers adjust the base stats. This chapter catalogues those materials, their practical properties, and their supernatural implications.
}}

{{CoreBody
Material is not the same as craftsmanship. A masterfully-made iron sword and a clumsily-made steel sword are different questions, answered by different chapters. Craftsmanship and quality are handled in Chapter 3. This chapter covers only what the thing is made of.
}}

## How Material Modifies an Item

{{CoreBody
Each non-default material in this chapter lists a compact row of modifiers, shown under the material's name. The columns are:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Damage.** Applies to weapons only. Uses the modifier tier language (Slight, Moderate, Major, Severe) from Appendix A. Affects damage rolls, not hit rolls.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Durability.** Applies to both weapons and armor. Affects how long the item survives under use. In systems that do not track item damage (Style B and default Style C), this modifier is cosmetic unless the table imports the optional durability rules from Chapter 4.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Weight.** Modifies the base item's weight using the Weight Multiplier table in Appendix A. A large enough change may shift the item's bulk rating as well; see Appendix D.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Cost.** A multiplier applied to the book's default price, using the Cost Multiplier table in Appendix A. Multiple cost modifiers — material, craftsmanship, region — stack multiplicatively.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Traits.** Unique properties that resist numeric description: silver bypasses supernatural resistances; cold iron disrupts fae glamors; sky-glass chips on first impact but pierces ordinary protection.
}}

{{CoreBody
Where a material's modifier row is silent in a column, treat that attribute as unchanged from the base item.
}}

<!-- ART -- material samples arranged on a workbench: bronze ingot, iron bar, polished steel, pattern-welded blade section, silver bangle, dragon-bone shard -->

\page

## Mundane Metals

{{CoreHanging
**Bronze.** The oldest war-metal. An alloy of copper and tin, bronze takes a sharp edge but holds it poorly; it dulls quickly and dents rather than breaks. In an iron-age setting, bronze is the weapon of the poor, the old, or the deliberately archaic. In a bronze-age setting, bronze is the weapon of kings.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Slight penalty · Durability: Moderate penalty · Weight: —
Cost: Slight reduction (iron age) / Major bonus (bronze age)
Traits: Resists some fae protections that exclude iron.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Iron.** Workable, common, structurally sound — but soft. The default metal of the rural smith who lacks the fuel or skill to produce true steel. Iron blades bend before they break, and a soldier on campaign is expected to straighten them against a tree each evening.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: — · Durability: Slight penalty · Weight: —
Cost: Slight reduction.
Traits: See Cold Iron, below.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Cold Iron.** Iron worked below a critical temperature and never re-fired afterward. The process yields a brittle, blackish-grey metal of limited practical value — save that fae creatures, certain demons, and several kinds of spirit react to it as steel reacts to salt water.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: — · Durability: Moderate penalty · Weight: —
Cost: Moderate bonus.
Traits: Bypasses fae glamors. Disrupts wards and compacts that name iron. Cannot be re-tempered once made.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Steel.** The baseline. Every weapon and metallic armor entry in this book is steel unless stated otherwise. The book assumes mid-grade forge steel — reliable, forgivable, and maintainable by any competent smith.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: — · Durability: — · Weight: — · Cost: —
Traits: None inherent.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Pattern-Welded Steel.** Also known as Damascus or watered steel. Produced by folding multiple steel billets together before forging, yielding the characteristic rippled patterns. Structurally superior to uniform steel, though the real-world edge is smaller than legend suggests.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Slight bonus · Durability: Moderate bonus · Weight: —
Cost: Major bonus.
Traits: Visually distinctive. Carries Presence weight when worn openly; a Slight bonus to negotiations in martial company.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Silver.** Soft, heavy, beautiful, and nearly useless as a primary weapon material. Most "silver" weapons are steel cores inlaid or plated with silver, giving the strike the metal's supernatural qualities without sacrificing structural soundness. True solid-silver blades exist only as ceremonial or specialist weapons for hunters of werewolves and certain undead.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Slight penalty (solid) / — (inlaid) · Durability: Major penalty (solid) / — (inlaid) · Weight: Slight bonus
Cost: Major bonus (inlaid) / Severe bonus (solid).
Traits: Bypasses the supernatural resistances of werewolves, certain fae, and specific breeds of undead. The lore varies by setting; the mechanical effect does not.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Lead.** Almost never a weapon material in pure form — it is too soft to take an edge. Its role is in sling bullets, arrow-weighting, blackjack filling, and the sewn pockets of rogues' gambesons.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: — (as bullet) · Durability: Moderate penalty · Weight: Major bonus
Cost: Moderate reduction.
Traits: Prolonged contact causes slow poisoning; alchemists handle it with care.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Gold.** A ceremonial metal only. Gold weapons are decorative, impractically heavy, and poor at holding shape. Gold-plated weapons exist as signs of office; the plating has no mechanical benefit.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Moderate penalty (solid) · Durability: Severe penalty (solid) · Weight: Major bonus
Cost: Severe bonus.
Traits: Significant Presence bonus when displayed; a target for theft in any non-formal setting.
}}

## Mundane Organics

{{CoreHanging
**Oak.** The default wood for shields, shafts, and tool handles. Dense, straight-grained, reliably strong. Most items in this book made of wood are oak unless stated otherwise.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage, Durability, Weight, Cost: Baseline for wooden items.
Traits: Seasons well. Tolerates wet conditions better than most.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Ash.** Flexible where oak is rigid, ash is the wood of spear shafts and bow staves. Less durable under direct impact but better at distributing force without shattering.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: — · Durability: Slight penalty · Weight: Slight reduction
Cost: —
Traits: Preferred for long shafts (spears, pikes, polearms); a Slight bonus against haft-breakage on a parry.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Yew.** The bow-wood. Yew's combination of flexible sapwood and rigid heartwood makes it the near-perfect material for self-bows and many composite bow cores.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Slight bonus (bows only) · Durability: — · Weight: —
Cost: Moderate bonus.
Traits: Sacred in some cultures. Mildly poisonous; green yew should not be handled near food.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Ironwood.** A general term for various tropical and magical hardwoods whose density approaches that of metal. Ironwood is heavy, dense, and difficult to work — smiths avoid it; carpenters curse it; weapon-makers prize it for clubs, staves, and shield cores meant to survive a very bad day.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Slight bonus (blunt only) · Durability: Moderate bonus · Weight: Moderate bonus
Cost: Major bonus.
Traits: Resists fire (Moderate); resists normal edged weapons (Slight).
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Tanned Leather.** The baseline for leather armor, belts, scabbards, and most bindings. "Tanned" here means vegetable-tanned oak-bark leather, a months-long process that yields durable, water-resistant hide.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage, Durability, Weight, Cost: Baseline for leather items.
Traits: Softens and fails if left wet; reclaims most of its strength when dried carefully.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Boiled Leather (Cuir Bouilli).** Hardened leather produced by soaking it in hot wax or water. Significantly more rigid than normal leather; used for reinforcing caps, vambraces, and cheap plate substitutes.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: — · Durability: Moderate bonus · Weight: Slight bonus
Cost: Moderate bonus.
Traits: Brittle in deep cold; melts near open flame.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Rawhide.** Untanned, sun-dried hide. Cheaper than tanned leather and nearly as tough when dry, but it softens completely in water. Used for temporary armor, wagon coverings, and the cores of composite bows.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: — · Durability: Slight penalty · Weight: —
Cost: Moderate reduction.
Traits: Becomes useless when wet; shrinks powerfully as it dries, a property exploited by torturers.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Hide.** Fur-on, minimally processed pelts. Offers protection through sheer thickness rather than cured strength. Common among cultures without industrial tanneries.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: — · Durability: Moderate penalty · Weight: Moderate bonus
Cost: Slight reduction.
Traits: Slight bonus to cold resistance. Attracts vermin if uncared for.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Bone.** Worked from large animal skeletons. Takes an edge poorly but holds a point well; common in cultures lacking metal. Bone knives, spear tips, and arrow heads are all credible weapons in the right hands.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Slight penalty · Durability: Moderate penalty · Weight: Slight reduction
Cost: Moderate reduction.
Traits: Splinters on impact against hard protection; some settings consider bone-weapons unholy.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Horn and Sinew.** Not structural materials in themselves, but critical components of composite bows, lamellar armor bindings, and traditional armor construction. Horn for compression resistance; sinew for tension. Entries that specify "composite" construction assume both.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Used in composition with other materials; no independent stat line.
Traits: Composite bows using horn and sinew backing gain a Slight damage bonus over single-material self-bows.
}}

## Mundane Minerals

{{CoreHanging
**Flint and Obsidian.** Chipped stone takes a razor edge that no metal can quite equal — for a single strike. Flint and obsidian blades are terrifyingly sharp when new and nearly useless after three serious blows.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Slight bonus (on first strike of a combat) · Durability: Severe penalty · Weight: Slight reduction
Cost: Major reduction (if locally quarried).
Traits: First strike of each combat ignores one tier of Protection. Any critical failure or after three solid hits, the blade shatters.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Stone.** Chisel-worked, ground, or polished — useful for maces, clubs, sling bullets, and certain axe heads. Heavy, blunt, unfashionable.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: — (blunt) · Durability: Slight penalty · Weight: Moderate bonus
Cost: Major reduction.
Traits: Near-immunity to corrosion; sinks instantly in water.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Ceramic (Fired Clay).** Occasionally used for body armor in lands lacking metal. Light, cheap, and surprisingly effective against a first hit; useless afterward.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: n/a · Durability: Severe penalty · Weight: Slight reduction
Cost: Major reduction.
Traits: Designed to break. First significant hit absorbs damage as the tier above baseline; any second hit reduces the armor to fragments.
}}

## Fantasy Materials

{{CoreBody
The following materials do not exist in the natural world — or exist only as rumored, sacred, or stolen things. Where the mundane materials above describe the working metal of a civilization, these describe the stuff of heirlooms and legends. A single dragon-bone axe will be more valuable than the village that smelts it.
}}

{{CoreBody
Setting-makers may rename any material here to fit their own mythology; the mechanical profile travels regardless of the name chosen. Where the book refers to these materials by name in later chapters, the reference is always to the profile, not to any particular cosmology.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Dark-Iron.** A black, matte, slightly warm metal extracted from meteorite cores and, in some settings, from the bones of slain stone-titans. Dark-iron is heavier than steel but substantially tougher — a dark-iron blade will out-survive its wielder by several generations.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Slight bonus · Durability: Major bonus · Weight: Moderate bonus
Cost: Severe bonus.
Traits: Absorbs heat rather than conducts it — wielders gain a Moderate bonus against fire attacks. Cannot be re-forged once shaped; all shaping must be done during initial smithing.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Star-Silver.** A cold-gleaming, whitish-blue metal said to fall from the night sky on the rarest of meteor showers. Star-silver is dramatically lighter than steel while retaining comparable edge-holding properties. Swords of star-silver feel weightless to a trained hand.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: — · Durability: Moderate bonus · Weight: Major reduction
Cost: Severe bonus.
Traits: Carries the supernatural properties of silver (bypasses lycanthropic and certain fae resistances). Wielder gains a Slight bonus to initiative due to reduced weapon weight. Will not take a permanent black polish.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Sky-Glass.** A volcanic material in origin — though some settings attribute it to fallen sky-shards or the remains of extinct dragons. Sky-glass is black, glossy, and terrifyingly sharp. A blade of sky-glass can shear through cheap metal armor on a clean strike, but the blade itself is nearly as fragile as true glass.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Moderate bonus · Durability: Major penalty · Weight: Slight reduction
Cost: Major bonus.
Traits: First strike of each combat ignores one tier of Protection; if the strike is parried or deflected by a hard surface, roll Durability against breakage. Sky-glass cannot be resharpened — only re-knapped, and only by specialists.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Dragon-Bone.** The calcified remains of great draconic creatures. Dragon-bone is nearly as hard as steel, far lighter, and retains a thread of its source's elemental nature. Bone taken from a fire-drake weeps heat; bone from a frost-drake bites like winter to the touch.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Slight bonus · Durability: Moderate bonus · Weight: Moderate reduction
Cost: Severe bonus.
Traits: Retains elemental affinity of its source (a weapon made of fire-drake bone deals an additional Slight amount of fire damage; frost-drake bone, cold; etc.). Some settings consider dragon-bone weapons cursed, attracting the enmity of living dragons of the same lineage.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Deep-Salt Crystal.** Great translucent rose-white crystals mined from the drowned salt-seams beneath certain desert cities. Deep-salt crystal is structurally sound enough to take an edge, and its surface slowly draws moisture from nearby flesh — a slow, persistent drain that most opponents do not notice until minutes into a fight.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: — · Durability: Slight penalty · Weight: Moderate reduction
Cost: Major bonus.
Traits: A Deep-salt weapon imposes a Slight Endurance penalty on any wound it inflicts for one scene after contact (dehydration and tissue stiffening). Dissolves in prolonged water contact; cannot be carried by sea for any length of time.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Moon-Silver.** An elven reforging of natural silver using techniques unknown to human smiths. Moon-silver retains silver's supernatural properties while gaining hardness equal to steel. Moon-silver weapons glow faintly in moonlight and are rumored to whisper to their wielders.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: — · Durability: Slight bonus · Weight: Slight reduction
Cost: Severe bonus.
Traits: Carries all supernatural properties of silver. Sheds dim light in moonlight (ten-foot radius). Will not hold a non-moon-silver edge; repairs require an elven smith.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Living Wood.** Branches of certain sacred trees harvested without killing the parent tree, then shaped by druidic craft. Living wood remains faintly alive; it heals small nicks and cuts overnight when placed in contact with fresh earth and living water.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: — · Durability: Slight bonus · Weight: —
Cost: Major bonus.
Traits: Self-repairs minor damage over one night of proper care. Cannot be lit on fire by mundane flame. Will not strike a druid of the source order, pulling itself from the wielder's grip on the attempt.
}}

## The Materials Cheat-Sheet

{{CoreBody
The following table gathers every material in this chapter in one view for quick reference. Use it to adjust a base item's stats at the forge or at the scavenging field.
}}

\page

{{TableTitle
Materials Cheat-Sheet (Weapons)
}}

| Material | Damage | Durability | Weight | Cost | Notable Trait |
|:--|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:--|
| Bronze | −Slight | −Moderate | — | −Slight / +Major | Bypasses iron-ward |
| Iron | — | −Slight | — | −Slight | — |
| Cold Iron | — | −Moderate | — | +Moderate | Disrupts fae |
| Steel | — | — | — | — | Baseline |
| Pattern-Welded | +Slight | +Moderate | — | +Major | Presence bonus |
| Silver (inlaid) | — | — | — | +Major | Bypasses lycanthrope |
| Silver (solid) | −Slight | −Major | +Slight | +Severe | Bypasses lycanthrope |
| Lead (bullet) | — | −Moderate | +Major | −Moderate | — |
| Flint / Obsidian | +Slight (1st) | −Severe | −Slight | −Major | Pierces 1 Protection tier, 1st hit |
| Bone | −Slight | −Moderate | −Slight | −Moderate | May splinter |
| Stone | — | −Slight | +Moderate | −Major | Corrosion-proof |
| Ironwood (blunt) | +Slight | +Moderate | +Moderate | +Major | Fire-resistant |
| Dark-Iron | +Slight | +Major | +Moderate | +Severe | Fire-absorbing |
| Star-Silver | — | +Moderate | −Major | +Severe | Silver + initiative |
| Sky-Glass | +Moderate | −Major | −Slight | +Major | Pierces 1 Protection tier, 1st hit |
| Dragon-Bone | +Slight | +Moderate | −Moderate | +Severe | Elemental affinity |
| Deep-Salt Crystal | — | −Slight | −Moderate | +Major | Dehydration effect |
| Moon-Silver | — | +Slight | −Slight | +Severe | Silver + glows in moonlight |
| Living Wood | — | +Slight | — | +Major | Self-repairs, fireproof |

{{TableTitle
Materials Cheat-Sheet (Armor)
}}

| Material | Durability | Weight | Cost | Notable Trait |
|:--|:-:|:-:|:-:|:--|
| Bronze plate | −Moderate | — | −Slight / +Major | Bypasses iron-ward |
| Iron mail | −Slight | — | −Slight | — |
| Steel (any) | — | — | — | Baseline |
| Tanned Leather | — | — | — | Baseline (leather) |
| Boiled Leather | +Moderate | +Slight | +Moderate | Brittle in cold |
| Rawhide | −Slight | — | −Moderate | Fails when wet |
| Hide (fur-on) | −Moderate | +Moderate | −Slight | Cold resistance |
| Ceramic | −Severe | −Slight | −Major | Absorbs one hit, then shatters |
| Ironwood | +Moderate | +Moderate | +Major | Fire-resistant |
| Dark-Iron plate | +Major | +Moderate | +Severe | Fire-absorbing |
| Dragon-Bone scale | +Moderate | −Moderate | +Severe | Elemental resistance matches source |
| Living Wood | +Slight | — | +Major | Self-repairing, fireproof |

\page

## Material Availability

{{CoreBody
Not every material in this chapter is available in every settlement. Chapter 3 covers the economics of sourcing raw materials for crafting; Appendix E covers the market availability of finished goods in those materials. A few quick notes apply at purchase time:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Mundane materials** are widely available in any iron-age setting of market-town size or larger. A rural smith may stock only iron, not steel; a coastal village may have no bronze worth the name.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Pattern-welded steel** is a reasonable stretch goal in any capital city, but every master smith who can produce it has a three-month waiting list.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Fantasy materials** are almost never stocked. A capital city's finest shop may have one or two pieces of star-silver or dark-iron jewelry on display; weapons and armor in such materials are commissioned, inherited, or looted, not purchased. Assume any attempt to buy a finished fantasy-material item takes months of searching and costs significantly more than the book's listed multiplier.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Raw fantasy materials** — an uncut chunk of dragon-bone, a handful of sky-glass shards — are more readily available than finished goods, but still extraordinary. Treat them as adventure rewards first and commodities second.
}}

## A Note on Imports and Reskinning

{{CoreBody
The seven fantasy materials in this chapter are not meant to be exhaustive. A given setting may use three of them, rename two, invent four more, and strip the rest. The book's later chapters refer to the profiles here by name only when a specific mechanical effect needs to be specified; a GM running a setting where "Star-Silver" is called "moonstone" or "quicksilver" can substitute freely without reworking any numbers.
}}

{{CoreBody
Where an enchantment or legendary item in Chapter 19 requires a fantasy material, the reference is always to the profile — "any material with the silver trait," "any material with self-repair" — rather than the specific name. Substitute to taste.
}}

{{pageNumber,auto}}
{{footnote
The Ultimate Provisioner's Handbook — Chapter 2: Materials & Construction

v0.1 working draft
}}

\page

# Chapter 3: Crafting, Repair & the Working Economy

{{Epigraph
*"Everyone wants to be the smith. Nobody wants to haul the charcoal."*
}}
{{EpigraphAuthor
— Halver the Shorter, apprentice-master, Ashenford Guild
}}

## What This Chapter Covers

{{CoreBody
This is the longest chapter in the book, and it earns its length. Crafting is the engine that makes the rest of the compendium meaningful — a longsword is not a 90 gp object that appears when the player hands over 90 gp. It is a bar of steel, a week of work, a guild's cut, a shop's rent, and a buyer who wants that particular sword in that particular week.
}}

{{CoreBody
The chapter covers, in order: the crafting loop, the skills involved, the economics that keep players from printing money, quality and masterwork rules, repair and maintenance, self-harvesting raw materials from adventure-rewards, and the special cases (brewing, cooking, alchemy) that are handled more fully in their own later chapters.
}}

{{CoreBody
A GM who does not plan to use crafting mechanics in their campaign can skip this chapter without losing anything. The prose descriptions of items elsewhere in the book are self-contained. Where a later chapter references "material cost" or "labor days," those references are to this chapter and can be ignored safely.
}}

## The Crafting Loop

{{CoreBody
A completed crafted item is the product of four ingredients in sequence: sourcing, facility, labor, and finishing. Each step has its own cost and its own failure modes.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Sourcing.** The raw materials. Iron ingots, oak timber, tanned hides, bronze stock. Material cost typically runs 25% to 35% of the item's final retail price, with 25% as the book's default. This is where player-harvested materials (see later in this chapter) can save significant coin.
}}

{{note
##### GM Tuning: The Grit Knob

The material fraction is the single most powerful lever a GM has for adjusting the feel of the economy. The book defaults to 25% — crafting is hard but workable. Raise it to 30% for a grittier setting where margins are tight and one bad month can ruin a shop. Raise it to 35% for a genuinely punishing economy where only masters in large cities can make it work. Lower it to 20% for a forgiving, high-fantasy economy where crafting is a reliable livelihood.

No other parameter shifts the math as cleanly. Adjust this one first, and leave the rest at book defaults unless you have a specific reason to tune them.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Facility.** The forge, the tannery, the shipyard, the cooper's shop. Crafting requires tools, space, and heat. A character working without a proper facility suffers a Major skill penalty and may be unable to produce high-tier items at all. Facility rental or ownership is a monthly cost that runs roughly 5% of a working crafter's monthly output.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Labor.** The days of work required to complete the item. Labor time scales with the item's tier and the crafter's skill. A master crafter finishes in half the time a journeyman requires.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Finishing.** Sharpening, polishing, fitting, adjusting, binding, and all the small touches that distinguish a working tool from a finished good. Finishing is often done by a specialist rather than the primary crafter.
}}

\column

## Skills Used in Crafting

{{CoreBody
The book names crafting skills generically. Translate each to your system's closest equivalent using Appendix A.
}}

{{TableTitle
Primary Crafting Skills
}}

| Skill | Covers |
|:--|:--|
| Smithing | Weapons, metal armor, tools, nails, horseshoes, hinges |
| Carpentry | Shields, shafts, bows, furniture, wagons, ship-work |
| Tanning | Raw hide → leather; leather goods generally |
| Stonework | Masonry, millstones, siege-engine stone balls |
| Bowyer | Bows and crossbow-prods specifically |
| Fletcher | Arrows and crossbow bolts |
| Tailor | Cloth goods, padded armor, clothing, pouches, banners |
| Jeweler | Rings, clasps, fine inlay, enchantment anchors |
| Brewing | Beer, wine, mead, spirits (Chapter 15) |
| Cooking | Rations, preserved foods, specialty meals (Chapter 15) |
| Alchemy | Consumables, poisons, infusions (Chapter 16) |

{{CoreBody
Many items require more than one skill. A functional bow requires Bowyer for the stave and Fletcher for the string and nocks; a suit of plate requires Smithing for the metal and Tanning for the straps and padding. When a crafter lacks a required secondary skill, the book's default is to hire the work out at a Slight cost bonus — or accept a Moderate penalty to the finished item's quality.
}}

\page

## Labor Time by Item Tier

{{CoreBody
The book sorts crafted items into five tiers by complexity and labor intensity. Each tier has a baseline number of labor days at journeyman skill; master crafters complete in half the listed time, apprentices take twice as long.
}}

{{TableTitle
Labor Days by Tier
}}

| Tier | Typical Items | Journeyman Days | Master Days | Apprentice Days |
|:--|:--|:-:|:-:|:-:|
| Trivial | Nail, horseshoe, simple belt | Hours | <1 | 1 |
| Simple | Dagger, club, padded jerkin, sling | 1–2 | 1 | 3 |
| Standard | Longsword, leather armor, shield, bow | 5–10 | 3–5 | 15 |
| Complex | Plate cuirass, heavy crossbow, fine blade | 15–30 | 8–15 | 45+ |
| Masterwork | Full plate harness, siege weapon, legendary piece | 60–120 | 30–60 | Not feasible |

{{CoreBody
A crafter working a standard day produces roughly one day's labor output. Long days, assistant labor, or rush jobs can compress schedules but generally at the cost of quality. A crafter who halves their labor time through rushing imposes a Moderate penalty on the quality roll at the end.
}}

## Quality and the Finishing Roll

{{CoreBody
At the end of the labor period, the crafter makes a single Crafting check at the difficulty appropriate to the item's tier. The result determines the finished item's quality.
}}

{{TableTitle
Quality Outcomes
}}

| Result | Quality | Effect |
|:--|:--|:--|
| Critical Success | Masterwork | Full stat bonus tier (see below). Triples the listed sale price. |
| Success | Standard | Book-listed stats. Sells at book price. |
| Partial (within 5% or 1 of target) | Serviceable | One step below baseline in Durability. Sells at 75% price. |
| Failure | Flawed | Moderate Durability penalty; Slight penalty to one combat stat. Sells at 50% price. |
| Critical Failure | Scrap | Unusable; half materials recoverable if examined by another crafter. |

{{CoreBody
A crafter who fails by a significant margin may be able to salvage the attempt by starting over with reclaimed materials and fresh labor. A crafter who critically fails has wasted the materials and the time both.
}}

\column

## Masterwork Items

{{CoreBody
A masterwork item is distinguished by superior fit, balance, and finish. It is not magical. It is simply very, very well made.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Masterwork Weapon.** Grants a Slight bonus to damage and a Slight bonus to all attack rolls. Durability is shifted one tier higher than the base item.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Masterwork Armor.** Grants a Slight bonus to Protection tier effectiveness (interpretation varies by style; see Appendix A). The armor's bulk and weight are reduced by one tier step. Durability is shifted one tier higher.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Masterwork Tool.** Grants a Slight bonus to any skill check made with the tool. A masterwork set of thieves' tools, a masterwork forge, or a masterwork instrument improves the wielder's work across the board.
}}

{{CoreBody
Masterwork status is not the same as enchantment. An item may be masterwork, enchanted, both, or neither. A masterwork longsword with no enchantment is still meaningfully better than a standard longsword; a merely standard longsword with a single rune-binding is still meaningfully better than a masterwork one with none. Stacking both — a masterwork rune-bound longsword — is the work of dynasties.
}}

{{CoreBody
A crafter can attempt to deliberately produce a masterwork item, but the attempt costs a Major labor-time bonus (three times baseline) and the final Crafting check suffers a Moderate penalty. Most masterwork items are the happy accidents of ordinary commissions, not deliberate productions.
}}

\page

## Why Players Don't Get Rich

{{CoreBody
Every GM has met the player who announces: "I have Smithing. I will craft swords and sell them at the listed price. I am now rich."
}}

{{CoreBody
The correct answer is not to deny the attempt but to model what actually happens. The economics of a working crafter are hostile to sudden wealth for reasons that have nothing to do with the GM's preferences. They have to do with facility overhead, guild dues, market saturation, and the simple fact that not everyone in a village needs a new sword this month.
}}

## Where the Money Goes

{{CoreBody
A crafter who sells an item at its full book-listed price is not keeping the full book-listed price. The following costs apply to every sale, and most apply to every month of operation regardless of sales.
}}

{{TableTitle
Cost Deductions from Retail Price
}}

| Cost | Typical % of Sale |
|:--|:-:|
| Raw materials | 25–35% |
| Facility rent / maintenance | 5% |
| Guild dues | 10% |
| Local sales tax | 5% |
| Fuel and consumables (coal, oil) | 5% |
| Apprentice/assistant wages (if applicable) | 5–10% |

{{CoreBody
A crafter who has sold a 90 gp longsword has gross-received 90 gp and net-earned somewhere between 40 gp and 55 gp after materials, guild dues, tax, and fuel. That sounds workable until you subtract the crafter's own lifestyle expenses for the eight-to-ten days it took to make the sword — about 1 gp per day for a modest working lifestyle, or roughly 8–10 gp of the margin — and the share of the monthly workshop rent that ate into the period as well.
}}

{{CoreBody
A licensed journeyman crafter in a town, selling three or four Standard-tier items in a typical month, nets somewhere between 20 and 50 gp after all expenses. That is respectable artisan income, not an infinite money pump. Good months clear 80 to 100; bad months — quality failures, buyers who didn't materialize, the unexpected plague of rats in the workshop — can run into the red.
}}

\column

## Guild Dues and the Unlicensed Crafter

{{CoreBody
Most settlements above village size have working guilds — Blacksmiths' Guild, Carpenters' Guild, Brewers' Guild, and so on. A guild license is the price of doing business legally and openly.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Licensed Crafters.** Pay 10% of their gross monthly output to the guild. In exchange, they gain access to the guild's facility (often shared), bulk material discounts, legal protection, and the right to hang a sign.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Unlicensed Crafters.** Keep the 10%, but assume all risk. Unlicensed goods sold in a guild town are subject to seizure, fine, or confiscation. A first offense typically costs 50% of the goods' value; a second offense costs all goods plus a steep fine; a third offense costs all goods, the fine, and an unfriendly visit from licensed members.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Rural and Frontier Crafters.** No guild — and no guild protection. The crafter can sell freely, but also without guild backing when disputes arise. Most frontier smiths work at lower prices (Moderate reduction) because their market is small and poor.
}}

{{note
##### The Village Smith's Real Economy

The monthly-demand tables later in this chapter are blunt about villages: there simply is not enough buying power in a community of five hundred people to sustain a full-time weapon-and-armor crafter. A village smith who tried to live on new longsword sales would starve.

Real village smiths did not. They survived because they were not, functionally, weapon-and-armor crafters. They were the village's blacksmith, which meant: they shod horses, repaired plow-blades, forged nails by the barrel, fixed hinges, fixed kettles, and sometimes fixed swords. They held a small plot of land worked by their family. They accepted payment in eggs, cheese, and labor as often as in coin.

A player character who wants to run a village smithing operation should assume this model: roughly half the income comes from repair and horseshoeing (not covered in detail by this book, but a reasonable GM estimate is 15–20 gp per month of steady work), a quarter from farming or foraging (effectively halving the lifestyle cost), and a quarter from occasional new-production sales. The crafting rules above still apply to the new-production portion; the rest is color. Set repair income much higher than 20 gp/month and the village smith starts outearning town artisans — which undermines the book's implicit premise that towns are the prestige path for crafting as a career.

A pure new-production smith belongs in a town or larger. The rural smith belongs in a story.
}}

{{note
##### Foreign Crafters in Guild Towns

A crafter passing through a guild town with goods made elsewhere is generally permitted to sell at a market stall for a fee — typically one day's sale at 15% of gross. Goods demonstrably made in the host town by an outsider are another matter entirely and typically require either guild membership or a very convincing story.
}}

\page

## Market Saturation by Settlement Size

{{CoreBody
The other answer to "why am I not rich" is "who's buying?" A village of two hundred souls has exactly zero inhabitants looking to drop 90 gp on a longsword this Tuesday. A crafter seeking a buyer must either wait, travel, or lower the price.
}}

{{TableTitle
Monthly Demand by Settlement Size
}}

| Settlement | Pop. | Items < 10 gp | Items < 100 gp | Items < 500 gp | Items > 500 gp |
|:--|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|
| Thorpe | <100 | 2–4 | 0–1 | 0 | 0 |
| Hamlet | 100–500 | 4–8 | 1–2 | 0 | 0 |
| Village | 500–1,500 | 10–20 | 2–5 | 0–1 | 0 |
| Town | 1,500–6,000 | Unlimited | 10–20 | 2–4 | 0–1 |
| City | 6,000–25,000 | Unlimited | Unlimited | 8–15 | 1–3 |
| Capital | 25,000+ | Unlimited | Unlimited | Unlimited | 5–10 |

{{CoreBody
The numbers are monthly demand, not total. A player character crafting in a town might sell four high-end items per month without difficulty; attempting to sell a fifth requires waiting, traveling, or discounting.
}}

{{CoreBody
Appendix E provides the fuller version of this table with per-item-category breakdowns and region-specific variance. For normal play, the summary above is enough.
}}

## Finding a Buyer for a Specific Item

{{CoreBody
A crafter with a finished high-value item in hand must actually find the right buyer. This is not free. It costs time, shoe-leather, and sometimes social standing. The Find-a-Buyer check uses the crafter's Persuasion skill at a difficulty based on settlement size and item value.
}}

{{TableTitle
Find-a-Buyer Difficulty
}}

| Settlement | Item < 100 gp | Item < 500 gp | Item < 2,000 gp | Item > 2,000 gp |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| Thorpe | Challenging | Near-impossible | Impossible | Impossible |
| Village | Routine | Demanding | Near-impossible | Impossible |
| Town | Trivial | Challenging | Demanding | Near-impossible |
| City | Trivial | Routine | Challenging | Demanding |
| Capital | Trivial | Trivial | Routine | Challenging |

\column

{{CoreBody
A successful Find-a-Buyer check takes one to three days of legwork and yields a buyer at full book price. A failed check produces no buyer this week; the crafter may retry with a Moderate difficulty increase after seven days. A successful check with a partial margin yields a buyer at a Slight-to-Moderate price discount.
}}

{{CoreBody
Crafters with an established shop, a guild membership, or a reputation may skip the Find-a-Buyer roll for items below the top tier of the town's market, selling directly at standard prices. Building that reputation is its own long project, typically six months to a year of reliable work in the same settlement.
}}

## Repair and Maintenance

{{CoreBody
Repair is the other side of the crafting coin. Most items in this book can be repaired by anyone with the relevant crafting skill, facility, and materials. The rules are the same as for crafting, with reduced cost and time.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Minor Repair.** Restoring Durability lost to ordinary use. Takes a Crafting check at Routine difficulty and materials equal to 5% of the item's value. One day of labor restores up to half the item's maximum Durability.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Major Repair.** Restoring a badly damaged item — a broken blade, a crushed helm, a snapped bow. Takes a Crafting check at Challenging difficulty, materials equal to 25% of the item's value, and three to five days of labor. Not all items can be repaired this way; some must be scrapped and reforged.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Field Repair.** Quick work with a portable kit. Restores a Slight amount of Durability per attempt, requires a Crafting check at Challenging difficulty, and consumes a single use of a repair kit. A portable smith's kit holds ten such uses and weighs 12 lbs.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Reforging.** Destroying a damaged item to recover its materials, then building a new one. Recovers 50% of the original material cost; full labor time applies.
}}

\page

## Maintenance Between Sessions

{{CoreBody
A character who does not maintain their gear will, over time, see its quality degrade. The book assumes ordinary maintenance is part of ordinary downtime — a character with spare hours each evening is cleaning, oiling, and sharpening as a matter of course.
}}

{{CoreBody
When a character is on a long push without downtime — a forced march, a prolonged dungeon crawl, an extended naval voyage — the GM may call for maintenance checks. The defaults are:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Weapons.** Seven days of hard use without attention shifts a weapon's Durability down one step and imposes a Slight penalty to damage rolls until maintained. Edged weapons degrade twice as fast as blunt.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Metal Armor.** Fourteen days of hard use without maintenance produces a Slight Protection penalty from rust, dents, and loose straps. Extended exposure to salt water or volcanic conditions cuts that timeline in half.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Leather and Cloth.** Seven days of soaking wet use without drying and tending produces cracking, mildew, or rot. Imposes a Slight Durability penalty until properly dried and treated.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Bows and Bowstrings.** Extreme humidity, cold, or heat can ruin a bow in days. A well-kept bow lasts decades; a neglected one, a single wet autumn. Most archers carry at least two spare strings.
}}

\column

## Self-Harvesting Raw Materials

{{CoreBody
Player characters can recover crafting materials from adventure rewards — the bear they killed, the wyvern whose corpse they dragged home, the bandit camp's leftover iron. Properly harvested and prepared, these materials can substantially reduce the material cost of subsequent crafting.
}}

{{CoreBody
The book does not attempt to model tanning, butchery, smelting, or quarrying as full subsystems. Those are trades in themselves, usually requiring a facility the party does not have. Instead, the rules below abstract harvesting into a single Skill check and a defined material yield.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Harvest Check.** Survival for animals; Smithing for metal from scrap or ore; Stonework for stone; the appropriate skill for the target. Difficulty is Routine for common prey, Challenging for exotic creatures or difficult extraction, Demanding for very large or unusual specimens.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Time Required.** One to four hours per successful harvest. A dragon may take days, and requires proper butchery tools.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Material Value.** A successful harvest yields raw materials worth a Moderate cost reduction compared to buying the equivalent materials at a guild shop. A critical success yields a Major reduction. A partial success yields only salvage-grade materials, usable only for Simple-tier crafting.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Processing Time.** Raw harvested materials usually require further preparation before they can be used in crafting — tanning takes weeks, smelting takes days, curing meat takes months. The book abstracts this as a flat "processing delay" before materials are usable, with the specific delay left to GM discretion based on the material and the party's circumstances.
}}

\page

{{TableTitle
Typical Harvest Yields
}}

| Source | Primary Yield | Secondary Yield | Processing |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| Deer, boar, goat | Hide (1 leather item) | Meat (3–7 days of rations), sinew, horn | Weeks (hide) / days (meat) |
| Wolf, large dog | Pelt (1 hide item) | Teeth, sinew | Weeks |
| Bear | Heavy pelt (2 hide items) | Meat, claws, fat | Weeks |
| Big cat | Fine pelt (1 luxury-grade item) | Teeth, claws | Weeks |
| Bandits / soldiers | Mundane iron/steel scrap, cloth, some coin | Boots, belts, small tools | Days |
| Giant snake, lizard | Scales (1 scale-armor piece) | Venom (if present), meat | Weeks |
| Giant insect | Chitin (1 light armor piece) | Acidic fluids (Alchemy interest) | Days |
| Wyvern, drake | Scales (1 partial armor), claws, teeth | Trace dragon-bone (uncertain yield) | Weeks |
| True dragon | Dragon-bone (several items), scales (full armor), teeth, claws, heart | Organs (Alchemy, preservation required) | Months, specialist required |
| Rockslide, deposit | Stone, occasional ore | — | Immediate (stone) / weeks (smelting) |
| Wreckage, ruined keep | Scrap metal, timber, cloth | Occasional finished goods | Days |

{{CoreBody
A party that plans ahead — bringing salt, oil, coopering tools, and clean cloth — preserves more of a kill than a party that does not. A party that is fleeing pursuers after a fight loses most of the above by default.
}}

## Selling Self-Harvested Raw Materials

{{CoreBody
Raw materials can be sold as well as used. The market for them is smaller than for finished goods, and the prices reflect that.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Standard Raw Materials.** A hide, a bundle of iron scrap, a sack of wool. Sells for approximately 20–30% of the cost of equivalent shop-bought materials. Expect to find buyers in any town or larger.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Exotic Raw Materials.** Dragon scales, wyvern claws, moonsilver shavings. Sells for wildly variable prices depending on who wants what this season. A city is required to find serious buyers; a capital is often required for the very exotic. Use the Find-a-Buyer table with the item categorized as Complex or Masterwork by value.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Contaminated or Dubious Materials.** Fell beast hide, necrotic bone, tomb-iron. Many buyers refuse such goods on principle; others pay a Moderate bonus specifically because of the provenance. Plot-relevant in most cases.
}}

\column

## Worked Example: Erla the Smith

{{CoreBody
Erla is a journeyman smith in the town of Hollowford (population 3,500). She has decided to set up an unlicensed shop in her rented workspace and see how the math actually shakes out. Her monthly base costs are: workshop rent, 15 gp. Lifestyle (small room above the shop, meals, occasional ale), about 30 gp. Tool maintenance, 2 gp. Total before production: 47 gp.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Output.** Erla focuses on longswords (90 gp list, 8 days each) and mail shirts (250 gp list, 20 days each). In a typical month she completes three longswords and begins — but does not finish — a mail shirt.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Month One, Unlicensed.** Three longswords produced. Quality rolls come out mixed: two are standard, one is a partial success that sells at 75% price. Finding buyers is normally easy in a town, but one sword goes two weeks without a taker. At the end of the month a guild inspector visits; one finished sword is confiscated and Erla is fined 90 gp for selling unlicensed. She sold one standard longsword (90 gp) and one partial (67 gp) for a gross of 157 gp. Materials on three swords: 81 gp. Guild fine: 90 gp. Tax and fuel: 16 gp. Base costs: 47 gp. Net: −77 gp. Erla has lost money this month.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Month Two, Licensed.** Erla pays 3 gp for a guild permit and resumes work. Three longswords: two standard sells, one unsold this month. Gross: 180 gp. Materials: 81 gp. Guild dues (10%): 18 gp. Tax (5%): 9 gp. Fuel (5%): 9 gp. Base costs: 47 gp. Net: +16 gp. The unsold sword carries into next month as inventory.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Month Three, a Good Month.** The unsold sword from last month finds a buyer early. Erla completes three more swords and finishes the mail shirt she started weeks ago. All four longswords sell at full price; the mail shirt sells for 250 gp. Gross: 610 gp. Materials (three swords + the finished mail shirt's final portion): 115 gp. Guild: 61. Tax: 30. Fuel: 30. Base costs: 47. Net: +327 gp. A very good month.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Month Four, a Bad Month.** A supply chain issue triples the price of iron stock for two weeks; Erla completes only two longswords. One fails the quality roll and sells for 45 gp. Gross: 135 gp. Materials at the inflated price: 85 gp. Guild: 13.5. Tax: 6.75. Fuel: 6.75. Base costs: 47. Net: −24 gp.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Averaged Truth.** Across a twelve-month year of ordinary production, Erla nets around 30 to 40 gp per month. After six months she has saved somewhere between 150 and 250 gp. This is respectable artisan income. It is not adventure income. A typical thieves' haul from a single dungeon crawl will exceed a year of her honest labor.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Ten-Year Path.** A crafter who sticks with the work — apprentice through journeyman through master, in a town that graduates to a city when their skill demands the larger market — can expect to accumulate roughly 8,000 gp in savings across a decade of practice. That is real wealth. It is also ten years of daily labor, not a single quest's reward. Players considering the crafter's life should measure their ambitions against that timeline and decide whether the slow path suits them.
}}

\page

## Specialty Crafting Pointers

{{CoreBody
Certain crafting disciplines are complex enough to warrant their own dedicated chapters. The rules here apply to all of them, but the chapter-specific content adds flavor, tables, and special cases.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Brewing (Chapter 15).** Beer, wine, mead, and distilled spirits. Long timescales measured in months rather than days; the "labor days" here represent active work, not elapsed calendar time. Flavor variation is a significant economic factor.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Cooking (Chapter 15).** Preserving rations, preparing specialty meals, banqueting. Short timescales but high variance in quality; a well-cooked meal has social and sometimes mechanical effects.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Alchemy (Chapter 16).** Consumables, poisons, healing salves, elemental flasks. Alchemy uses the same crafting loop but with an additional "stability" check at the end — an alchemical product that fails the stability check may be a dud, a delayed-release hazard, or a surprise entirely different substance.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Enchantment (Chapter 19).** Rune-binding, infusions, legendary craft. Treated separately because the materials and processes differ sharply from mundane crafting. Enchantment typically happens after an item is already crafted, not during.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Shipbuilding (Chapter 11).** The largest project most characters will ever attempt. Uses the crafting loop but with a dedicated shipyard facility, multiple skilled crews, and timescales measured in seasons. A single character cannot build a ship; they can, at most, oversee the construction.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Siege Engines (Chapter 12).** Field-expedient versions assemble in hours; proper siege engines take weeks. Uses Carpentry or Smithing as primary skill; a Siegecraft secondary covers the specialized joinery and operation-friendliness.
}}

\column

## A Note on Downtime

{{CoreBody
Crafting mostly happens in the space between adventures — the weeks and months when the party is between quests, at home, or waiting on something else. A campaign that never leaves its characters time to breathe is a campaign where crafting will feel like a burden rather than a choice.
}}

{{CoreBody
The book recommends GMs allow at least one week of in-character downtime between adventures for crafting purposes, and ideally more. A month of downtime is enough for most Standard-tier items; a season is enough for Complex work; a year and change covers Masterwork.
}}

{{CoreBody
Where a campaign runs too tightly for any downtime, the book recommends the GM decouple crafting from the main adventure entirely — the party's resident smith is always working in the background, and completed items arrive when the GM feels the pacing is right. The economic rules in this chapter then become color rather than a hard accounting.
}}

## Summary Tables for Quick Reference

{{TableTitle
Crafting at a Glance
}}

| Step | Cost | Time |
|:--|:-:|:-:|
| Source raw materials | 25–35% of retail | Varies |
| Rent / use a facility | 5% of monthly output | Monthly |
| Do the labor | (see Labor Table) | Days–seasons |
| Make the quality roll | — | 1 action at completion |
| Finish to a buyer | Sale at book price | 1–3 days find-buyer |
| Pay guild + tax | 15% of gross | Per sale |

{{TableTitle
Quick-Reference: Is This Worth Trying?
}}

| Situation | Answer |
|:--|:--|
| PC wants to craft in a village | Only small items sell reliably. |
| PC wants to craft in a town, licensed | Yes — artisan income, not adventure income. |
| PC wants to craft unlicensed in a guild town | Sooner or later, expensive. |
| PC wants to build a stronghold's gear | Ideal — own facility, guild irrelevant. |
| PC wants to "print money" | The book is specifically designed to say no. |

{{pageNumber,auto}}
{{footnote
The Ultimate Provisioner's Handbook — Chapter 3: Crafting, Repair & the Working Economy

v0.1 working draft
}}

\page
