# Part II: Arms & Armor

{{vignette
<!-- VIGNETTE GOES HERE -->
}}

\page

# Chapter 4: Armor & Shields

{{Epigraph
*"Plate stops the sword. It does not stop the arrow, the horse, the cold, the fire, the rain, the fatigue, the cost of a new set of straps, or the fact that you will be buried in it if it cannot be removed by morning."*
}}
{{EpigraphAuthor
— Master Armorer Vedric, *Notes on Commissioned Work*
}}

## The Basics

{{CoreBody
Armor is the single largest investment most adventurers will ever make. It is also the most personal. A weapon is a tool; armor is an outfit. It fits, it creaks, it bruises, it saves lives, and it wears out.
}}

{{CoreBody
This chapter presents armor and shields in three sections. The first lays out the optional piecemeal system, which allows characters to build a suit from parts rather than buying a complete outfit off the rack. The second presents full-suit armor by Protection tier, from padded cloth through full plate. The third covers shields — from knuckle-sized bucklers to wall-sized pavises, with a generous tour of the historical curiosities that never quite caught on.
}}

{{CoreBody
Helmets are treated as their own short section between the full-suit armor and the shields, because a helmet is almost always chosen independently of the rest of the harness. A soldier in a mail hauberk with a nasal helm has made two different decisions.
}}

## Reading an Armor Entry

{{CoreBody
Every armor entry follows the format established in Chapter 1, with one addition specific to armor: a Coverage line.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Coverage.** The zones of the body that this piece of armor protects. Zones not listed use the wearer's natural Protection tier (typically Minimal for an ordinary person in clothing) unless filled in with a separate piece of armor.
}}

{{CoreBody
An armor entry with Coverage "Torso, upper arms, thighs" leaves the head, forearms, calves, and hands unprotected. That armor can still be worn without issue — but a strike that lands on an uncovered zone ignores the armor entirely.
}}

{{CoreBody
Whether zones matter in your game depends on whether your group uses the piecemeal system below. If you do not, treat the Coverage line as atmospheric detail and use the main Protection tier as a single global value.
}}

\column

## The Piecemeal System (Optional)

{{CoreBody
Traditional armor rules treat a suit of mail or plate as a single object with a single Protection value. That abstraction works well for fast play. It produces the odd result, however, that an unlucky arrow to an unhelmeted head provides exactly the same protection as a blow to the well-armored chest.
}}

{{CoreBody
The piecemeal system corrects this by tracking armor by zone. A character wearing a chain hauberk, iron greaves, and a leather cap has three distinct protection values, one per zone, and the attacker's hit roll determines which zone is struck.
}}

{{CoreBody
The system works with any of the three rules families covered in Appendix A. It adds one extra roll per attack (the hit location roll) and one per-zone bookkeeping column on the character sheet.
}}

### Hit Location Rolls

{{CoreBody
When a strike connects, roll a standard twenty-sided die (or convert to your system's equivalent).
}}

{{TableTitle
Hit Locations (d20)
}}

| Roll | Location |
|:-:|:--|
| 1–3 | Head |
| 4–6 | Right Arm |
| 7–9 | Left Arm |
| 10–14 | Torso (Chest) |
| 15 | Torso (Abdomen) |
| 16–17 | Right Leg |
| 18–19 | Left Leg |
| 20 | Attacker's choice (if critical) or re-roll (if not) |

{{CoreBody
The roll above is weighted toward the torso — the largest and most reliably struck target — with proportional weight given to head, arms, and legs. Groups preferring finer granularity can split Arms into Upper and Forearm, and Legs into Thigh and Shin, on a d100.
}}

{{CoreBody
When an attack specifically targets a zone (a "called shot" in many systems), skip the roll and apply the attack at whatever penalty the targeting system imposes.
}}

\page

### Per-Zone Protection

{{CoreBody
Each piece of armor lists its Protection tier and its Coverage. When a zone is struck, the protection applied is whatever armor covers that zone — and only that armor.
}}

{{CoreBody
A character wearing a chain shirt (covering torso and upper arms), a gambeson (covering torso under the chain, and extending to upper legs), and no helmet would have the following per-zone profile:
}}

{{TableTitle
Example Piecemeal Profile
}}

| Zone | Armor | Protection |
|:--|:--|:--|
| Head | None | Minimal (natural) |
| Arms (upper) | Chain + gambeson | Medium |
| Arms (lower) | None | Minimal |
| Torso | Chain + gambeson | Medium + Slight (layered) |
| Legs (upper) | Gambeson | Minimal |
| Legs (lower) | None | Minimal |

{{CoreBody
Layering generally grants a Slight improvement to Protection where multiple pieces overlap. Three layers do not stack to a Moderate improvement; layering past two is wasted weight.
}}

### Per-Zone Durability

{{CoreBody
Systems that track armor Durability (Style A and optional for Style B) apply the Durability number to each zone separately. A chain hauberk with a Durability of 50 covers the torso, upper arms, and thighs — each zone has its own 50-point Durability pool.
}}

{{CoreBody
When a zone's Durability reaches zero, that zone's armor is destroyed. The zone's Protection drops to whatever other armor covers it (layered gambeson, for example) or to Minimal if nothing else is in place. Repairing a zone requires the same process as repairing any other armor (Chapter 3).
}}

### Playing Without Piecemeal

{{CoreBody
Groups skipping the piecemeal system should read only the main Protection tier line in each entry and treat the Coverage line as atmospheric. The armor protects globally at its listed tier. This produces faster combat at the cost of some tactical texture.
}}

{{note
##### Called Shots Without Piecemeal

Groups skipping the piecemeal system still benefit from a lightweight called-shot rule: when an attacker targets a visibly unarmored zone (a helmetless head, a bare forearm), apply a Moderate penalty to the strike; on a hit, the armor provides no benefit on that strike. This preserves the feel of piecemeal without the bookkeeping.
}}

\column

## Soft Armor — Minimal Tier

{{CoreBody
Cloth, quilting, and heavy wool. The armor of the poor, the practical, and the prepared-for-weather. Soft armor is rarely a complete defense on its own, but it is the universal foundation for everything above it — every suit of mail and plate is worn over something like this.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Padded Gambeson.** A quilted cloth coat stuffed with wool, rags, or cotton waste, stitched into vertical channels. The foundation garment under nearly all medieval armor. Worn alone, a gambeson turns glancing blows and absorbs some of the shock of heavier hits.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Minimal
Durability: 30
Weight: 8 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 12 gp
Coverage: Torso, upper arms, upper legs
Traits: Layering Foundation (Slight bonus to the effective Protection tier of any metal armor worn over it)
}}

{{CoreBody
A gambeson will keep its wearer warm in cool weather and soaked and miserable in hot. The cheap versions are stuffed with rag waste and stink within a season; the expensive versions are stuffed with carded wool or cotton and last years.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Heavy Cloth Coat.** A cloak, tunic, or traveling coat of doubled wool or felted hide. Not designed as armor, but thick enough to deflect minor blows.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Minimal (half tier; Slight in game terms)
Durability: 15
Weight: 4 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 5 gp
Coverage: Torso
Traits: Winter warmth.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Aketon.** A heavier, longer version of the gambeson, extending to the knees and worn alone by foot soldiers who could not afford mail. Functionally similar to the gambeson but with fuller coverage.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Minimal
Durability: 40
Weight: 12 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 20 gp
Coverage: Torso, upper arms, thighs, calves
Traits: Layering Foundation.
}}

\page

{{note
##### Silk and Other Surprises

Historical Mongol and Chinese light cavalry wore silk under their armor. Silk does not stop an arrow — but if the arrow penetrated cloth and flesh, the silk would wrap around the arrowhead rather than tearing, allowing the wound to be cleaned and the arrow withdrawn cleanly rather than pushed through. Groups wanting to model this can treat silk as granting a Slight bonus to recovery rolls from arrow wounds.

A full silk shirt appropriate to this use runs 40 gp and weighs practically nothing, but acquiring one outside a trading city is difficult.
}}

## Light Armor — Light Tier

{{CoreHanging
**Leather Armor.** A tunic or vest of tough tanned leather, often reinforced at the shoulders and waist with doubled strips or rivets. Common among skirmishers, scouts, and characters who expect to move more than they fight.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Light
Durability: 40
Weight: 10 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 30 gp
Coverage: Torso, upper arms
Traits: Slight Agility penalty if wearer is untrained.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Hardened Leather (Cuir Bouilli).** Leather stiffened by soaking in hot water or wax, producing nearly the rigidity of light wood. Used for breastplates, vambraces, and greaves in cultures without widespread metalworking.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Light (approaching Medium when reinforced)
Durability: 55
Weight: 14 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 50 gp
Coverage: Torso
Traits: Brittle in deep cold (Slight Durability penalty below freezing). Melts near open flame — Moderate Durability penalty against fire attacks.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Studded Leather.** Leather armor reinforced with small metal rivets or studs. Most of the actual protection comes from the leather itself; the studs are ornamentation and a Slight edge against glancing blows.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Light
Durability: 45
Weight: 13 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 45 gp
Coverage: Torso, upper arms
Traits: Slight bonus against slashing weapons.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Hide Armor.** Fur-on, minimally processed heavy hides — bearskin, elk, auroch. Worn by cultures without industrial tanneries. Bulkier than cured leather and heavier, but offers comparable protection and significant warmth.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Light
Durability: 50
Weight: 18 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 25 gp
Coverage: Torso, upper arms, upper legs
Traits: Moderate cold resistance. Attracts vermin if uncared for.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Light Scale Shirt.** A short, sleeveless coat of overlapping metal scales sewn to a cloth or leather backing. Flexible where solid plate is rigid; heavier than leather; louder than mail.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Light (Medium against thrusting)
Durability: 55
Weight: 22 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 85 gp
Coverage: Torso
Traits: Slight bonus vs. thrust attacks. Rattles audibly — Moderate Stealth penalty.
}}

## Medium Armor — Medium Tier

{{CoreHanging
**Chain Shirt (Hauberk).** A shirt of interlinked steel rings, waist or knee length, with or without sleeves. The workhorse of medieval infantry. Reliably available in any substantial settlement, maintainable in the field, and protective against almost everything except concentrated mass.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Medium
Durability: 50
Weight: 30 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 250 gp
Coverage: Torso, upper arms, thighs (knee-length version)
Traits: Moderate penalty to Agility-based skills while worn. Requires a gambeson underneath to avoid bruising from transmitted blows.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Full Chain Hauberk.** A longer, fuller version of the chain shirt, extending to the knees and including integral mittens and a coif (hood). Adds weight and coverage but reduces mobility further.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Medium
Durability: 70
Weight: 45 lbs · Bulk: 4
Cost: 400 gp
Coverage: Torso, full arms, thighs, calves, head
Traits: Major penalty to Agility. Major penalty to swimming (drowning risk).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Scale Armor.** A long coat of overlapping metal scales, reaching to mid-thigh. Historically common across the Byzantine, Persian, and steppe cultures. Heavier than chain but slightly more resistant to thrusts.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Medium
Durability: 65
Weight: 40 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 300 gp
Coverage: Torso, upper arms, thighs
Traits: Slight bonus vs. thrust. Moderate Stealth penalty (rattling).
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Ring Mail.** Armor of metal rings sewn flat to a leather or cloth backing — distinct from true chain, in which the rings are interlinked. Cheaper than chain, less protective, and much more common among town guards and minor nobility.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Medium (low end)
Durability: 40
Weight: 28 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 130 gp
Coverage: Torso, upper arms
Traits: Slight Durability penalty from rust if not maintained.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Lamellar.** A system of small rectangular plates laced together with leather thongs into rigid horizontal strips, then into a coat or cuirass. Extremely flexible, very protective, and notoriously time-consuming to repair in the field.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Medium
Durability: 70
Weight: 35 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 320 gp
Coverage: Torso
Traits: Major Durability bonus against slash. Repair requires specialized skill and takes twice as long as chain.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Light Brigandine.** A cloth or leather jacket with small steel plates riveted between the layers, hidden from view. Offers plate-like protection from the outside; looks like a nice coat from the outside.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Medium
Durability: 60
Weight: 25 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 280 gp
Coverage: Torso
Traits: Slight bonus to Disguise and Persuasion while worn in civilian contexts (appears to be a fine coat).
}}

## Heavy Armor — Heavy Tier

{{CoreHanging
**Plated Mail.** Chain armor with large steel plates riveted or sewn over the torso, upper arms, and outer thighs. The transitional bridge between mail and plate; common in Eastern Europe and the Ottoman world.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Heavy
Durability: 90
Weight: 50 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 550 gp
Coverage: Torso, upper arms, thighs
Traits: Moderate Agility penalty. Major Stealth penalty. Requires gambeson foundation.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Heavy Brigandine.** A full-torso coat with larger, closer-packed steel plates, often with shoulder-plates (pauldrons) and a mail skirt. The choice of many town militias and professional mercenaries.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Heavy
Durability: 85
Weight: 40 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 620 gp
Coverage: Torso, upper arms
Traits: Moderate Agility penalty. Slight bonus to Disguise (still appears as a heavy coat from a distance).
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Partial Plate.** A breastplate and backplate with associated arm-plates and thigh-plates, worn over a gambeson and usually a chain shirt. Covers the vital zones in solid steel and leaves the limbs lighter for mobility.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Heavy (Extreme on the torso zone only)
Durability: 80 (torso) / 60 (limbs)
Weight: 45 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 700 gp
Coverage: Torso, upper arms, thighs
Traits: Moderate Agility penalty. Slight Presence bonus in martial contexts.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Coat of Plates.** A stiffened canvas or leather coat with a grid of large rectangular steel plates riveted throughout. The immediate predecessor of plate armor; popular from the 13th through early 15th centuries.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Heavy
Durability: 75
Weight: 35 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 480 gp
Coverage: Torso
Traits: Moderate Agility penalty.
}}

## Extreme Armor — Extreme Tier

{{CoreHanging
**Full Plate Harness.** A fully articulated steel suit covering the wearer from head to foot, with flexible joints at the elbows, knees, and hips. The pinnacle of pre-industrial armor. Expensive, custom-fitted, and almost invulnerable to the sword — and almost useless against a warhammer, a crossbow at close range, or a coordinated tackle.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Extreme
Durability: 140
Weight: 55 lbs · Bulk: 3 (surprisingly mobile for its weight when fitted properly)
Cost: 3,000 gp
Coverage: Entire body except face and palms
Traits: Major Agility penalty when ill-fitted; Slight when fitted. Requires a dedicated squire to don (Challenging skill check in under thirty minutes). Major Swimming penalty (wearer will drown if unconscious in water). Moderate cold conduction.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Transitional Plate (Gothic Harness).** The refined, late-medieval evolution of full plate, with fluted surfaces designed to deflect strikes and bolts, articulated gauntlets and sabatons, and integrated mail skirt.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Extreme
Durability: 160
Weight: 50 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 5,000 gp
Coverage: Entire body except face
Traits: Slight deflection bonus (fluted surfaces redirect edged strikes). Slight Presence bonus in martial contexts (the suit is unmistakably expensive). Other traits as full plate.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Tournament Plate.** A specialized, reinforced variant designed for jousting and ceremonial combat. Heavier and less mobile than war-fit plate, but with enhanced protection against controlled-contact weapons like lances and maces. See Chapter 18 for the full rules on tournament combat.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Extreme (Devastating threshold against lance impacts)
Durability: 200
Weight: 75 lbs · Bulk: 4
Cost: 6,000 gp
Coverage: Entire body
Traits: Severe Agility penalty outside of mounted combat. Wearer cannot run; cannot mount an unbarded horse unaided.
}}

{{note
##### Fantasy Variant: Dwarven Plate

Dwarven-forged plate of any tier gains a Moderate Durability bonus, a Slight weight reduction, and a characteristic "ringing" note when struck — the sound is used by dwarven smiths as a quality test. A set of dwarven full plate runs 8,000 gp (Major cost bonus over human work) and takes approximately three times the baseline labor. Dwarven artisans refuse to sell to non-dwarven buyers in some settings.
}}

{{note
##### Fantasy Variant: Elven Moon-Silver Mail

A chain hauberk woven from moon-silver rings retains the full Protection of steel chain while reducing weight to 12 lbs and bulk to 1. The wearer gains a Slight Agility bonus. The armor glows faintly in moonlight (ten-foot dim radius) and cannot be repaired outside an elven forge. Cost: 3,000 gp (Severe bonus over human work).
}}

{{note
##### Fantasy Variant: Dragon-Bone Scale

A scale coat built from calcified dragon-bone scales. Protection tier matches lamellar (Medium), but weight is 25 lbs, and the armor carries the elemental affinity of its source dragon — fire-drake scale grants Moderate fire resistance; frost-drake, cold; lightning, electrical. Cost: varies with source, starting at 2,500 gp and climbing. Many dragons consider wearing their kin's scale a provocation worth hunting over.
}}

\column

## Helmets

{{CoreBody
Helmets are almost always chosen independently of the suit of armor under them. A soldier in a mail hauberk with a kettle helm has made two separate decisions, and the reader of this chapter is encouraged to do the same. The entries below cover the typical options from peasant to prince.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Skull Cap.** A padded leather cap reinforced with thin iron strips or a simple steel skull. Common among militia, sergeants, and anyone who could not afford anything better.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Light (head zone only)
Durability: 20
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 10 gp
Coverage: Head (top only, no ear or nape protection)
Traits: No vision or hearing penalty.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Kettle Helm.** A wide-brimmed iron hat, simple to manufacture and popular with foot soldiers, siege crews, and anyone who expected to be shot at from above.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Medium (head zone)
Durability: 35
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 35 gp
Coverage: Head (top and sides)
Traits: Moderate bonus to rainy or sunny weather comfort. Slight protection vs. arrows and thrown projectiles from above.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Nasal Helm.** A rounded steel helm with a vertical bar descending to cover the nose. Common from the Viking age through the early crusades.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Medium
Durability: 40
Weight: 4 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 55 gp
Coverage: Head (top, sides, nose)
Traits: Slight Perception penalty (the nasal bar obstructs some vision).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Great Helm.** A large, flat-topped cylindrical helmet enclosing the entire head, with narrow eye-slits and breathing holes. Heavy, hot, and nearly invulnerable — the iconic helmet of crusading knights.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Heavy
Durability: 70
Weight: 9 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 180 gp
Coverage: Entire head including neck
Traits: Major Perception penalty (narrow slits, muffled hearing). Slight Initiative penalty. Wearer cannot drink or eat while wearing.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Sallet.** A streamlined steel helmet extending back to protect the neck, worn with a separate bevor covering the throat and lower face. The 15th-century successor to the great helm, offering comparable protection with far better visibility.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Heavy
Durability: 80
Weight: 6 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 220 gp
Coverage: Head, upper neck (lower face with bevor)
Traits: Slight Perception penalty only with visor lowered; none with visor raised.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Armet.** A close-fitting, articulated helmet with a hinged visor and integrated chin-plate. The standard knight's helmet from the late 15th century onward; fitted to the wearer, comfortable, and fully protective.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Extreme
Durability: 90
Weight: 6 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 400 gp
Coverage: Entire head
Traits: Slight Perception penalty with visor lowered. Requires custom fitting (standard sizes fit poorly).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Barbute.** A T-shaped opening in an otherwise fully enclosed helmet, offering a compromise between the great helm's protection and the sallet's visibility. An Italian favorite.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Heavy
Durability: 75
Weight: 6 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 200 gp
Coverage: Head
Traits: Moderate Perception penalty. No visor to raise — the opening is fixed.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Zischägge (Lobster-Tail Helm).** An open-faced helmet with a characteristic articulated neck-guard of horizontal plates descending in imbricated style. Favored by heavy cavalry from the 16th through 18th centuries.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Heavy
Durability: 85
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 280 gp
Coverage: Head, neck
Traits: No Perception penalty (open face). Slight Cold penalty (open face in winter). Extensive articulated neck protection — Major bonus vs. strikes to the neck from behind.
}}

\column

## Shields

{{CoreBody
A shield is a second armor system, worn on one arm, that provides two distinct benefits: a Parry bonus (added to the wielder's defensive rolls) and a separate Durability pool that absorbs damage that would otherwise hit the wielder's armor or body.
}}

{{CoreBody
Shields can be actively used in combat and also passively "planted" as cover. A tower shield or pavise is usually planted; a buckler is never. The shield entries below note which modes each supports.
}}

### Shield Mechanics in Brief

{{CoreHanging
**Parry Bonus.** Applied to any defensive roll while the shield is wielded. Typical values are Slight (buckler) to Moderate (kite) with rare exceptions.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Shield Durability.** A separate pool of hit points. When a strike beats the wielder's Protection and would hit, the shield can intercept — absorbing the damage against Shield Durability first. When Durability reaches zero, the shield is Shattered and the Parry bonus is lost.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Planted / Cover Mode.** Large shields can be braced against the ground, providing total or partial cover to the wielder and anyone crouching behind them. A planted shield cannot Parry; the shield is stationary.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Active / Parry Mode.** The shield is carried and used dynamically. Grants Parry bonus; Shield Durability absorbs damage normally. Default for small and medium shields.
}}

{{CoreBody
A round shield strapped to the back while the wielder uses two hands on a polearm provides no benefit and can be struck normally. Shields grant their mechanical benefit only when actively carried.
}}

### Buckler

{{CoreHanging
**Steel Buckler.** A small, circular steel shield approximately ten inches across, held in the fist rather than strapped to the forearm. Too small to protect the body by interposition; instead used to deflect and disrupt the opponent's weapon. Preferred by duelists and light skirmishers.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Parry Bonus: Slight (Moderate against a specifically-declared parry-and-bind)
Shield Durability: 35
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 0
Cost: 30 gp
Mode: Active only.
Traits: Cannot be planted. Allows the wielder to fight normally with their primary weapon. Slight bonus to Disarm attempts.
}}

\page

### Small and Medium Shields

{{CoreHanging
**Target (Small Round Shield).** A round wooden shield approximately two feet across, faced with hide or thin steel and centered on a hand-grip behind a steel boss. The signature shield of the Viking age and the Scottish Highlands.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Parry Bonus: Slight
Shield Durability: 55
Weight: 4 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 35 gp
Mode: Active only.
Traits: Spike variants add a Slight bonus to unarmed strike damage when shield-bashing.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Round Shield (Medium).** A larger wooden shield, three to four feet across, faced with rawhide or steel, carried on the arm via straps and a forearm-grip. The standard shield of most pre-kite-era foot soldiers.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Parry Bonus: Moderate
Shield Durability: 80
Weight: 7 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 50 gp
Mode: Active. Can be planted in emergencies (Slight cover).
Traits: Can be formed into a shield wall with adjacent allies (see Chapter 8).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Heater Shield.** A flattened triangular shield, descended from the kite shield but shortened to be easier to use on horseback and on foot alike. The shield of the high medieval knight.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Parry Bonus: Moderate
Shield Durability: 75
Weight: 8 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 80 gp
Mode: Active.
Traits: Often emblazoned with heraldry (Slight Presence bonus when recognized). Slight bonus to mounted combat.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Kite Shield.** A long, teardrop-shaped shield descending from the shoulder to the knee. Favored by Norman cavalry and early crusading infantry; excellent protection for a mounted leg.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Parry Bonus: Moderate
Shield Durability: 95
Weight: 10 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 90 gp
Mode: Active.
Traits: Slight bonus vs. low-angle strikes (mounted use). Moderate bonus to covering an adjacent ally.
}}

\column

### Large Shields

{{CoreHanging
**Tower Shield.** A massive rectangular wooden shield, nearly as tall as the wielder, carried on the arm and braced against the shoulder. Too heavy for extended active combat; most effective when planted.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Parry Bonus: Slight (active) / Cover (planted)
Shield Durability: 120
Weight: 18 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 120 gp
Mode: Both. Shifts between active and planted as an action.
Traits: Planted: provides Moderate cover to wielder, Slight cover to one adjacent ally. Severe penalty to movement while actively carried.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Pavise.** A large, rectangular standing shield with a spike or foot at the base, planted in front of a crossbowman or gunner. Originally Italian; spread throughout medieval and renaissance warfare.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Parry Bonus: Slight (active; awkward)
Shield Durability: 140
Weight: 16 lbs · Bulk: 4
Cost: 110 gp
Mode: Primarily planted.
Traits: Planted: Major cover to wielder (while crouching) and any one adjacent character. Standard for siege crews, crossbowmen, and gun-batteries.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Heavy Pavise (Siege).** An oversized pavise mounted on wheels or a fold-out stand, used by siege engineers and sappers to shelter from arrows while approaching fortified walls.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Parry Bonus: — (cannot be used actively)
Shield Durability: 200
Weight: 45 lbs · Bulk: 5
Cost: 180 gp
Mode: Planted only.
Traits: Major cover to 1–3 characters. Requires two characters to move. Can be stacked with other pavises for additional cover.
}}

\page

### Historical Curiosities

{{note
##### The Kalkan — The Arrow-Eater

A light shield of concentric woven reeds bound with silk, with a central steel boss. Used by Crimean Tartars and Polish light cavalry through the 17th century. Far more effective against arrows than its light weight would suggest: the flexible reed absorbs the force of a thrust rather than breaking.

**Parry:** Moderate · **Durability:** 40 · **Weight:** 3 lbs · **Bulk:** 1 · **Cost:** 90 gp

**Traits:** Moderate bonus vs. arrows and crossbow bolts. Slight Durability penalty against heavy edged weapons.
}}

{{note
##### The Hungarian Tartsche — The Winged Shield

An asymmetrical shield with a tall "wing" extending up one side, designed specifically to protect the rider's neck and shoulder from lances and sabers while not obstructing the rider's sword arm.

**Parry:** Moderate · **Durability:** 70 · **Weight:** 6 lbs · **Bulk:** 2 · **Cost:** 140 gp

**Traits:** Moderate bonus against attacks targeting the head or neck.
}}

{{note
##### The Lantern-Shield

An Italian experimental design: a buckler with a shuttered lantern mounted at its center, a serrated "sword-breaker" edge along the rim, and a hook for catching bridle reins.

**Parry:** Slight · **Durability:** 40 · **Weight:** 4 lbs · **Bulk:** 1 · **Cost:** 250 gp

**Traits:** Lantern shuttered closed: normal buckler. Lantern unshuttered: any opponent directly in front must make a Willpower check at Challenging difficulty or be Dazzled (Moderate penalty to attack rolls for one round). Serrated edge attempts Disarm at Slight bonus. Reliably malfunctions in rain.
}}

\column

{{note
##### The Zertsalo — The Mirror-Plate

Not a shield held in the hand, but a large circular polished steel plate strapped over the center of the chest, as a reinforcement layer over a mail or brigandine suit. A Russian and Persian tradition; the polished surface was both practical (deflecting arrows) and ceremonial.

**Coverage:** Torso (upgrade) · **Durability Bonus:** +20 to torso · **Weight:** 4 lbs · **Bulk:** 1 · **Cost:** 140 gp

**Traits:** When worn over existing medium-or-heavier armor, upgrades torso Protection by a Slight bonus. Slight Presence bonus (the polished steel is impressive). In direct sunlight, a character with a clear line of sight to the mirror must make a Willpower check at Routine difficulty or be Dazzled (Slight Perception penalty for one round).
}}

{{note
##### The Zertsalo-Shield — The Mirror Array

A theoretical siege shield mounted with rows of small polished steel plates, designed to reflect the sun into the eyes of defenders on a castle wall. Historically almost never used, because the wielder became the most obvious target on the entire battlefield.

**Parry:** Slight · **Durability:** 90 · **Weight:** 14 lbs · **Bulk:** 3 · **Cost:** 300 gp

**Traits:** In bright sunlight and with a clear line of sight to the enemy position, enemies attacking from the front suffer a Moderate penalty to Strike for as long as the wielder stands still. The wielder is considered Visible — any ranged attack against them, including by allies of the intended targets, gains a Slight bonus. Only useful in very specific conditions; rarely practical.
}}

\page

{{note
##### The Tarkh — The Gun-Shield

A heavy iron buckler or round shield with a built-in matchlock pistol barrel protruding through the boss. When the shield was pointed at the target, the wielder could fire the single ball before closing to melee.

**Parry:** Slight · **Durability:** 50 · **Weight:** 10 lbs · **Bulk:** 2 · **Cost:** 400 gp (plus ammunition)

**Traits:** Contains a single-shot smoothbore barrel (Solid damage on a hit; range limited to 30 feet before accuracy collapses). Reload requires 3 actions and a secure position. Historically, recoil bruised the wielder's arm badly enough that the weapon was rarely used twice in the same engagement. Moderate Weight penalty and Slight Agility penalty due to bulk.
}}

{{note
##### The Bronze Heraldic Shield

A ceremonial variant of any wooden shield, faced with hammered bronze and ornately decorated with embossed heraldry. Offers no mechanical advantage over a painted wooden shield, but signals noble bearing. Worn into tournament and ceremonial combat; rarely taken to war.

**Parry:** As base shield · **Durability:** As base · **Weight:** +2 lbs · **Bulk:** +0 · **Cost:** +Major cost bonus

**Traits:** Moderate Presence bonus in social and ceremonial contexts. Moderate Durability penalty against heavy blows (the bronze face dents visibly).
}}

\column

## Material Variations for Armor and Shields

{{CoreBody
Any armor or shield in this chapter can be made of any reasonable material from Chapter 2. The adjustments work as they do for weapons — material modifiers apply to the base item's Durability, Weight, and Cost, with special traits noted.
}}

{{TableTitle
Common Material Swaps for Armor
}}

| Material | Durability | Weight | Cost | Special |
|:--|:-:|:-:|:-:|:--|
| Iron (vs. steel) | −Slight | — | −Slight | More maintenance |
| Bronze | −Moderate | — | −Slight/+Major | Bypasses iron-ward |
| Pattern-welded | +Moderate | — | +Major | Presence bonus |
| Boiled leather | +Moderate | +Slight | +Moderate | Fire vulnerable |
| Ceramic plates | −Severe | −Slight | −Major | One-hit shatter |
| Dwarven steel | +Moderate | −Slight | +Major | Ringing tone |
| Dark-Iron | +Major | +Moderate | +Severe | Fire-absorbing |
| Dragon-Bone scale | +Moderate | −Moderate | +Severe | Elemental affinity |
| Moon-Silver chain | — | −Major | +Severe | Silver properties |
| Living Wood shield | +Slight | — | +Major | Self-repairing |

## Donning, Doffing, and Daily Wear

{{CoreBody
Putting on armor is not instantaneous. The heavier the armor, the longer it takes — and the more likely it is that the wearer needs help.
}}

{{TableTitle
Time to Don/Doff Armor
}}

| Armor Tier | Alone | With Assistance |
|:--|:-:|:-:|
| Minimal (soft) | 1 minute | — |
| Light | 3 minutes | 2 minutes |
| Medium | 10 minutes | 6 minutes |
| Heavy | 20 minutes | 12 minutes |
| Extreme | Cannot be donned alone | 20–30 minutes |

{{CoreBody
A character surprised while armored must often make do with partial coverage. The GM determines on a case-by-case basis which pieces are available; a reasonable default is that a character wakes in their gambeson and undergarments only, with the full suit across the room on a rack.
}}

{{CoreBody
Extended wear has its own costs. A character in full plate for more than six hours without rest suffers Moderate fatigue effects (Slight penalty to all physical checks); longer wear worsens this. Most knights removed the harness at every opportunity.
}}

\page

## Maintenance, Repair, and Resale

{{CoreBody
The general rules of Chapter 3 apply to armor. A few specifics:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Oil Daily.** Metal armor that is not oiled after each use develops surface rust within a week of damp weather. Rusted armor carries a Slight Durability penalty until cleaned.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Strap Replacement.** Leather straps, padding, and lacing fail long before the metal they bind. Budget a Slight cost bonus (×1.25) per year of active use for replacement consumables.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Battlefield Repair.** Field-expedient repair with a smith's portable kit restores a Slight amount of Durability per day; proper repair requires a facility and the rules in Chapter 3.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Resale.** Used armor sells for 50% of its book price at standard condition, scaling down with Durability loss. A set of battle-damaged mail at 30% Durability sells for perhaps 20% of list. Distinctive ornamentation, heraldry, or visible damage (blood, dents) may make the armor nearly unsellable at any price.
}}

## A Note on Fitting

{{CoreBody
Most of the armor in this chapter comes in standard sizes: Small, Medium, Large. Items of partial plate and above are generally fitted to the wearer and cannot be comfortably transferred between characters of different size and build.
}}

{{CoreBody
A character wearing armor fitted to someone else suffers a Moderate Agility penalty and a Slight Protection penalty (the armor is held at odd angles, gaps open where the wearer's body does not fill the suit). A skilled armorer can re-fit a suit of plate for a new wearer — treat as a Complex crafting project at 25% of the base labor time and material cost.
}}

{{CoreBody
Magical armor resists re-fitting in some settings; a rune-bound or infused suit may not accept a new wearer at all. See Chapter 19 for enchantment rules.
}}

\column

\page

# Chapter 5: Blades & Bludgeons

{{Epigraph
*"A sword is a conversation. A club is a question."*
}}
{{EpigraphAuthor
— Master-at-Arms Orlin, *The Veteran's Primer*
}}

## How to Read This Chapter

{{CoreBody
Hand weapons — the blades and bludgeons that a person carries in one or two hands — are organized here by function rather than size. Knives come first, as the smallest and most universal. Then swords, in ascending weight and reach. Then axes, then maces and hammers, then flails, then the specialty and exotic pieces that fall outside the main categories.
}}

{{CoreBody
Each weapon entry follows the format introduced in Chapter 1: a brief descriptor, a stat block, a paragraph or two of prose, and where relevant, sidebar notes for fantasy variants, curiosities, or scaling concerns. Polearms — pikes, halberds, bec-de-corbin, and the rest of the long-reach family — are treated separately in Chapter 6 because their mechanics differ significantly.
}}

## Weapon Traits

{{CoreBody
Many weapons grant specific mechanical benefits beyond their raw damage. These are abbreviated as "traits" in stat blocks and listed below for reference. Where a weapon has more than one trait, all of them apply.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Balanced.** Well-distributed weight; grants a Slight bonus to defensive rolls when used to parry.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Versatile.** Usable one-handed or two-handed. Two-handed use adds a Slight damage bonus and sacrifices shield use.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Two-Handed.** Cannot be used one-handed without a Major penalty. Prevents shield use.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Reach.** Extends effective melee range beyond a normal weapon. Grants the first strike in an engagement where the opponent has lesser reach; suffers a Moderate penalty in close quarters (within grappling distance).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Finesse.** Attack rolls use the wielder's Agility rather than Strength. Damage uses whichever is lower (finesse weapons reward precision, not power).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Quick.** Slight Initiative bonus. Strikes faster than most weapons of its tier.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Concealable.** Can be hidden on the person under ordinary clothing. Grants a Slight Stealth bonus to drawing the weapon unnoticed.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Armor-Crack.** Designed to defeat armor. Bypasses one Protection tier on a successful hit against heavy or extreme armor.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Hooking.** The weapon has a hook, pick, or crook. Grants a Slight bonus to Disarm and Trip attempts.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Entangling.** Can wrap around weapons, limbs, or small shields. Grants a Slight bonus to Disarm and ignores shield Parry bonus on a successful hit.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Brutal.** Designed to drop an opponent through sheer impact. Grants a Slight bonus to Knockdown and Stun attempts.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Vitals.** Short, sharp, designed for precision thrusts in tight spaces. Grants a Moderate bonus to Called Shots against exposed zones.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Parrying.** Primarily a defensive weapon. Grants a Moderate Parry bonus but Slight damage penalty on attacks.
}}

\page

## Knives & Daggers

{{CoreBody
Every adventurer carries a knife. Most carry several. Knives are the universal tool, the last-ditch weapon, and the most-concealed implement in the book. A dagger is technically a knife optimized for combat over utility — though most actual fighters care far less about the distinction than the book does.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Utility Knife.** A small belt knife, four to six inches of blade, used for eating, cutting rope, skinning game, and occasional self-defense. Every peasant owns one.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Glancing
Weight: 0.5 lbs · Bulk: 0
Cost: 2 gp
Traits: Concealable, Quick.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Dagger.** A double-edged blade of eight to twelve inches, designed primarily for combat. The classic off-hand or backup weapon. Can be thrown in a pinch.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Glancing
Weight: 1 lb · Bulk: 0
Cost: 15 gp
Traits: Concealable, Quick, Finesse.
Thrown Range: 20 feet.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Stiletto.** A narrow, needle-pointed blade without cutting edges, designed specifically to thrust between the plates of armor or into the soft spots of the body. Italian by origin; adopted universally by assassins and armor-clad duelists.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Glancing
Weight: 0.8 lbs · Bulk: 0
Cost: 25 gp
Traits: Concealable, Vitals, Armor-Crack.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Cinquedea.** A five-finger-wide, wedge-shaped Italian dagger that approaches a shortsword in length but retains dagger-class handling. Designed for powerful thrusts in crowded indoor combat where a full sword has no room to swing.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 0
Cost: 40 gp
Traits: Vitals, Quick. No Reach penalty in tight quarters.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Sword-Breaker.** A dagger-length parrying weapon with deep serrated notches along one or both edges, designed to catch and bind an opponent's blade. Nearly useless as a primary attacking weapon; excellent in a skilled off-hand.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Glancing (−Slight)
Weight: 1.5 lbs · Bulk: 0
Cost: 55 gp
Traits: Parrying, Entangling. Moderate bonus to Disarm.
}}

\column

## Short Blades

{{CoreHanging
**Shortsword.** A straight, double-edged blade of eighteen to twenty-four inches. The universal sidearm of foot soldiers from antiquity through the early renaissance. Cheap enough to issue by the hundreds, reliable enough to win wars with.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 50 gp
Traits: Balanced, Quick.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Gladius.** A leaf-shaped Roman shortsword, slightly shorter and heavier than the standard shortsword. Optimized for thrusting from behind a shield wall.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid (+Slight vs. unarmored)
Weight: 2.5 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 60 gp
Traits: Balanced. Slight damage bonus when used in a shield wall (see Chapter 8).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Seax / Scramasax.** A single-edged Germanic utility blade, twelve to twenty inches long. Bridges the gap between large knife and shortsword. Worn openly as a belt weapon; respectable where a shortsword would mark the carrier as a soldier.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 1.8 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 40 gp
Traits: Quick. Slight Stealth bonus (read as a tool, not a weapon).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Rapier.** A long, narrow thrusting blade with a complex guard, thirty-six to forty-four inches of blade. A civilian dueling weapon and court sidearm; not a battlefield weapon, despite fantasy's insistence otherwise.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 2.5 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 120 gp
Traits: Finesse, Quick, Vitals, Balanced.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Hanger.** A short curved infantry saber, twenty-four to thirty inches. Carried by sailors, scouts, and officers who wanted a real sword but not a full cavalry blade.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 2.2 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 70 gp
Traits: Balanced, Quick. Slight bonus on a ship's deck or in dense foliage.
}}

\page

## One-Handed Swords

{{CoreHanging
**Arming Sword.** The medieval knight's sidearm and foot-soldier's standard sword. Straight, cross-hilted, and double-edged, thirty to thirty-four inches. Reliable, repairable, and universally understood.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 3 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 85 gp
Traits: Balanced, Versatile.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Longsword.** A balanced cut-and-thrust blade, thirty-six to forty-four inches, designed for one- or two-handed use. The iconic medieval infantry and knightly weapon from the 14th through 16th centuries.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy (Crushing two-handed)
Weight: 4 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 90 gp
Traits: Versatile, Balanced.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Falchion.** A heavy, cleaver-like single-edged blade, twenty-eight to thirty-four inches. Favored by foot soldiers for its ability to deliver devastating cuts without requiring a sword-smith's finesse to make.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 3.5 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 70 gp
Traits: Brutal.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Sabre (Light).** A curved, single-edged cavalry sword, thirty-two to thirty-eight inches. Designed for mounted slashing attacks against unarmored or lightly-armored targets.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 2.8 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 100 gp
Traits: Balanced, Quick. Slight damage bonus from horseback.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Scimitar.** A broad curved blade, twenty-eight to thirty-six inches, heavier in the cut than a sabre. Common across the eastern and southern empires.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 3.2 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 95 gp
Traits: Balanced, Brutal.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Khopesh.** An ancient curved sword with an inverted hook at the tip, used to catch shields and pull opponents off balance. Bronze-age origin; still encountered in desert-kingdom settings.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 3.5 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 110 gp
Traits: Hooking, Brutal. Can be used to pull away an opponent's shield.
}}

\column

## Two-Handed Swords

{{CoreHanging
**Bastard Sword.** A longsword built slightly longer and heavier than standard, optimized for two-handed use but still manageable one-handed by a strong wielder. A compromise sword favored by cavalry and elite infantry.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 140 gp
Traits: Versatile (at Moderate penalty one-handed), Balanced.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Greatsword.** A true two-handed infantry sword, fifty to sixty inches overall. Designed to counter pike formations and hack through the lightly-armored bodies behind them.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 6 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 200 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Slight).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Zweihänder.** An oversized two-handed sword, sixty to seventy-two inches overall, specialized for breaking pike formations. Wielded by the German *Doppelsöldner*, who were paid double for the privilege of getting to the pikes first.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Devastating
Weight: 8 lbs · Bulk: 4
Cost: 350 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Moderate). Severe penalty in tight quarters. Slight bonus against pike formations.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Claymore.** The Scottish two-handed sword, fifty-five to sixty-five inches overall, with a characteristic downward-angled cross-guard. Lighter and faster than the zweihänder, with better balance for individual combat.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing (+Slight against unshielded)
Weight: 5.5 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 220 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Slight), Balanced.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Flamberge.** A two-handed sword with a distinctive wavy, "flame-bladed" edge. The waves produce a vibrating-saw effect on cuts that prolongs healing and terrifies wounded opponents. Functionally similar to a zweihänder but heavier on damage and lighter on speed.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 7 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 400 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Slight). Wounds caused by the flamberge bleed longer — Slight Endurance penalty applied per wound for one scene.
}}

\page

## Axes

{{CoreBody
Axes are the working-class weapon. Cheaper to make than swords, easier to repair, more forgiving of poor technique, and lethal to lightly-armored troops. The greatsword may be the weapon of myth; the axe wins most of the battles.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Hand Axe.** A working axe's cousin, twenty to twenty-six inches long, usable as a tool or a weapon interchangeably. The most common weapon in the developing world for very good reason.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 3 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 25 gp
Traits: Brutal. Thrown Range: 20 feet.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Battle Axe.** A proper military axe, twenty-eight to thirty-four inches, with a wider blade, a back-spike, and a reinforced haft. Not a working tool; made specifically to harm.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 4 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 55 gp
Traits: Brutal, Hooking. Versatile.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Bearded Axe.** A Norse-style axe with a distinctive long, hooked lower edge that extends below the haft. The extension catches shields and limbs, enabling disarms and trips that a regular axe cannot.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 4.5 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 75 gp
Traits: Brutal, Hooking (Moderate). Versatile.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Great Axe.** A full two-handed axe, fifty to sixty inches, with a broad head and heavy haft. The peer of the greatsword in battlefield damage.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 7 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 180 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Brutal.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Dane Axe.** A longer, thinner-bladed two-handed axe, often with a six-foot haft. Favored by Varangian guards and heavy northern infantry. More reach than a great axe; somewhat less weight.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 6 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 200 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Slight), Brutal, Hooking.
}}

\column

## Maces & Hammers

{{CoreBody
The crushing weapon family is older than metalworking — a rock on a stick predates civilization — and never fell out of use even as armor advanced. A mace or hammer does not need to pierce plate; it needs only to deliver its impact to the body inside. A well-struck warhammer will crush the ribs of a man in a breastplate without marking the plate itself.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Club.** A wooden bludgeon, thirty inches long, with no metal. The default weapon of the desperate and the weapon-poor.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 5 gp
Traits: Brutal.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Heavy Club / Cudgel.** A reinforced wooden club, heavier and often studded or banded with iron. Civilian-legal in many settings; effective against most unarmored opponents.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 3 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 10 gp
Traits: Brutal.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Mace (Smooth).** A metal-headed bludgeon on a short wooden or iron haft, twenty-four to thirty inches overall. Simple, cheap, and effective against unarmored or lightly-armored opponents.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 35 gp
Traits: Brutal.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Flanged Mace.** A mace with vertical flanges radiating from a central shaft, designed to concentrate impact force and dent or split armor plate. The anti-armor mace of choice.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 5.5 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 65 gp
Traits: Brutal, Armor-Crack.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Morning Star.** A spiked mace — a club or mace with prominent steel spikes protruding from the head. The spikes concentrate force into a smaller contact area, improving armor penetration at the cost of hafting reliability.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 50 gp
Traits: Brutal, Armor-Crack (Slight).
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Warhammer.** A war-optimized hammer with a heavy striking face on one side and a sharp beak or spike on the other. The beak is specifically designed to pierce plate armor.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 4 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 60 gp
Traits: Brutal, Armor-Crack, Versatile.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Maul.** A two-handed war-sledgehammer, forty-eight to sixty inches overall, with a heavy iron head. Slower than a greatsword but devastating against any armor.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 7 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 80 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Brutal (Moderate), Armor-Crack.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Great Maul.** A massive two-handed warhammer, sixty to seventy-two inches, with a head weighing ten pounds or more. Cannot be wielded by small or weak characters; requires both great strength and great room to swing.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Devastating
Weight: 11 lbs · Bulk: 4
Cost: 150 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Brutal (Major), Armor-Crack. Severe penalty in tight quarters.
}}

## Flails

{{CoreHanging
**One-Handed Flail.** A hafted weapon with a short chain or strap connecting the handle to one or more weighted striking heads. The flexibility defeats shields — the head can curve around the shield's edge — but also makes precise strikes difficult.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 3.5 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 50 gp
Traits: Brutal, Entangling. Ignores shield Parry bonus.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Two-Handed Flail.** A longer flail with a two-handed haft and often multiple heads. Historically derived from peasant threshing flails repurposed for war — the weapon of the Hussite Wars and countless later rebellions.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 6 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 95 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Brutal, Entangling. Ignores shield Parry bonus. Moderate penalty in tight quarters.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Ball-and-Chain Flail.** A single large spiked iron ball on a long chain. Romanticized in fantasy, impractical in reality — the weapon requires enormous strength to wield without injuring its user, and the chain offers no leverage for a skilled strike.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 8 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 120 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Brutal (Moderate), Entangling. Armor-Crack. On a natural failure, the wielder suffers Slight self-damage from the swing.
}}

## Specialty & Exotic Weapons

{{CoreBody
The weapons below do not fit cleanly into the major families. Some are culturally specific; some are mechanical oddities that never caught on outside a single region; some are hybrids of multiple weapon types. All of them are encountered in fiction and in actual history, and most are more practical than they look.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Katana.** A curved single-edged two-handed sword, twenty-four to thirty inches of blade with a thirty-six-inch total length. A fine-grade infantry sword developed in the Japanese tradition; requires master-level smithing to produce well.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy (Crushing two-handed)
Weight: 3 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 200 gp (standard) / 600 gp (masterwork; which is the only kind the tradition respects)
Traits: Versatile, Balanced, Quick.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Wakizashi.** A shorter companion to the katana, worn paired. Eighteen to twenty-four inches of blade.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 1.5 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 90 gp
Traits: Finesse, Quick, Concealable. Slight bonus when paired with a katana (off-hand parry).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Naginata.** A pole-mounted curved blade, similar to a Western glaive but with specific Japanese construction traditions. Technically a polearm (see Chapter 6) but often grouped with two-handed swords for its handling.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 180 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Slight), Balanced.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Kriss.** A wavy-bladed short sword or long dagger from the Malay archipelago. The waves are believed in some traditions to carry ancestral spirits; in mechanical terms, they produce the same prolonged-wound effect as the flamberge.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 1.5 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 80 gp
Traits: Finesse, Quick. Wounds bleed longer (Slight Endurance penalty per wound for one scene).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Urumi.** A flexible, whip-like steel sword with a long sharpened blade-ribbon, often carried coiled at the waist. Indian in origin; one of the few weapons in history whose users probably injured themselves as often as their opponents.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 1.5 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 200 gp
Traits: Finesse, Entangling, Reach (Moderate). Requires specialized training; characters without it suffer a Major penalty. Can strike opponents behind cover (bends around corners).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Shotel.** An Ethiopian curved sword with an extreme sickle-like curve, designed specifically to reach behind an opponent's shield. Strange in the hand and strange in the strike.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 3 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 130 gp
Traits: Hooking. Partially ignores shield Parry bonus (Slight bypass).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Jian.** A straight, double-edged Chinese sword with a tapering point, thirty to thirty-four inches. An elegant civilian and scholar's sword as much as a battlefield weapon.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 2.5 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 150 gp
Traits: Finesse, Balanced, Quick.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Dao.** A curved, single-edged Chinese saber, thirty-two to thirty-eight inches. The military counterpart to the jian.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 3 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 110 gp
Traits: Balanced, Brutal.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Boomerang (Non-Returning).** Heavy throwing-stick used by some steppe and tribal cultures. The common "returning" variety is a hunting and sport implement; the war variety is simply a well-balanced throwing club.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 25 gp
Traits: Thrown Range: 40 feet. Can be wielded in melee as a club without penalty.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Punch Dagger (Katar).** A triangular or diamond-bladed dagger with a horizontal grip between two parallel guards, held in a closed fist. Indian in origin. The punch-grip delivers the blade with the full force of a hammer blow.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 1.2 lbs · Bulk: 0
Cost: 60 gp
Traits: Finesse, Quick, Armor-Crack (Slight).
}}

## Historical Curiosities

{{note
##### The Tsar's Gauntlet

A theoretical design recorded in Russian metalworking manuals: an armored gauntlet with a spring-loaded stiletto concealed inside the forearm casing. A small trigger in the palm released the blade, allowing a seemingly-unarmed wielder to deliver a close-range strike. No confirmed working examples are known to have been produced.

**Damage:** Glancing (if trigger succeeds) · **Weight:** +2 lbs on the gauntlet · **Cost:** 300 gp

**Traits:** A hidden strike; no Stealth penalty to reveal. On the first round of a surprise attack, grants a Moderate bonus to Strike. Triggers jam on a failed Agility check at Routine difficulty.
}}

{{note
##### The Hook-Sword (Shuang Gou)

A Chinese paired weapon: two curved swords with hooks at the point and crescent guards at the hilt. Can be interlocked at the hooks to extend reach or catch an opponent's weapon.

**Damage:** Heavy · **Weight:** 3 lbs each · **Cost:** 250 gp the pair

**Traits:** Finesse, Hooking, Entangling. When linked: Reach (Moderate) and Moderate bonus to Disarm. Users without specific training suffer a Major penalty.
}}

\page

{{note
##### The Kukri

A curved, forward-angled short blade used as both tool and weapon throughout the Himalayan region. The forward-angled geometry concentrates the force of the cut toward the blade's belly, producing chops harder than a similar-mass straight blade can deliver.

**Damage:** Solid (+Slight vs. unarmored) · **Weight:** 1.5 lbs · **Bulk:** 0 · **Cost:** 35 gp

**Traits:** Quick, Brutal. Can be thrown (Range 15 feet). Functions as a utility tool without penalty (skinning, chopping wood).
}}

{{note
##### The Macuahuitl

A Mesoamerican sword-club of wood inlaid with obsidian edges. A pre-conquest weapon capable of decapitating a horse in a single well-struck blow, at the cost of losing its obsidian edges after a few engagements.

**Damage:** Heavy (Crushing on first strike of combat) · **Weight:** 4 lbs · **Bulk:** 2 · **Cost:** 60 gp

**Traits:** First strike of each combat ignores one Protection tier (obsidian edge). Each further strike risks breaking an edge — on any natural failure, Slight Durability penalty until re-edged. Re-edging requires obsidian flakes and a day of work.
}}

{{note
##### The Atgeir (Saga Polearm-Sword)

A Norse hybrid weapon of uncertain exact form, referenced in the sagas as a weapon halfway between a spear and a sword. Modern interpretations suggest a long-bladed spear useful for both thrusting and cutting.

**Damage:** Heavy · **Weight:** 5 lbs · **Bulk:** 3 · **Cost:** 120 gp

**Traits:** Two-Handed, Reach (Slight), Versatile between thrust and cut. A skilled wielder can switch grips to use it either as a thrusting spear or a cutting sword.
}}

\column

## Fantasy Variants

{{note
##### Dwarven Axe

A battle axe or great axe forged in the dwarven tradition gains a Moderate Durability bonus, a Slight damage bonus, and the characteristic ringing tone when struck against armor. Dwarven axes come in standard and two-handed sizes; the signature "singer's edge" is a mirror-polished bevel that can be used to test the harmony of a blade by tone.

Cost: +Major bonus to base axe price (×3).
}}

{{note
##### Elven Moon-Silver Blade

A sword or dagger woven from moon-silver rather than steel. Retains all silver's supernatural properties (bypassing lycanthrope and certain fae resistances) while carrying a Slight damage bonus and Major weight reduction. Glows faintly in moonlight.

Cost: +Severe bonus to base weapon price (×10).
}}

{{note
##### Dragon-Bone Weapon

A weapon carved from dragon-bone rather than forged from metal. Carries a Slight damage bonus, a Moderate weight reduction, and the elemental affinity of its source — fire-drake weapons deal additional Slight fire damage, frost-drake cold, and so on.

Cost: +Severe bonus to base weapon price.

A dragon whose kin's bone is wielded openly in its territory will not take kindly to the wielder. Some settings consider dragon-bone weapons cursed outright.
}}

{{note
##### Rune-Bound Weapons

Any weapon of sufficient quality can have a single rune bound into it, granting a specific effect per Chapter 19's rules. A rune-bound weapon of otherwise standard construction becomes the beginning of a legendary item — but only the beginning. Most named legendary blades in myth are rune-bound masterworks of specific exotic materials.

Cost for standard rune-binding: +Major bonus (×3) on top of the base weapon. See Chapter 19 for the full process and effects.
}}

\page

## Material Variations

{{CoreBody
Any weapon in this chapter can be made of any reasonable material from Chapter 2. The modifiers stack as described there — a bronze longsword is made to last, a dark-iron longsword is made to be inherited, a sky-glass longsword is made to ruin the day of one very unlucky opponent before breaking on the second.
}}

{{CoreBody
A few weapons are strongly associated with specific materials by tradition. A falchion in cold iron is common; a falchion in moon-silver is nonsensical. A katana is only ever pattern-welded or better. A kriss is traditionally hammered from layered steel and wrought-iron to produce its characteristic surface.
}}

{{CoreBody
The table below highlights the most common material choices for the major weapon families, showing the modifier adjustments against steel baseline.
}}

{{TableTitle
Common Material Choices by Weapon Family
}}

| Family | Typical Material | Common Variants | Fantasy Variants |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| Daggers | Steel | Silver-inlaid, Cold Iron | Moon-Silver, Star-Silver |
| Short blades | Steel | Pattern-welded | Star-Silver, Moon-Silver |
| One-handed swords | Steel | Pattern-welded | All fantasy materials |
| Two-handed swords | Steel | Pattern-welded (rare) | Dragon-Bone, Dark-Iron |
| Axes | Iron head + wood haft | Steel head | Dwarven forgework |
| Maces / hammers | Iron head + wood haft | Dark-Iron (rare) | Dragon-Bone head |
| Flails | Iron head + chain | — | — |
| Rapier / fine blades | Pattern-welded | Toledo-grade steel | Moon-Silver |

## Maintenance and Wear

{{CoreBody
The general maintenance rules of Chapter 3 apply. Specifically for hand weapons:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Edge Maintenance.** Edged weapons dull with use. After seven days of hard combat use without attention, a blade's damage drops by a Slight tier; after fourteen days, by Moderate. Sharpening is a Routine check and takes an hour with a whetstone.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Haft Care.** Wooden hafts crack in the cold, swell in the damp, and shrink in the dry. A yearly re-hafting is normal for heavy bludgeons and axes. Field replacement is possible with appropriate wood and a half-day's work.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Hilt Wrapping.** Leather or cord wrapping on sword hilts wears through every few years of active use. Replacement is trivial — any leatherworker can re-wrap a hilt in an afternoon for 3 gp.
}}

\column

}}
