\page

# Chapter 6: Polearms & Long Weapons

{{Epigraph
*"An infantryman with a pike is not a soldier. A hundred infantrymen with pikes is a fortress."*
}}
{{EpigraphAuthor
— Captain Adelaise of the Brigandine Watch
}}

## Why Polearms

{{CoreBody
For most of history, the weapon that won most battles was not the sword. It was the long-hafted pole-weapon — the spear, the pike, the halberd, the bill — wielded by a disciplined rank of foot soldiers who could keep cavalry at arm's length and reach over their shields to punish anyone who closed. A knight with a greatsword is a story; a century of pikemen is history.
}}

{{CoreBody
This chapter covers the weapons that earn their keep by reach, mass, and formation. A sword is a personal weapon. A polearm is a weapon that presumes company.
}}

## How Polearms Work

{{CoreBody
Four special mechanics apply to most of the weapons in this chapter. Each is referenced in individual entries by trait name; the rules live here so the entries stay short.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Reach.** A polearm strikes from further than a standard melee weapon. In a one-on-one fight, a weapon with Reach takes the first strike of the first round against any weapon without Reach. In any fight in a zone where the polearm user has more room than the opponent's weapon requires, the Reach bonus continues throughout the engagement. In close quarters — specifically, when the opponent is within grappling distance — Reach inverts into a Moderate penalty. A pike wielder who lets a knife-fighter inside their blade has lost.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Set for Charge (Bracing).** A polearm user can plant the butt of the weapon against the ground or their foot and brace against an incoming charge. This requires one action to prepare. A braced polearm deals Crushing damage (or the weapon's next higher tier, whichever is greater) against any enemy who moves into melee range during the round after bracing. Bracing is lost if the wielder moves, changes facing, or is knocked down.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Formation Dependency.** Some polearms — particularly pikes — rely on being wielded by a rank of soldiers in coordinated formation. A pikeman alone is a man with a very awkward stick. A hundred pikemen in formation are a wall of iron. Formation bonuses are covered in Chapter 8; individual polearm entries note which weapons benefit.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Two-Handed Standard.** Unless otherwise noted, all polearms in this chapter require two hands. They cannot be wielded with a shield. A pike used one-handed is effectively a very heavy walking stick.
}}

\column

## Spears

{{CoreBody
The spear is the oldest designed weapon. Every culture that ever made weapons made spears. Spears remain useful long after the pike has been retired to ceremony, because a four-foot spear is a thrusting weapon one can actually maneuver in a room.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Short Spear (Boar Spear, Partisan-Spear).** A four-to-six-foot hafted blade, light enough to use with a shield and short enough to thrust in close quarters. The soldier's spear of antiquity through the middle ages.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy (thrust)
Weight: 3 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 25 gp
Traits: Versatile (one-hand with shield; two-hand for bonus), Reach (Slight).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**War Spear.** A longer, heavier infantry spear, six to eight feet, thrust from behind a shield wall or wielded two-handed in open combat. The standard arm of most organized armies from antiquity through the early medieval period.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 4 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 35 gp
Traits: Two-Handed (or one-handed with Slight damage penalty), Reach (Moderate), Set for Charge.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Long Spear.** An eight-to-ten-foot thrusting weapon, used by infantry tightly packed in formation. The intermediate step between the war spear and the pike.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 4
Cost: 50 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Major), Set for Charge, Formation Dependent. Major penalty in tight quarters.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Pike.** A fourteen-to-eighteen-foot spear with a small, reinforced head. Designed to be wielded by the front two or three ranks of a large formation simultaneously, creating a wall of points that cavalry cannot penetrate and infantry must die to cross.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing (on a braced charge); Heavy otherwise
Weight: 9 lbs · Bulk: 5
Cost: 60 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Severe), Set for Charge (Major), Formation Dependent (strongly). Unusable in tight quarters; Severe penalty in any space narrower than six feet. Cannot be used from horseback.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Hunting Spear.** A lighter, shorter spear intended primarily for hunting large game — boar, deer, the occasional wolf. A crossbar at the base of the head prevents a wounded animal from driving up the shaft and reaching the hunter.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 3 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 18 gp
Traits: Versatile, Set for Charge (against unarmored foe). The crossbar grants a Slight bonus against any target that would be driven forward by pain.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Javelin.** A throwing spear, shorter and lighter than the war spear. Carried in bundles, thrown in volleys, and rarely recovered. A staple of light-infantry and skirmisher equipment across most of the ancient world.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid (thrown); Glancing (melee)
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 10 gp each
Traits: Thrown Range: 40 feet. Poor melee weapon (Slight penalty).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Pilum.** The Roman heavy javelin: a long-stemmed throwing spear with a weighted head and an intentionally weak neck, designed so the head would bend on impact and render the weapon unusable to the enemy. Also designed so that if it struck a shield, it could not be easily removed, forcing the defender to abandon the shield.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy (thrown); unusable in melee after a single throw
Weight: 4 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 25 gp
Traits: Thrown Range: 30 feet. Any shield struck by a pilum becomes encumbered — wielder takes a Moderate penalty until the pilum is cut away (one action).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Angon.** A Frankish javelin similar to the pilum, with a barbed head designed to lodge in a shield and a long iron shaft that could not be easily chopped through.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy (thrown)
Weight: 3.5 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 20 gp
Traits: Thrown Range: 25 feet. Any shield struck by an angon suffers the same encumbrance effect as a pilum, but the angon cannot be cut away without destroying the shield.
}}

\column

## Hewing Polearms

{{CoreBody
Where spears thrust, the hewing polearms cut. A halberd or bardiche combines the reach of a spear with the chopping mass of an axe — an argument that persuades most arguments to shut up.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Halberd.** A pole-mounted combination weapon: a forward-pointing spear-spike for thrusting, a heavy axe-blade for hewing, and a rear hook for catching armor or dismounting riders. The signature weapon of professional soldiers from the 14th through 17th centuries, and the honor-guard weapon for much longer.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 7 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 120 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Moderate), Set for Charge, Hooking, Armor-Crack.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Bardiche.** A very long, curved axe-blade mounted on a five-to-six-foot haft. Lighter than a halberd, more forgiving to wield, and specifically excellent at cleaving through shields and light armor. Popular with Eastern European infantry and Russian streltsy.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 6 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 95 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Slight), Brutal. Can be rested on a forked stand for use as a firearm rest.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Voulge.** A heavy chopping blade mounted on a long pole, similar to the bardiche but shorter-bladed and typically without back-hooks. A cruder weapon than the halberd; easier to produce and quite effective against unarmored targets.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 6.5 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 80 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Slight), Brutal.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Glaive.** A single-edged, curved blade mounted on a six-foot shaft. A cut-and-thrust weapon with better reach than the voulge and better precision than the bardiche. Versatile; favored by guardsmen.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 110 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Moderate), Balanced.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Guisarme.** A glaive with a prominent back-hook, designed to pull riders from saddles and catch at the back of the knee. A mechanical variant that bridges glaive and halberd.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 5.5 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 125 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Moderate), Hooking (Moderate). Moderate bonus to Trip and Dismount attempts.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Bill / Brown Bill.** An English agricultural billhook — a curved, hooked blade on a pole — scaled up to military size. The standard weapon of English infantry through most of the Wars of the Roses and beyond.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 6 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 90 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Moderate), Hooking, Brutal.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Fauchard.** A curved, scythe-like blade mounted on a pole, derived from the farmer's sickle. More theatrical than practical; the curved blade limits thrusting and makes precise cuts difficult. Popular with peasant militias who could not afford a proper halberd.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 60 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Slight), Hooking.
}}

## Pick & Beak Polearms

{{CoreBody
When plate armor became common, the polearm family evolved again. The hewing blades could not reliably punch through well-made plate; a new class of weapons was needed. The answer was the pick, the beak, and the spike — narrow, heavy points driven by long leverage, designed specifically to concentrate force into the smallest possible area and drive through steel.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Bec-de-Corbin.** The "crow's beak." A poll-mounted hammer with a long, curved beak on the opposite side and a top-spike above. Designed to deliver a sledge blow, a piercing beak-thrust, or an overhead thrust, depending on how the wielder chose to swing. A late-medieval specialist's weapon against full plate.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 6 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 180 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Moderate), Armor-Crack (Moderate), Brutal.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Lucerne Hammer.** A Swiss variant of the bec-de-corbin, with a three-or-four-pronged hammer head (for improved grip on armor) and a long, needle-sharp top-spike. Developed for mountain warfare and rapidly adopted elsewhere.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 6.5 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 200 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Moderate), Armor-Crack (Moderate), Hooking (Slight).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**War Pick.** A smaller, one-handed version of the pick-weapons, essentially a small hammer with a sharp beak. Used from horseback and on foot, it is a polearm only in the loosest sense but belongs with this family by function.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 3 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 70 gp
Traits: One-Handed, Armor-Crack.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Horseman's Pick.** A longer-hafted war pick, three to four feet overall, designed for mounted use. Splits the difference between a pick and a polearm.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 4 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 95 gp
Traits: One-Handed, Armor-Crack, Reach (Slight from horseback only).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Poleaxe.** A short, balanced polearm, about five feet overall, with a hammer and spike head. Distinct from the halberd in its weight and proportions: the poleaxe is built for individual knightly combat, not for infantry ranks. The tournament weapon of the late medieval nobility.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 220 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Slight), Armor-Crack, Balanced. Favored weapon for armored foot combat at tournaments (Chapter 18).
}}

\page

## Specialty Polearms

{{CoreHanging
**Partisan.** A broad-bladed spear with two symmetrical side-projections (lugs) at the base of the main blade. The lugs catch and pin opposing weapons during the thrust, and prevent the partisan from driving too deep into a target to withdraw cleanly.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 100 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Moderate), Set for Charge, Entangling.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Ranseur.** A spear with flared side-lugs shaped into hooks, able to grab and wrench an opponent's weapon. Similar to the partisan but more aggressive in its side-projections.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 5.5 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 115 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Moderate), Entangling, Hooking (Slight). Moderate bonus to Disarm.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Spetum.** A short-hafted partisan, about five feet overall, usable in somewhat tighter quarters than a standard partisan or ranseur. Popular among guards patrolling narrow streets and indoor passages.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 4.5 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 85 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Slight), Set for Charge, Entangling. No tight-quarters penalty at Slight Reach.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Man-Catcher.** A U-shaped or ring-shaped head on a long pole, spring-loaded to close around the target's neck, waist, or limb. Used to capture rather than kill — a weapon for sheriffs, tournament marshals, and slavers.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Glancing (contact only)
Weight: 8 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 60 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Moderate). Attempts to Grapple rather than strike; on a successful hit, the target is held in place with a Strength check at Demanding difficulty to escape. Wielder must remain in contact to maintain the grapple.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Awl Pike.** A narrow, needle-pointed thrusting spear, essentially a stiletto on a twelve-foot pole. Designed for one purpose: to drive through the smallest gaps in plate armor when braced against a charge. The specialist's anti-knight pike.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 8 lbs · Bulk: 4
Cost: 85 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Major), Set for Charge (Major), Armor-Crack (Moderate), Formation Dependent. Severely penalized in tight quarters.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Sarissa.** The long Macedonian pike, sixteen to eighteen feet, designed for phalanx warfare. Longer than a European pike; required specialized training to handle effectively. Found in settings with classical-era analogs.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing (braced); Heavy otherwise
Weight: 11 lbs · Bulk: 5
Cost: 80 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Severe), Set for Charge (Major), Formation Dependent (strongly). Unusable outside formation or tight quarters.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Couteau de Brèche (Breach Cleaver).** A French short-polearm with a large, curved cleaving blade specifically designed for storming breaches in castle walls. A specialist weapon for combat in extremely tight quarters — the blade is wide and short, optimized for chopping in a space a halberd cannot swing.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 110 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Brutal. No tight-quarters penalty.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Ji (Chinese Halberd).** A thrusting spear with a single or double side-blade at the base of the point. The Chinese equivalent of the partisan or ji, historically favored by palace guards and elite infantry.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 4.5 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 105 gp
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Moderate), Balanced, Entangling.
}}

\page

## Historical Curiosities

{{note
##### The Flamberge Pike

A pike with a wavy, fire-edged head — the pike equivalent of the flamberge sword. The waves produced prolonged-bleeding wounds when a pikeman managed to withdraw after a thrust, which was rarer than you might think. Mostly ceremonial; parade weapons for elite guards.

**Damage:** as pike, plus Slight per-wound bleed for one scene · **Cost:** +Major cost bonus (×3)

**Traits:** as pike, plus wounds bleed (see flamberge sword, Chapter 5).
}}

{{note
##### The Triple-Head Pike

A late-renaissance experimental design with three parallel pike-heads on a single shaft, intended to triple the chance of finding armor gaps at a charge. In practice, the added weight made it difficult to hold steady, and the heads deflected one another rather than compensating for each other's misses. Rare outside museum collections.

**Damage:** as pike · **Cost:** +Moderate cost bonus · **Weight:** +Moderate

**Traits:** Slight bonus to hit on a braced charge; Moderate Agility penalty at all other times.
}}

{{note
##### The Gun-Spear

A matchlock or wheellock pistol mechanism built into the shaft of a long spear, allowing a single shot to be fired before the spear was committed to melee. Similar concept to the gun-shield (Chapter 4), and with similar problems: the mechanism added weight, the recoil was unbalanced, and the mechanism was vulnerable to the blow that the spear was about to deliver.

**Damage:** as short spear (melee), Solid (fired) · **Cost:** 350 gp · **Weight:** +3 lbs

**Traits:** as short spear, plus single shot (Solid damage, 30 ft range, 3 actions to reload).
}}

\column

## Fantasy Variants

{{note
##### Dwarven Halberd

A halberd forged by dwarven smiths gains a Moderate Durability bonus and a Slight damage bonus, with the characteristic ringing tone common to all dwarven work. Dwarven halberds are typically inherited through families and never sold; the fact that one is available for purchase tells a story.

Cost: +Major cost bonus over standard halberd.
}}

{{note
##### Dragon-Bone Pike

A pike carved from dragon-bone rather than forged from steel. Carries the elemental affinity of the source dragon (see Chapter 2); a fire-drake pike deals an additional Slight fire damage, frost-drake cold, etc. Major weight reduction makes it usable by smaller characters who could not normally wield a pike.

Cost: +Severe cost bonus.
}}

{{note
##### Living-Wood Stave

A two-handed fighting staff made of living wood (see Chapter 2). Not a true weapon so much as a druid's tool, but a skilled wielder can use it as a quarterstaff with the Versatile trait and a Slight damage bonus. Self-repairs overnight when rested against the earth; cannot be lit on fire.

Cost: +Major cost bonus over a standard quarterstaff.
}}

\page

## The Quarterstaff

{{CoreBody
The quarterstaff deserves its own brief entry. Technically a polearm by virtue of its length, it is also the cheapest, most universally available weapon in this book.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Quarterstaff.** A straight wooden staff of hardwood, five to seven feet long, usually shod with iron ferrules at the ends. A walking staff, a traveler's defense, a monastic training weapon, and a surprisingly effective combat implement in skilled hands.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 4 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 3 gp (or free, from any tree)
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Slight), Balanced. Does not count as a weapon for many legal purposes — a character carrying a quarterstaff through a city may not be challenged where one with a sword would.
}}

## Using Polearms in Specific Situations

{{CoreHanging
**In Tight Quarters.** A polearm wielder in a corridor narrower than their weapon's practical swing suffers the penalties noted per weapon. A pike, a sarissa, or an awl pike is effectively unusable indoors. A halberd is hampered but functional. A partisan or spetum handles well in most indoor spaces.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**From Horseback.** Weapons without a horseback-specific design cannot be effectively couched from a saddle. A lance (treated separately in Chapter 9) is the mounted equivalent of the spear family. Polearms can be strapped to the saddle and wielded dismounted; a few (glaive, guisarme) can be used two-handed from a stationary mount with a Moderate penalty.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Against Cavalry.** Any polearm with Set for Charge deals its bracing damage against a horseman who charges into range. Against a charging heavy cavalry formation, this is often the difference between a rout and a hold. See Chapter 8 for formation-level rules.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**In Water.** Polearms lose most of their advantage underwater or in deep shallow water. Thrusting weapons work approximately normally; cutting weapons suffer a Major damage penalty due to resistance. See Chapter 8 for environmental modifiers.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**In Dense Foliage.** Long polearms (Reach Major or Severe) are effectively unusable in heavy woodland or thick undergrowth. The weapon's length becomes an obstacle rather than an advantage. Short spears and poleaxes handle well enough.
}}

\column

## Maintenance and Wear

{{CoreBody
The standard rules of Chapter 3 apply. Specific notes for polearms:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Haft Care.** Wooden polearm hafts are the single most common failure point. A polearm wielder should budget one haft replacement per year of active use, or more in damp climates. Field replacement is possible with appropriate timber and half a day's work; the head is unbolted from the old haft and mounted on the new.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Head Security.** A polearm head that loosens during combat is a disaster. Check and tighten the mounting before any significant engagement (a Routine check; automatic success with a few minutes of care).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Transportation.** Long polearms are the most awkward weapon in the book to travel with. A pike will not fit in a standard farmhouse doorway; a sarissa cannot be carried through any normal town gate. Campaigns involving significant polearm use should plan for this — either by accepting the inconvenience, by disassembling heads from hafts, or by traveling with a wagon.
}}

# Chapter 7: Ranged Weapons

{{Epigraph
*"The best sword in the world is worth less than the worst bow in the hands of a patient man on a hilltop."*
}}
{{EpigraphAuthor
— Sergeant Brand of the Longshire Archers
}}

## The Logic of Range

{{CoreBody
A ranged weapon is a melee weapon that doesn't require you to be within arm's length of your problem. This sounds like a minor distinction until you consider what a well-aimed arrow does to a cavalry charge at two hundred yards — which is to say, it ends it, sometimes before the cavalry knows an archer is there.
}}

{{CoreBody
This chapter covers bows, crossbows, slings, and thrown weapons. Early firearms (hand cannons, matchlocks, wheellocks) are treated separately in Chapter 13, because their mechanics differ enough — and their history is distinct enough — to warrant their own chapter.
}}

## Range Bands and Rates of Fire

{{CoreBody
Every ranged weapon has three statistics beyond the baseline item block: effective range, rate of fire, and ammunition type.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Effective Range.** The distance at which a trained user can reliably hit a man-sized target. Most weapons can reach further than their effective range — that's the *maximum* range — but accuracy drops sharply past the effective number. The book's stats list effective range only; the GM should treat any shot beyond 2× effective as Near-Impossible.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Rate of Fire.** How often the weapon can be fired in the book's standard combat round. "1/round" means once per round; "1/2 rounds" means every second round; and so on. Rate of fire accounts for reloading, re-aiming, and resuming firing posture.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Ammunition Type.** What the weapon fires. Arrows for bows, bolts for crossbows, stones or bullets for slings, and so on. Ammunition is tracked separately from the weapon. A bow without arrows is firewood.
}}

## Range Band Tiers

{{CoreBody
Rather than quote effective range in feet for every weapon, entries use range bands that translate to specific distances. Bands scale roughly with the weapon's draw power or projectile mass.
}}

{{TableTitle
Range Bands
}}

| Band | Effective Range | Max Range |
|:--|:-:|:-:|
| Close | 30 ft | 60 ft |
| Short | 60 ft | 150 ft |
| Medium | 150 ft | 300 ft |
| Long | 300 ft | 600 ft |
| Extreme | 600 ft | 1,200 ft |

\column

## Bows

{{CoreHanging
**Hunting Bow (Self-Bow).** A simple wooden longbow, typically three to five feet tall, drawn to the cheek or ear. The everyday bow of hunters, foresters, and peasant militias.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 30 gp
Range: Medium · Rate of Fire: 1/round · Ammo: Arrows
Traits: Two-Handed. Requires seasoned yew, ash, or similar wood.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Shortbow.** A compact bow, two to three feet tall, built for rapid use from horseback or in tight cover. Easier to handle than a longbow at the cost of power and range.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 1.5 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 35 gp
Range: Short · Rate of Fire: 2/round · Ammo: Arrows
Traits: Two-Handed. Usable from horseback. Slight bonus to rate of fire for trained archers.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Longbow.** A tall, heavy-draw bow, five to six feet or more, wielded by trained specialists. The English longbow at Agincourt and Crécy was roughly this weapon.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 3 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 80 gp
Range: Long · Rate of Fire: 1/round · Ammo: Arrows (heavy)
Traits: Two-Handed, Armor-Crack (Slight with bodkin arrows). Requires a trained archer — untrained wielders suffer a Major damage penalty and a band's loss of range due to inability to draw properly.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Composite Bow.** A layered bow built from wood, horn, and sinew, producing greater draw weight than a single-material bow of the same size. The standard bow of steppe cavalry and many eastern armies.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 150 gp
Range: Long · Rate of Fire: 1/round · Ammo: Arrows
Traits: Two-Handed. Usable from horseback (no penalty for a trained rider).
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Recurve Bow.** A variant of the composite bow with tips curved away from the archer at rest; the design stores more energy in a shorter stave. Common in cavalry and skirmisher units across steppe and Mediterranean cultures.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 180 gp
Range: Long · Rate of Fire: 1/round · Ammo: Arrows
Traits: Two-Handed. Usable from horseback. Slight range bonus over standard composite.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Horn Bow (Heavy Composite).** An exotic heavy composite bow made of water-buffalo horn, sinew, and strategic wood cores. Used in Ottoman and Mongol warfare. Expensive, slow to build (six months to a year curing), and devastatingly effective.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy (+Slight)
Weight: 2.5 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 400 gp
Range: Extreme · Rate of Fire: 1/round · Ammo: Arrows
Traits: Two-Handed. Usable from horseback. Severe Durability penalty in damp climates (the layered horn delaminates).
}}

## Crossbows

{{CoreBody
A crossbow is a mechanical bow: a short bow-prod mounted crosswise on a shoulder stock, with a trigger and a mechanical draw. Crossbows trade rate of fire for accuracy, ease of training, and armor-piercing power.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Hand Crossbow.** A small, one-handed crossbow, drawn by pulling the string back by hand or with a simple lever. The concealable sidearm of the assassin, the urban spy, and the desperate duelist.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 3 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 150 gp
Range: Short · Rate of Fire: 1/round · Ammo: Bolts
Traits: One-Handed. Concealable under heavy cloak.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Light Crossbow.** A two-handed, foot-spanned crossbow with a wooden or composite prod. The common infantry crossbow of early medieval warfare.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 80 gp
Range: Medium · Rate of Fire: 1/round · Ammo: Bolts
Traits: Two-Handed.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Heavy Crossbow.** A steel-prod crossbow, drawn with a cranequin (geared winch) or goat's-foot lever. Greater range and penetration than the light crossbow at a much slower rate of fire.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 8 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 200 gp
Range: Long · Rate of Fire: 1/2 rounds · Ammo: Heavy Bolts
Traits: Two-Handed, Armor-Crack. Drawing requires a cranequin (free with weapon) and takes a full round.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Arbalest.** A massive military crossbow with a steel prod and mandatory mechanical spanner. Used from fortifications and in siege work more often than in open combat. Essentially a man-portable ballista.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Devastating
Weight: 15 lbs · Bulk: 4
Cost: 350 gp
Range: Long · Rate of Fire: 1/3 rounds · Ammo: War Bolts
Traits: Two-Handed, Armor-Crack (Moderate). Drawing requires a cranequin and takes two full rounds. Can only be fired from a stationary position.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Repeating Crossbow.** A magazine-fed crossbow with a sliding lever mechanism. Popular in Chinese military usage; encountered rarely in other traditions. Fires rapidly at short range but with reduced damage per shot.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 6 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 300 gp
Range: Close · Rate of Fire: 3/round (up to 10 bolts before reload) · Ammo: Short Bolts
Traits: Two-Handed. Magazine reload: 1 action per 10-bolt magazine.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Wall-Crossbow (Mounted).** A crossbow too heavy for a single person to fire offhand, mounted on a swivel or wall-ring. A fortification weapon.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Devastating
Weight: 30 lbs · Bulk: 5 (mounted)
Cost: 600 gp (weapon + mount)
Range: Extreme · Rate of Fire: 1/3 rounds · Ammo: Wall Bolts
Traits: Stationary; mount required. Two-person crew ideal. Reload: 2 rounds with both hands free.
}}

\page

## Thrown Weapons and Slings

{{CoreHanging
**Sling.** A leather or cord pouch with two strings, used to launch stones or lead bullets at high velocity. Quiet, light, and — in a trained hand — surprisingly deadly. The shepherd's weapon; also the biblical weapon of choice against giants.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 0.5 lbs · Bulk: 0
Cost: 3 gp (plus ammunition)
Range: Medium · Rate of Fire: 1/round · Ammo: Stones or lead bullets
Traits: One-Handed. Silent on release. Slight bonus to Stealth when carried openly (reads as rope or strap).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Staff Sling.** A sling attached to a three-foot staff, providing a longer lever arm for greater throwing power at the cost of concealment and rate of fire.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 2.5 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 10 gp
Range: Long · Rate of Fire: 1/2 rounds · Ammo: Stones or bullets
Traits: Two-Handed. Can hurl fire-pots or alchemical flasks (see Chapter 16).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Throwing Knife.** A balanced blade sized for throwing. Not a particularly effective weapon compared to an arrow, but easily concealed and silent. The assassin's tool.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Glancing
Weight: 0.7 lbs · Bulk: 0
Cost: 12 gp each
Range: Close · Rate of Fire: 2/round · Ammo: Self (recoverable)
Traits: Concealable. Quick.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Throwing Axe.** A hand axe specifically balanced for throwing, heavier and shorter-hafted than a working axe. Favored by Frankish and Norse warriors.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 25 gp
Range: Close · Rate of Fire: 1/round · Ammo: Self (recoverable)
Traits: Brutal. Usable in melee as a hand axe.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Dart (Plumbata).** A small weighted throwing dart, traditionally carried by Roman legionaries in bundles of five, tucked behind the shield. Shorter range than a javelin but easier to carry in quantity.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Glancing
Weight: 0.5 lbs · Bulk: 0
Cost: 5 gp each
Range: Short · Rate of Fire: 2/round · Ammo: Self
Traits: Concealable.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Dzherid.** A specialized quiver of short throwing darts, carried on the belt, drawn and thrown in one motion. Steppe cavalry weapon.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 1 lb per dart · Bulk: 0
Cost: 15 gp each (quiver of four: 70 gp)
Range: Short · Rate of Fire: 2/round · Ammo: Self
Traits: Rapid Draw — drawing a dzherid does not count as a separate action. Usable from horseback.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Chakram.** A flat metal throwing ring with a razor outer edge. Indian in origin; spun on the finger before release, producing a flat, nearly-horizontal flight path.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid
Weight: 0.3 lbs · Bulk: 0
Cost: 30 gp each
Range: Close · Rate of Fire: 2/round · Ammo: Self
Traits: Finesse, Concealable. Can be worn openly as a bracelet or pendant.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Bola.** Two or three weighted balls connected by cords, thrown to entangle the legs of running prey. Not a combat weapon in origin, but used against cavalry by some cultures.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Glancing
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 15 gp
Range: Close · Rate of Fire: 1/round · Ammo: Self
Traits: On a successful strike against a leg or mount, target must make a Challenging Agility check or fall. Entangling.
}}

## Ammunition Variants

{{CoreBody
Arrows, bolts, and sling bullets come in specialized varieties for specific situations. All variants cost more than standard ammunition; the price multiplier is noted in each entry.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Standard Arrow/Bolt.** The default. Does normal damage. Costs 1 gp per arrow, 2 gp per bolt.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Bodkin Arrow/Bolt.** A narrow, four-edged steel head designed to pierce plate. Slight damage reduction, Moderate Armor-Crack bonus. Cost: +Moderate.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Broadhead (Hunting Head).** A wide, leaf-shaped cutting head designed to inflict devastating wounds on unarmored prey. Slight damage bonus against unarmored; Slight damage penalty against armored. Cost: +Slight.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Crescent/Chisel Head.** A U-shaped or chisel-tipped arrowhead designed to cut rigging, bowstrings, and tendons. Normal damage but a Moderate bonus to Called Shots against limbs and to cutting ropes or cords. Cost: +Moderate.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Blunt (Target) Arrow/Bolt.** Padded or blunted for training or non-lethal use. Deals no lethal damage but can cause Stun (Challenging Endurance check or lose next action). Cost: −Slight (cheaper than standard).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Whistling Arrow.** Hollow, aerodynamically slotted head that produces a high-pitched shriek in flight. Standard damage. On use as a volley (3+ arrows in the air at once toward the same target group), anyone in the group must make a Willpower check at Challenging difficulty or suffer Slight penalty to all rolls for one round (psychological disruption). Cost: +Moderate.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Incendiary Arrow.** An arrow wrapped in pitch-soaked cloth, set alight before firing. Deals normal damage plus a chance to ignite flammable materials at the point of impact. Accuracy penalty: Slight (the flame disrupts flight). Cost: +Slight. Not usable in rain or heavy wind.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Signal Arrow.** A hollow head filled with colored smoke or a small firework, used for battlefield signaling. No combat damage. Cost: 5 gp each.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Lead Sling Bullet.** A cast lead bullet, heavier than a stone and better aerodynamically. Moderate damage bonus over stones. Cost: 0.5 gp each (or free, cast from scrap).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Ceramic Sling Bullet (Fire-Pot).** A small ceramic sphere filled with alchemical fire or oil, thrown by a staff sling. Deals incendiary effect on impact; see Chapter 16 for the specific alchemical contents.
}}

\column

## Historical Curiosities

{{note
##### The Slur (Bullet-Bow)

A specialized crossbow with a split string and a small pouch rather than a bolt-groove, designed to fire lead pellets or stones. Used for hunting small game silently — the pellet exits the weapon with almost no sound — and for non-lethal crowd dispersal in some urban watch units.

**Damage:** Solid · **Range:** Short · **Weight:** 4 lbs · **Cost:** 110 gp

**Traits:** Silent release. Slight Stun bonus (high knockdown). Cannot fire standard bolts.
}}

{{note
##### The Multi-Bolt Organ Crossbow

A theoretical siege defense weapon: five to seven crossbow prods mounted on a common frame with a shared trigger bar. Fires a spread of bolts in a five-foot arc. Historically never proved reliable — the tension was impossible to balance across prods, and the machines typically failed within three firings.

**Damage:** Solid per bolt · **Range:** Short · **Weight:** 40 lbs (fixed mount) · **Cost:** 500 gp

**Traits:** Fires 1d4 bolts on each shot. On a natural failure, the frame cracks and the weapon is destroyed. Reload time: 5 rounds per bolt set.
}}

{{note
##### The Spring-Flinger (Hand Ballista)

A portable torsion-powered launcher, hand-cranked and carried by two people. Hurls fire-pots, stones, or alchemical flasks. A weapon for defending a window or tower rather than an open field.

**Damage:** Crushing (plus alchemical or fire effects per payload) · **Range:** Medium · **Weight:** 35 lbs · **Cost:** 450 gp

**Traits:** Two-person crew. Torsion ropes fail in freezing weather (Moderate Durability penalty and fouling risk).
}}

\page

{{note
##### The Blowpipe

A hollow tube, three to five feet long, through which small darts are launched by breath. Silent, short-ranged, and almost never lethal on its own — but the ideal delivery mechanism for contact poisons.

**Damage:** Glancing (darts themselves) · **Range:** Close · **Weight:** 1 lb · **Cost:** 25 gp

**Traits:** Silent. Darts hold one dose of contact poison each (see Chapter 16). Slight Stealth bonus when carried disguised as a walking-stick.
}}

{{note
##### The Yumi (Asymmetric Bow)

A Japanese longbow in which the grip is positioned below the center, producing an asymmetric shape. Designed for use from horseback and from a kneeling position, where the lower limb would not strike the ground or saddle.

**Damage:** Heavy · **Range:** Long · **Weight:** 2.5 lbs · **Cost:** 200 gp

**Traits:** Two-Handed. No penalty when fired from horseback, from a kneeling position, or from a crouching position. Moderate penalty in standard standing archery (wielder trained in yumi method is less accustomed to the conventional stance).
}}

## Fantasy Variants

{{note
##### Elven Moon-Silver Bow

A bow of moon-silver with silver bowstring, granting all silver properties (bypasses lycanthrope resistance). The arrows fired from this bow gain silver properties for the duration of flight. Range is extended by one band (Long becomes Extreme). Cost: +Severe (×10) over base bow.
}}

{{note
##### Dragon-Bone Crossbow

A hand or light crossbow with a dragon-bone prod rather than wood or steel. Slight damage bonus and Major weight reduction. Carries the elemental affinity of the source dragon — bolts fired from a fire-drake crossbow add Slight fire damage, etc. Cost: +Severe over base crossbow.
}}

{{note
##### Rune-Bound Ammunition

Individual arrows or bolts can be rune-bound with minor effects (see Chapter 19). A rune-bound ignition arrow always ignites on impact even in rain; a rune-bound silver bolt carries silver's properties without requiring a silver warhead; a rune-bound piercing arrow gains a full Armor-Crack bonus. Cost: +Major per arrow for standard runes, +Severe for advanced. Most characters cannot afford rune-bound ammunition by the quiver; a handful of special arrows for critical shots is the normal usage pattern.
}}

\column

## Using Ranged Weapons in Specific Situations

{{CoreHanging
**In Melee.** Firing a ranged weapon while an enemy is within melee range of you imposes a Moderate penalty to the shot and provokes a free strike from that enemy. Some hand crossbows and small bows are the exception; noted in entries.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**From Horseback.** Most bows can be fired from horseback by a trained rider. Crossbows of medium or heavier class cannot. Check individual entries for the explicit note.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**In Weather.** Rain damages bowstrings (Slight damage and range penalty); heavy rain damages crossbow mechanisms (Moderate). Cold makes string and horn brittle (Slight Durability penalty). Wind imposes an accuracy penalty scaling with strength (Slight in light wind; up to Severe in gale).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**In Dense Foliage.** Arrows and bolts are partially deflected or blocked by leaves and branches. Moderate penalty to hit; Slight damage reduction.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Underwater.** Standard arrows and bolts are almost useless underwater — the water resistance collapses the shot within a few feet. Specialty underwater bolts (short, heavy, broad-finned) exist but are exotic. See Chapter 8.
}}

## Maintenance

{{CoreBody
Ranged weapons suffer from their own specific wear.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Bowstrings.** A bow's weakest component. Strings fray with use, stretch with moisture, and snap with cold. Most archers carry at least two spare strings. Replacement is a one-minute task. Cost: 2 gp per string.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Unstringing.** A bow left strung for long periods loses its draw strength permanently. Archers unstring bows when not in use — except in the middle of a battle, obviously.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Crossbow Prod.** Steel prods last decades; wooden prods may crack after a few thousand shots. Wooden-prod replacement is a specialist's job.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Ammunition Recovery.** On a battlefield, approximately half of arrows can be recovered intact after combat. Bolts are harder — they tend to bend or splinter on hard targets. Slings bullets are usually recoverable.
}}

\page
