\page

# Chapter 8: Combat Techniques

{{Epigraph
*"Most soldiers fight one battle their whole lives. The ones who fight many learn to fight in the spaces between the blows — the ground underfoot, the line to your left, the weather on your face."*
}}
{{EpigraphAuthor
— Captain Marsal of the Third Pike
}}

## What This Chapter Covers

{{CoreBody
Combat in a fantasy setting is not a series of duels. It is a mess of bodies, terrain, weather, formation, and momentum. This chapter provides rules for the conditions that modify combat beyond weapon and armor choice: formations, environments, and common tactical situations.
}}

{{CoreBody
The chapter has three sections. The first covers formations — the group-level rules that turn a handful of soldiers into a functioning unit. The second covers environmental modifiers — the penalties and bonuses that terrain, weather, and exotic conditions impose on combat. The third covers specific tactical situations that come up often enough to warrant their own rules: mounted versus unmounted, called shots, fighting wounded, and so on.
}}

{{CoreBody
The book's system is deliberately modular. Any of the rules below can be used or ignored without breaking the rest. A group that wants pure duel-style combat can skip the formation section and lose nothing; a group that loves tactical mass battles can use every rule in this chapter plus any more they care to add.
}}

## Formations

{{CoreBody
Formation rules apply to groups of three or more characters acting together. Most formations require a specific minimum number of participants, a weapon or shield type, and a shared facing. A character in formation gains the listed bonus; breaking formation (moving independently, turning, being knocked down) loses the bonus until re-formed.
}}

### Shield Wall

{{CoreHanging
**Requirement.** Three or more characters with medium-or-larger shields (round, kite, heater, tower) standing adjacent and facing the same direction.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Benefit.** Each character in the wall grants a Moderate Protection bonus to themselves and a Slight Protection bonus to the character immediately on their weapon side (the side their sword arm is on). The bonus applies against ranged attacks as well as melee.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Restriction.** Characters in a shield wall can only move in unison, at half their normal speed. Any character acting independently leaves the wall.
}}

\column

### Pike Phalanx

{{CoreHanging
**Requirement.** Five or more characters with pikes or long spears, standing in at least two ranks, facing the same direction.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Benefit.** The front rank strikes first at any enemy entering their range. The second rank (and any additional ranks behind) can also strike, their pikes projecting past the front rank. Any enemy charging the formation triggers the Set for Charge rule (Chapter 6) for all front-rank pikes simultaneously.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Restriction.** Phalanx movement is strictly limited — six feet per round at most, and only in a straight line. Any change of facing breaks the formation.
}}

### Wedge

{{CoreHanging
**Requirement.** Five or more characters with melee weapons, arranged in a triangle point-forward.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Benefit.** The formation gains Moderate damage bonus against any linear enemy formation it charges (such as a line of unbraced infantry). The point character takes all attacks first; the following characters gain a Slight bonus to their strikes.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Restriction.** Only useful on the charge. Once engaged, the wedge breaks and becomes individual melee.
}}

### Feigned Retreat

{{CoreHanging
**Requirement.** Any small group of mounted or lightly-armored skirmishers, led by a character with a Challenging or better Deception or Leadership check. Enemy must be susceptible to morale effects (ordinary troops, not elite or zealous).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Benefit.** The skirmishers intentionally break contact and retreat in apparent disarray. On a successful check, the enemy unit pursues, breaking their own formation in the process. The skirmishers lead the pursuers into prepared ground — an ambush, a narrow defile, a ready line of heavy infantry. The ambushing force gains a Major bonus on the first round of the re-engagement.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Restriction.** Works once per battle against any given unit. Once an enemy commander has been tricked, the trick does not work again.
}}

\page

### Cantabrian Circle

{{CoreHanging
**Requirement.** Six or more mounted archers or horse-darters, maintaining a rotating circle around the enemy at short-medium range.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Benefit.** The rotating horsemen are considered Moving Targets — ranged attacks against them suffer a Moderate penalty. The horsemen each fire one round per pass. If charged, they Parthian-shot (fire backward at full accuracy) while retreating.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Restriction.** Requires open ground. Cannot be maintained in dense foliage, tight quarters, or on ships.
}}

### Winged Charge

{{CoreHanging
**Requirement.** Three or more heavy cavalry with lances, arranged in a line-abreast charge. Lances must be longer than the enemy's pikes (polish-winged-hussar style) or the enemy must not have pikes.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Benefit.** The lance's extreme length allows the rider to strike before the pikeman's Set for Charge can trigger. The charge deals Crushing damage (or the weapon's next higher tier) and forces a Knockdown check on each target hit.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Restriction.** Requires specialized lances (the "Polish lance" or similar, 18–20 feet). Works only against pike formations; against other enemy types, the cavalry's normal charge applies.
}}

### Skirmish Line

{{CoreHanging
**Requirement.** Four or more characters with ranged weapons, arranged in a loose formation with gaps between them.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Benefit.** The gap formation makes the line a poor target for enemy formations; enemy charges that would hit a close formation slip through the gaps. Any enemy in the line suffers a Slight penalty to melee strikes.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Restriction.** Skirmishers cannot hold a position against determined heavy infantry — they must give ground. If pinned, they suffer Moderate penalty to all rolls.
}}

\column

## Environmental Combat

{{CoreBody
Combat on an open field in fair weather is the easy case. Real fights happen in mud, in rain, underwater, in the dark, in narrow corridors, and under the full sun of a salt-flat desert. Each environment imposes its own penalties.
}}

### Underwater

{{CoreHanging
**Movement.** Reduced to one-quarter normal speed unless the character swims. Swimming imposes a Moderate penalty to all Agility-based rolls.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Weapons.** Thrusting weapons (spears, tridents, daggers, stiletti) work at full effectiveness. Slashing and bludgeoning weapons lose a tier of damage due to water resistance. Ranged weapons other than specialty underwater-bolts are effectively useless.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Armor.** Heavy armor drags the wearer down — Moderate Endurance check each round or lose all movement. Extreme armor in water will drown an unconscious wearer.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Vision.** Murky water imposes a Moderate Perception penalty; pitch-black underwater is Major.
}}

### Dense Foliage

{{CoreHanging
**Movement.** Halved.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Weapons.** Long weapons (Reach Major or Severe) are effectively unusable. Short weapons and daggers function normally. Ranged weapons suffer Moderate hit penalty and Slight damage reduction (leaves and branches deflect or absorb shots).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Visibility.** Line of sight is drastically reduced. Treat Perception and ranged attacks as if the effective range is halved. Stealth gains a Moderate bonus.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Ambush.** Dense foliage heavily favors the ambusher. An Ambush roll made in dense foliage gains a Moderate bonus.
}}

\page

### Tight Quarters (Dungeons, Corridors, Alleys)

{{CoreHanging
**Movement.** Normal unless the corridor is narrower than five feet. Below that, movement halves.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Weapons.** Any weapon with Bulk 3 or higher suffers a Moderate penalty in five-foot corridors; Bulk 4 or higher cannot be used effectively. Reach weapons with Reach Major or Severe are unusable in corridors under six feet.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Favored Weapons.** Daggers, shortswords, maces, cinquedea, spetum, couteau de brèche, hand axe. These are the tools of the dungeon and the knife-fight alley.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Shield Advantage.** A shield wall in a corridor blocks the entire passage, granting Major cover to those behind it. A lone shieldbearer in a corridor can hold off multiple attackers single-handed.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Formation.** Most formations cannot be used. Shield wall is the exception (it shortens naturally to corridor width).
}}

### Volcanic and Geothermal Caverns

{{CoreHanging
**Heat.** Endurance check each round (Routine, escalating to Demanding near active flows). Failures impose cumulative Slight penalties until the character rests in cooler air.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Gas.** Sulfur or other toxic gas forces a Save vs. Poison (Challenging) each minute; failure causes coughing fit (Slight to all rolls until cleared) or unconsciousness (on a critical failure).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Terrain.** Obsidian fields cut feet; lava tubes collapse. Movement imposes Slight damage per round without proper footwear.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Steel.** Steel weapons and armor quenched rapidly by running water or chilled air may shatter; treat as a Durability check at Demanding difficulty. A good reason to favor bronze or leather in volcanic regions.
}}

### Ice and Snow

{{CoreHanging
**Movement.** Halved. Ice imposes a Slight Agility penalty; deep snow (knee or higher) imposes Moderate.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Weapons.** Brittle materials (obsidian, ceramic, aged leather) suffer a Major Durability penalty. Metal weapons are unaffected.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Clothing.** Improperly clothed characters take Slight cold damage per hour exposed. See Chapter 17 for cold-weather gear.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Tracking.** Snow makes tracking easy. Slight bonus to Survival checks for tracking or being tracked.
}}

\column

### Magical Darkness

{{CoreHanging
**Vision.** Normal vision is disabled. Darkvision and similar abilities function at reduced range (halved, as through fog). Dim light sources extend only their own radius and do not push back magical darkness beyond that.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Combat.** All melee attacks suffer a Moderate penalty; ranged attacks, Major. Defenders fighting blind take a Moderate penalty to Parry and Dodge. A character with proper echolocation or scent-based senses may fight at normal effectiveness.
}}

### Rain and Storm

{{CoreHanging
**Vision.** Moderate Perception penalty; ranged attacks beyond Short range impose Slight to Major accuracy penalties depending on intensity.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Weapons.** Bow strings and crossbow mechanisms suffer reduced effectiveness (Slight damage penalty in moderate rain, Major in torrential). Firearms may misfire (see Chapter 13).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Terrain.** Mud halves movement and imposes a Slight Agility penalty. Armor increases the mud penalty (Moderate for Heavy armor, Major for Extreme).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Morale.** Extended exposure to cold rain imposes a Slight penalty to Willpower rolls and morale checks.
}}

### Height Advantage and Disadvantage

{{CoreHanging
**Uphill / On Higher Ground.** Slight bonus to damage rolls; Slight bonus to initiative.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Downhill / Fighting Uphill.** Slight penalty to damage and initiative.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Significantly Higher (rooftop, cliff, horse vs. foot).** Moderate damage bonus to the higher party; Moderate attack bonus. If the lower party lacks reach, they cannot target the higher effectively in melee.
}}

\page

## Tactical Situations

### Called Shots

{{CoreBody
A called shot is an attack aimed at a specific zone of the target — the head, the sword arm, the eye, the horse's leg. The attacker takes a penalty to hit in exchange for a specific effect on hit.
}}

{{TableTitle
Called Shot Difficulty
}}

| Target | Hit Penalty | Effect on Hit |
|:--|:-:|:--|
| Torso (huge target) | — | Standard damage |
| Head | Moderate | Slight damage bonus; Stun check on critical |
| Eye / visor slit | Major | If it hits and bypasses helmet protection: Severe debuff (blind in that eye, Moderate Perception penalty permanently until healed) |
| Sword arm | Moderate | On Moderate-or-better damage, target drops weapon |
| Leg | Moderate | On Moderate-or-better damage, target's movement halved for 1 scene |
| Throat | Major | Slight damage bonus; Severe debuff if penetrated (bleed-out Endurance check each round) |
| Weapon | Moderate | Durability damage applied to weapon rather than wielder |
| Mount's leg | Major | On hit, mount falls (rider dismounted, takes fall damage) |
| Specific exposed zone | see armor | Ignores that zone's armor entirely |

{{CoreBody
Called shots stack with the piecemeal armor system (Chapter 4) for groups using both. A called shot to the head bypasses the hit-location roll and targets the head directly.
}}

### Grappling and Wrestling

{{CoreBody
Grappling is close-contact unarmed combat. The aim is to pin, disarm, throw, or strangle the opponent.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Initiation.** A grapple attempt is a Strength or Agility roll contested against the opponent's same. A successful grapple means the opponents are locked together.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Effects.** Both characters suffer a Major penalty to all other actions while grappled. Weapons longer than a dagger suffer a Moderate penalty. Movement is limited to shuffling.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Resolving.** Each round, either grappler can attempt a Pin (target loses next action), a Throw (target takes Slight damage and falls prone), a Disarm, or an Escape (contested roll to break free).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Specialized Weapons.** Daggers, stiletti, and cinquedea work at full effectiveness in a grapple. Anyone trying to grapple an armed knife-fighter without specific training will regret it.
}}

\column

### Fighting Mounted vs. Unmounted

{{CoreHanging
**Charge Bonus.** A mounted character charging an unmounted target in a straight line at full speed deals Moderate damage bonus (stacking with lance effects from Chapter 9).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Height.** Mounted combatants are higher — apply the "significantly higher" rule above.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Reach.** Mounted weapons gain Slight effective reach over the equivalent foot weapon.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Vulnerability.** The horse is a large target. An attacker may deliberately target the mount (called shot: mount's leg, at Major penalty). If the horse falls, the rider is dismounted.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Pursuit.** A mounted character can disengage at will against a foot opponent of normal speed. A foot opponent cannot pursue without entering a Chase (Chapter 8 sub-system at GM's discretion, or the character's speed-based rules).
}}

### Fighting While Wounded

{{CoreBody
A character who has taken significant damage — below half their Endurance or equivalent — begins to suffer the cumulative effects of wounds.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Half-Wounded.** Slight penalty to all physical rolls. This is the "walking wounded" state, applicable to most serious-but-not-dying combat injuries.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Heavily Wounded.** Moderate penalty. Speed reduced by half. The character is in danger of losing consciousness on further damage.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Near Death.** Major penalty. Each round requires an Endurance check to remain active.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Bleeding.** A specific wound type (called-shot throat, certain magical weapons) that produces ongoing damage per round. Stops only with proper first aid (Chapter 16 for healing alchemy; or a Medicine check).
}}

\page

### Fatigue and Stamina

{{CoreBody
Extended combat is exhausting. A character who fights for more than one round per minute of continuous combat begins to tire.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**First Scene.** No effect.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Second Scene.** Slight penalty to all physical rolls (fatigue).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Third Scene.** Moderate penalty. Heavy armor doubles this.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Extended Combat.** Beyond three scenes, Major penalty until the character has a full minute of recovery. Few historical battles featured sustained combat of this scale — they were interspersed with pauses, regrouping, and exchange of lines.
}}

### Morale and Breaking

{{CoreBody
Real soldiers flee when battles turn bad. Morale rolls apply to NPCs and sometimes to player characters, at GM discretion.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**When to Check.** First blood, loss of formation, loss of a leader, heavy casualties (30% down), flanking maneuver executed against them, terrifying opponent revealed.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**The Roll.** Willpower check at difficulty scaled to the situation. Success means the unit holds. Failure means they waver (Slight penalty next round) or break (Major penalty, begin retreat).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Leadership.** A character with a leadership skill can let an allied unit re-roll a failed morale check. Use of this ability is one of the most impactful non-combat actions a character can take on a battlefield.
}}

### Prone, Standing, and Stand-Up

{{CoreHanging
**Prone.** Characters on the ground take Moderate penalty to all combat rolls. Melee attackers against a prone target gain Moderate to-hit bonus; ranged attackers lose Moderate to-hit (the smaller target).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Standing Up.** Requires one action. While standing up, the character takes any attacks of opportunity against them normally.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Prone and Armored.** A character in Heavy or Extreme armor takes double penalty while prone. Standing up in plate requires a Challenging Endurance check without assistance.
}}

\column

### Exhaustion, Hunger, and Thirst

{{CoreBody
Long-term physical depletion impacts combat. Chapter 15 covers rations and food; this is the combat implementation.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Hunger (24+ hours without food).** Slight penalty to physical rolls. After 72 hours, Moderate. After a week, Major and declining fast.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Thirst (24+ hours without water).** Slight after 24 hours; Moderate after 48; effectively disabled within 72 hours.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Sleep Deprivation.** After 36 hours: Slight. After 60: Moderate. Sustained sleep deprivation produces unreliable decisions, Perception penalties, and eventual hallucinations.
}}

## A Note on GM Discretion

{{CoreBody
Every environment, every situation, every tactic in this chapter can be adjusted to taste. These rules are not meant to be enforced with legalistic precision; they are meant to give the GM a consistent starting point for rulings. When a situation arises that the chapter does not cover directly, choose the closest analog and apply its modifier tier. A fight in knee-deep swamp water is somewhere between "dense foliage" and "underwater." A fight on a rolling ship's deck is somewhere between "tight quarters" and "ice." The tier system is the common language; use it flexibly.
}}

{{CoreBody
Where two environmental conditions would apply simultaneously — underwater in dense kelp, in a storm on a rocking ship, in a volcanic cavern during a quake — take the larger of the two penalties rather than stacking. Stacking produces impossibly grim math. A single Major penalty is almost always more playable than three Moderates.
}}

\page

## Summary Tables

{{TableTitle
Formation Benefits
}}

| Formation | Min. Characters | Bonus |
|:--|:-:|:--|
| Shield Wall | 3+ shields | Moderate Protection, Slight to neighbor |
| Pike Phalanx | 5+ pikes | First strike; all pikes brace on charge |
| Wedge | 5+ melee | Moderate damage on linear charge |
| Feigned Retreat | Mounted skirmishers | Major bonus on ambush round |
| Cantabrian Circle | 6+ horse archers | Moderate ranged defense; Parthian shot |
| Winged Charge | 3+ long lancers | Bypasses pike Set for Charge |
| Skirmish Line | 4+ ranged | Melee-slip; Slight melee penalty imposed |

{{TableTitle
Environmental Modifiers
}}

| Environment | Movement | Weapons | Ranged | Vision | Other |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| Underwater | ×0.25 | Thrust only | None | Moderate | Drowning in heavy armor |
| Dense foliage | ×0.5 | Short only | Mod penalty | Halved | Stealth bonus |
| Tight quarters | ×0.5 (under 5ft) | Bulk ≤2 | — | — | Shield advantage |
| Volcanic | — | Steel shatter risk | — | — | Heat/gas checks |
| Ice/snow | ×0.5 | Brittle penalties | — | — | Cold damage |
| Magical dark | — | Mod penalty | Major penalty | Blind | — |
| Rain/storm | — | String/mechanism | Varies | Mod | Mud |
| Uphill | — | +Slight dmg | — | — | Initiative bonus |

{{TableTitle
Called Shot Quick Reference
}}

| Target | Penalty | Effect |
|:--|:--|:--|
| Head | Moderate | Slight dmg bonus, Stun check |
| Eye/slit | Major | Bypasses helmet, possible blindness |
| Arm | Moderate | Drop weapon on Moderate+ dmg |
| Leg | Moderate | Halved movement on Moderate+ dmg |
| Throat | Major | Bleed-out check |
| Weapon | Moderate | Damage weapon instead |
| Mount's leg | Major | Mount falls |

{{pageNumber,auto}}
{{footnote
The Ultimate Provisioner's Handbook — Chapter 8: Combat Techniques

v0.1 working draft
}}

\page

<!-- PART III OPENING VIGNETTE -- user to write: 150-250 words. Suggested speaker: a stablemaster, a caravan captain, or a shipwright. Mood: affection for the working beasts and vehicles that make adventure possible. -->

# Part III: Mounts, Vehicles & Siege

{{vignette
<!-- VIGNETTE GOES HERE -->
}}

\page

# Chapter 9: Mounts, Barding & Mounted Combat

{{Epigraph
*"A knight on foot is a story. A knight on a horse is a kingdom."*
}}
{{EpigraphAuthor
— Baron Yvon of Rosewater, *Letters to a Young Squire*
}}

## What This Chapter Covers

{{CoreBody
Horses first, because most readers will spend most of their time on horses. Then the other mounts — the working beasts, the war beasts, and the rare fantastical ones. Then barding (mount armor), mounted combat rules, care and feeding, and the economics of owning a horse. Barding splits off from Chapter 4 because it deserves its own treatment; mounted combat splits from Chapter 8 for the same reason.
}}

## Horse Breeds

{{CoreBody
Horses come in broad functional categories rather than rigid breeds. The book uses four main categories and notes specific cultural or fantasy variants within each.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Pony / Light Horse.** A small riding horse or mountain horse, fourteen hands or under. Tough, sure-footed, cheap. The horse of the working peasant, the scout, and the child learning to ride.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Solid · Speed: Normal · Carry: 200 lbs
Weight: 600 lbs · Cost: 60 gp
Traits: Sure-footed (Slight bonus on difficult terrain). Reduced eating requirement (half rations).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Rouncey.** A general-purpose riding horse, fifteen to sixteen hands, used by travelers, messengers, and men-at-arms of modest means. Not specialized for combat, but capable of carrying an armored man at speed.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Heavy · Speed: Fast · Carry: 300 lbs
Weight: 1,000 lbs · Cost: 200 gp
Traits: Balanced temperament; suitable for novice or intermediate riders.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Palfrey.** A smooth-gaited riding horse favored by nobility for long journeys. Trained to an ambling gait that is comfortable to the rider over hours of travel. Not a war horse.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Heavy · Speed: Fast · Carry: 280 lbs
Weight: 950 lbs · Cost: 350 gp
Traits: Smooth Gait (Moderate bonus to rider's Endurance during long rides). Spooks in combat without specific training (Moderate penalty to rider's mounted-combat rolls).
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Courser.** A fast, light, specifically-bred war horse. Favored for cavalry skirmishing, mounted archery, and rapid scouting. Less durable than a destrier but significantly faster.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Heavy · Speed: Very Fast · Carry: 280 lbs
Weight: 1,000 lbs · Cost: 600 gp
Traits: War-trained (no penalty to rider in combat). Slight bonus to initiative of rider.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Destrier.** The heavy war horse, bred specifically for shock cavalry combat. Muscular, tall (sixteen to seventeen hands), aggressive by training. The mount of a knight; rarely ridden outside of battle.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Crushing · Speed: Normal · Carry: 400 lbs
Weight: 1,400 lbs · Cost: 1,200 gp
Traits: War-trained. Moderate bonus to damage on a couched-lance charge. Reared-strike attack (attacks an attacker of the rider with Solid damage from hooves). Cannot be ridden without specific training; an untrained rider will be thrown.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Tarpan (Steppe Pony).** The tough, short, heat-and-cold-resistant horse of steppe and mountain cultures. Slower than a European courser but able to travel hundreds of miles without grain when other horses would die.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Crushing · Speed: Normal · Carry: 220 lbs
Weight: 800 lbs · Cost: 150 gp
Traits: Extreme endurance (Major bonus to long-ride Endurance). Eats grass alone where other horses need grain.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Desert Horse (Arabian / Akhal-Teke type).** A tall, lean, fast horse bred for hot-climate travel and light cavalry. The base stock from which most later horse breeds derive.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Heavy · Speed: Very Fast · Carry: 280 lbs
Weight: 900 lbs · Cost: 500 gp
Traits: Heat-tolerant. Slight bonus to initiative. Often nervous around large unfamiliar animals (Slight penalty to rider's control checks in mixed-mount groups).
}}

\page

## Working Mounts

{{CoreHanging
**Draft Horse.** A heavy work horse bred to pull loads rather than carry riders. Suitable for wagons, ploughs, and siege engines; clumsy and slow in combat.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Crushing · Speed: Slow · Carry: 250 lbs (ridden) / 2,000 lbs (pulled)
Weight: 1,800 lbs · Cost: 300 gp
Traits: Moderate bonus to load capacity when pulling. Cannot be war-trained.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Mule.** A hybrid of donkey and horse — sterile, tough, surly, absolutely invaluable. Carries more than a horse of equal size, over worse terrain, on worse food.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Crushing · Speed: Normal · Carry: 250 lbs
Weight: 800 lbs · Cost: 120 gp
Traits: Sure-footed (Moderate on difficult terrain). Eats rough forage. Stubborn — Agility-based control checks required to move through dangerous ground (mule refuses if it disagrees with you).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Donkey.** Smaller than a mule, simpler, cheaper. Carries less but needs less to carry it.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Heavy · Speed: Slow · Carry: 180 lbs
Weight: 500 lbs · Cost: 40 gp
Traits: Sure-footed, cheap. Brays loudly — Major Stealth penalty while accompanying.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Ox.** The baseline draft animal for heavy plowing and wagon-pulling in cultures that lack heavy horses. Slower than a horse but stronger; also edible when the work ends.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Crushing · Speed: Slow · Carry: 200 lbs (ridden, poorly) / 1,800 lbs (pulled)
Weight: 1,400 lbs · Cost: 180 gp (ox) / 300 gp (trained pair)
Traits: Cannot be ridden for any distance comfortably.
}}

\column

## Exotic and Regional Mounts

{{CoreHanging
**Camel (Dromedary).** A single-humped camel suited to hot desert travel. Outperforms any horse in sand, in heat, and over long waterless distances.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Crushing · Speed: Fast · Carry: 300 lbs
Weight: 1,100 lbs · Cost: 400 gp
Traits: Major bonus to desert travel Endurance. Can go days without water. Spits (trained or otherwise). Horses fear camels — Moderate penalty to mounted combat in mixed-mount units.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Camel (Bactrian).** Two-humped, suited to cold-desert and steppe conditions. Slower than the dromedary but even more durable.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Crushing · Speed: Normal · Carry: 400 lbs
Weight: 1,500 lbs · Cost: 500 gp
Traits: Cold-desert tolerance. Otherwise as dromedary.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**War Elephant.** A trained war beast, two tons of animal weight, with mahout riders and howdah platforms for multiple passengers.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Devastating · Speed: Normal · Carry: 800 lbs (or 2–4 passengers)
Weight: 12,000 lbs · Cost: 5,000 gp
Traits: Horses panic near elephants (Major penalty to cavalry within 50 feet). Elephant trampling deals Crushing damage. Moderate bonus to intimidation rolls while riding. Panics on Moderate damage — elephant retreats or rampages on Willpower check failure, potentially crushing friendly troops. Eats prodigiously.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Dire Wolf.** A hunting wolf of oversized build, the size of a small pony. Loyal only to a specific handler; dangerous to everyone else. Used as a mount by some steppe and wilderness cultures.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Crushing · Speed: Very Fast · Carry: 200 lbs
Weight: 350 lbs · Cost: 800 gp
Traits: Bite attack (Heavy damage). Track by scent. Refuses to be near tame horses.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Giant Lizard (Drake-Mount).** A large, docile reptilian mount, reaching the size of a draft horse. Native to desert and marshland settings. Slow in cold weather (Slight Agility penalty below 60°F).
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Heavy · Speed: Normal · Carry: 300 lbs
Weight: 1,000 lbs · Cost: 1,000 gp
Traits: Amphibious (swims capably). Tolerates heat, hates cold. Does not spook at fire.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Griffon.** A winged beast with the body of a lion and the head and wings of an eagle. Rare, expensive, and mythical-to-the-point-of-terrifying-to-horses.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Devastating · Speed: Very Fast (flight) · Carry: 250 lbs
Weight: 900 lbs · Cost: 25,000 gp (if available at all; typically quest rewards)
Traits: Flight. Beak-and-claw attack (Crushing). Intelligent — griffons bond to a specific rider; non-rider approach provokes attack. Cannot be ridden by an adult in full plate (too heavy).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Pegasus.** A winged horse. More civilized than a griffon, rarer, and somewhat faster. Traditionally bonded by gift rather than by purchase; a pegasus that has been captured for sale will often refuse its buyer.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Crushing · Speed: Very Fast (flight) · Carry: 300 lbs
Weight: 1,200 lbs · Cost: 30,000 gp (if purchased; most are gifted)
Traits: Flight. Hoof attack (Heavy). Will not carry an armored rider of aggressive alignment in some settings.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Giant Bird (Roc, Teratorn).** A massive flying bird capable of lifting a mounted rider or substantial cargo. Rare, wild, and largely untrainable — most characters encountering a roc find themselves its cargo rather than its rider.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Endurance: Devastating · Speed: Very Fast (flight) · Carry: 400 lbs
Weight: 2,000 lbs · Cost: Not commercially available
Traits: Flight. Talon attack (Devastating). Cannot be controlled by conventional riding skill; requires unique bond or magic.
}}

\column

## Barding (Mount Armor)

{{CoreBody
Barding is armor for a mount, following the same Protection tiers and material rules as personal armor (Chapter 4). Barding is almost always custom-fitted to a specific mount — a destrier's barding will not fit a courser, and certainly not a war elephant.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Light Barding (Leather or Quilt).** Padded cloth or leather covering the horse's chest, flanks, and part of the head. Mostly ceremonial or display-use; provides limited protection against glancing blows.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Light (mount)
Durability: 40 · Weight: 30 lbs · Cost: 150 gp
Coverage: Chest, flanks
Traits: Slight Speed penalty.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Mail Barding.** Chain barding covering the chest, flanks, and neck of the horse. Effective against glancing arrows and light blades.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Medium (mount)
Durability: 100 · Weight: 80 lbs · Cost: 700 gp
Coverage: Chest, flanks, neck
Traits: Moderate Speed penalty. Slight Endurance penalty during extended riding.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Plate Barding (Shaffron + Crinet + Peytral + Flanchard).** A full articulated plate barding, including face-plate (shaffron), neck-guard (crinet), chest-plate (peytral), and side-plates (flanchard).
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Heavy (mount)
Durability: 180 · Weight: 140 lbs · Cost: 2,500 gp
Coverage: Head, neck, chest, flanks
Traits: Major Speed penalty. Moderate Endurance penalty. Cannot be donned without assistance (two grooms, 30 minutes). Severe restriction on rough terrain (horse may stumble).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Full Plate Barding (Complete Caparison).** Full head-to-hoof plate, most commonly seen at tournaments and in the highest ranks of ceremonial cavalry. Practical combat value is limited — the horse tires quickly under the weight.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Extreme (mount)
Durability: 240 · Weight: 200 lbs · Cost: 5,000 gp
Coverage: Entire mount
Traits: Severe Speed penalty. Severe Endurance penalty. Not suitable for field combat; used in tournaments (see Chapter 18).
}}

\page

## Mounted Combat

{{CoreBody
Fighting from horseback combines the rules from Chapter 8 (height advantage, charge bonuses) with specific mount-related considerations. The most important mechanics are below.
}}

### The Charge

{{CoreBody
A charge is a mounted attack delivered at full speed in a straight line. It is the core offensive maneuver of cavalry, and the reason cavalry exists as a battlefield category.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Requirements.** At least 30 feet of open ground to build speed. Straight line of approach. Weapon appropriate to the charge (lance, spear, or charged sword).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Damage Bonus.** Moderate damage bonus (lance) or Slight bonus (sword or axe) on the initial strike.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Impact.** On a successful strike at Moderate-or-better damage, the target must make a Knockdown check (Endurance at Challenging difficulty, modified by target's mass and armor). Unmounted infantry are far more vulnerable to impact than mounted opponents.
}}

### The Lance

{{CoreHanging
**Lance (War Lance).** A long, straight wooden spear designed for mounted charging. Used once per charge, typically; many lances break on impact (and are designed to do so, to transfer impact energy).
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing (charged); Heavy (not charged)
Weight: 8 lbs · Bulk: 4
Cost: 40 gp (standard) / 120 gp (reusable hardwood)
Traits: Two-Handed (mounted only). Reach (Major). Set for Charge (always, from a mount). Standard lances have 50% chance to break on a successful Crushing-or-greater hit. Reusable lances (composite shaft) do not break.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Long Lance (Polish / Hussar Lance).** An extended lance of sixteen to twenty feet, with a narrow head and hollow shaft segments for weight reduction. The signature weapon of Polish winged hussars and similar heavy cavalry elites.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Devastating (charged)
Weight: 11 lbs · Bulk: 5
Cost: 180 gp
Traits: As lance, plus Reach (Severe). Breaks on any Crushing-or-greater hit. Specifically designed to bypass pike formations (see Winged Charge, Chapter 8).
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Jousting Lance.** A tournament variant with a blunt, often crown-shaped or branched head designed to unseat rather than kill. Hollow, weakened shaft that fragments spectacularly on impact.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid (for unseating purposes); no lethal damage
Weight: 14 lbs · Bulk: 5
Cost: 90 gp (disposable)
Traits: Tournament use only. Always breaks on impact. See Chapter 18 for tournament rules.
}}

### Mounted Archery

{{CoreHanging
**Trained Archer + Light Mount.** A trained mounted archer firing a shortbow, composite bow, or yumi from a moving horse suffers no penalty. Untrained archers (or archers on untrained horses) suffer Moderate penalty.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Firing on the Move.** A trained horse archer can fire while at full gallop. Firing while turning requires an additional trained-archer check (Routine). Firing backward (Parthian shot) requires specific training or a Challenging check.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Crossbow Restrictions.** Heavy and arbalest crossbows cannot be used from horseback. Light crossbows can, at Moderate penalty. Hand crossbows work at no penalty.
}}

### Dismounting and Falls

{{CoreHanging
**Voluntary Dismount.** One action; character is now on foot. Some mounts (destrier, dire wolf) may not wait for their rider and will charge into melee alone if agitated.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Forced Dismount.** Successful Called Shot to Mount's Leg, successful Grapple from adjacent foot opponent, horse knocked down by obstacle or spell. Rider makes Agility check at Challenging difficulty or falls prone (Slight fall damage).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Falling from a Moving Mount.** Rider takes Moderate fall damage scaled to speed; prone, one action to stand. Armored riders may take longer to regain feet.
}}

\page

## Care and Feeding

{{CoreBody
A horse is not a bicycle. It requires food, water, rest, and maintenance — most of which cost money, most of which cannot be skipped, and most of which the adventuring party will forget until someone wakes up in the morning to find their destrier has kicked a hole in the stable door.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Daily Grain and Hay.** A working horse needs 8–15 lbs of grain and hay per day, depending on breed and activity. At inn prices, this runs 2 sp to 1 gp per day per horse. Grazing can substitute for some of this (hay), but working horses cannot sustain on grass alone for long.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Water.** Five to ten gallons per day. Critical — a dehydrated horse can die within a few days in hot climates.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Stabling.** Most towns have public stables at 3 sp per night, inclusive of food. Private stabling in a noble household is free but presumes the favor of a noble household.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Farrier Services.** Horseshoes wear out every six weeks or so under hard use. Re-shoeing costs 2 gp and takes one hour. A horse with bad shoeing will become lame.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Veterinary Care.** Injuries require either a horse-doctor or a patient healer. Serious injuries — from combat, from a fall, from colic — can cost 50 gp or more to treat.
}}

### Mount Durability and Recovery

{{CoreBody
Mounts have Durability (or hit points) like characters. The same recovery rules apply, with a few adjustments.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Mount Durability.** Pony/Light: 40 · Rouncey/Courser: 60 · Destrier: 90 · Elephant: 200 · Camel: 70 · Dire Wolf: 80 · Giant Lizard: 85 · Griffon: 120 · Pegasus: 110
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Recovery.** Mounts recover at half the rate of a human character of comparable skill. A badly-wounded horse needs weeks of rest and active care to recover; a lamed horse may never fully recover.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Hard Math.** A horse that dies from combat cannot be raised cheaply, and a horse that has been wounded and mended may be permanently less valuable. Player characters who ride warhorses into every battle will lose horses. This is one of the major expenses of a professional cavalry force.
}}

\column

## Travel and Pace

{{TableTitle
Daily Travel Distances (8-hour day, normal terrain)
}}

| Mount | Easy Pace | Normal | Forced March |
|:--|:-:|:-:|:-:|
| Walking character | 15 mi | 24 mi | 30 mi |
| Pony / donkey | 20 mi | 30 mi | 35 mi |
| Rouncey / palfrey | 30 mi | 40 mi | 50 mi |
| Courser | 30 mi | 50 mi | 70 mi |
| Destrier | 25 mi | 35 mi | 45 mi |
| Tarpan | 25 mi | 40 mi | 60 mi |
| Camel | 30 mi | 50 mi | 80 mi (desert) |
| Mule | 25 mi | 35 mi | 40 mi |
| Ox-cart | 15 mi | 20 mi | 25 mi |
| War elephant | 20 mi | 30 mi | 35 mi |

{{CoreBody
Forced march requires an Endurance check each day; failure produces cumulative fatigue effects and possible injury. Three consecutive days of forced marching reliably breaks down all but the toughest mounts.
}}

## Fantasy Variants

{{note
##### Faye-touched Steeds

Some settings feature horses bred or blessed by fae powers — horses that do not tire, that run on water, that appear only by moonlight. Treat as a courser or destrier with Moderate bonus to all Endurance rolls, immunity to mundane fear, and a Severe price bonus. Specific lore varies by setting; the mechanical profile does not.
}}

{{note
##### Draconic Mounts

A young dragon, a drake, or a wyvern may be tamed by appropriate characters in some settings. Treat as a giant flying mount with flight speed, a breath weapon (see Chapter 16 for elemental effects, multiplied by ×3 damage), and intelligence that often exceeds the rider's. Price: not applicable; dragons are not bought. See Chapter 19 for how to build this relationship mechanically.
}}

{{note
##### Living-Wood Treants

A moving tree-creature mount, rare and specific to druidic traditions. Extremely slow (half walking character speed), but can pass through dense forest without penalty and can heal itself overnight. Carry: 400 lbs. Not commercially available.
}}

\page

## Summary Tables

{{TableTitle
Mount Quick Reference
}}

| Mount | Endurance | Speed | Carry | Cost |
|:--|:--|:--|:-:|:-:|
| Pony | Solid | Normal | 200 | 60 |
| Rouncey | Heavy | Fast | 300 | 200 |
| Palfrey | Heavy | Fast | 280 | 350 |
| Courser | Heavy | V.Fast | 280 | 600 |
| Destrier | Crushing | Normal | 400 | 1,200 |
| Tarpan | Crushing | Normal | 220 | 150 |
| Desert horse | Heavy | V.Fast | 280 | 500 |
| Draft horse | Crushing | Slow | 250/2,000 | 300 |
| Mule | Crushing | Normal | 250 | 120 |
| Donkey | Heavy | Slow | 180 | 40 |
| Ox | Crushing | Slow | 200/1,800 | 180 |
| Camel (drom.) | Crushing | Fast | 300 | 400 |
| Camel (bact.) | Crushing | Normal | 400 | 500 |
| War elephant | Devastating | Normal | 800 | 5,000 |
| Dire wolf | Crushing | V.Fast | 200 | 800 |
| Giant lizard | Heavy | Normal | 300 | 1,000 |
| Griffon | Devastating | V.Fast(fly) | 250 | 25,000+ |
| Pegasus | Crushing | V.Fast(fly) | 300 | 30,000+ |
| Giant bird | Devastating | V.Fast(fly) | 400 | — |

{{TableTitle
Barding Quick Reference
}}

| Barding | Protection | Dur | Wt | Cost | Speed |
|:--|:--|:-:|:-:|:-:|:--|
| Light | Light | 40 | 30 | 150 | −Slight |
| Mail | Medium | 100 | 80 | 700 | −Moderate |
| Plate | Heavy | 180 | 140 | 2,500 | −Major |
| Full plate | Extreme | 240 | 200 | 5,000 | −Severe |

{{pageNumber,auto}}

The Ultimate Provisioner's Handbook — Chapter 9: Mounts, Barding & Mounted Combat

v0.1 working draft
}}

\page

# Chapter 10: Land Vehicles

{{Epigraph
*"Nothing makes a road less impressive than trying to roll a laden wagon down it."*
}}
{{EpigraphAuthor
— Caravan-master Harl of the Ironway
}}

## The Vehicles of Travel and War

{{CoreBody
Carts, wagons, and carriages. Sleds, chariots, and war-wagons. The unglamorous machines that move supplies, furniture, prisoners, dowries, siege engines, grain harvests, and entire armies across a landscape. This chapter covers them by function: light transport, heavy transport, specialty vehicles, and war vehicles.
}}

## How Vehicles Are Stated

{{CoreBody
Each vehicle entry includes:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Cargo Capacity.** Weight the vehicle can carry reliably, in pounds.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Draft Animals.** How many mounts or creatures are required to pull the vehicle at normal speed.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Speed.** Miles per day on a good road. Reduce by half for rough terrain; by three-quarters or more for no road at all.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Durability.** The vehicle's structural hit points. Wagons that take enough damage break down.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Crew.** Minimum number of characters required to operate the vehicle. More crew may be helpful but are not required.
}}

## Light Transport

{{CoreHanging
**Handcart.** A small two-wheeled cart pushed or pulled by a single person. Capacity limited but portable through narrow spaces.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 200 lbs · Animals: 0 (person-powered) · Speed: 2 mph
Weight: 40 lbs · Durability: 30 · Crew: 1
Cost: 15 gp
Traits: Fits through doorways. Cannot be used on unimproved trails.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Wheelbarrow.** A one-wheeled garden cart, balanced by the operator. Smaller capacity than a handcart, greater maneuverability.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 80 lbs · Animals: 0 · Speed: 2 mph
Weight: 20 lbs · Durability: 20 · Crew: 1
Cost: 5 gp
Traits: Works on rough ground where a two-wheel cart cannot.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Pack Cart.** A small, two-wheeled cart drawn by one donkey, mule, or pony. The basic transport of farmers, tinkers, and peasant merchants.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 500 lbs · Animals: 1 · Speed: 12 mph (road) / 5 mph (rough)
Weight: 150 lbs · Durability: 50 · Crew: 1
Cost: 40 gp
Traits: Requires one draft animal. Suitable for rural roads; rough going cross-country.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Light Wagon.** A four-wheeled wagon drawn by two horses or oxen. The working transport of most rural economies.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 1,500 lbs · Animals: 2 · Speed: 15 mph (road) / 8 mph (rough)
Weight: 400 lbs · Durability: 120 · Crew: 1 driver + passengers
Cost: 120 gp
Traits: Standard road wagon. Can be covered with canvas for weather protection (add 15 gp).
}}

## Heavy Transport

{{CoreHanging
**Freight Wagon.** A heavy four-wheel wagon drawn by four draft animals. Used by traveling merchants, quartermasters, and long-distance caravans.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 3,000 lbs · Animals: 4 · Speed: 12 mph (road) / 5 mph (rough)
Weight: 800 lbs · Durability: 200 · Crew: 1 driver + 1 brake/guard
Cost: 250 gp
Traits: Heavy suspension. Requires maintained road for full speed. Good roads only — rough ground may permanently damage.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Conestoga (Heavy Covered Wagon).** A large, canvas-covered freight wagon with a curved hull, designed for long-haul travel across mixed terrain.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 5,000 lbs · Animals: 6 · Speed: 10 mph (road) / 4 mph (rough)
Weight: 1,400 lbs · Durability: 280 · Crew: 1–2 drivers
Cost: 400 gp
Traits: Watertight canvas cover; doubles as shelter. Can cross shallow rivers without unloading. Difficult to turn in tight spaces.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Caravan (Wagon Train).** Multiple freight wagons operated as a unit, with guards, cooks, and spare draft animals. Not a single vehicle but the standard format of long-distance trade.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 20,000+ lbs · Animals: 20+ · Speed: 8 mph (typical) / 4 mph (rough)
Durability: per wagon · Crew: 6–12 drivers/guards
Cost: 1,500 gp (base, per four wagons) plus crew salaries
Traits: Provides security by numbers. Slow; visible from miles away. Attractive target. See Appendix E for caravan economics.
}}

## Carriages and Personal Vehicles

{{CoreHanging
**Buggy / Gig.** A small, light personal carriage drawn by one horse. Two-wheeled; open or with a folding hood.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 400 lbs (2 persons + light cargo) · Animals: 1 · Speed: 15 mph
Weight: 200 lbs · Durability: 80 · Crew: 1
Cost: 150 gp
Traits: Built for speed and light personal transport. Poor in bad weather.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Coach (Standard).** A four-wheeled enclosed carriage drawn by two horses. The standard transport of middle-class professionals and urban delivery.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 800 lbs (4 persons) · Animals: 2 · Speed: 12 mph
Weight: 500 lbs · Durability: 150 · Crew: 1 driver
Cost: 300 gp
Traits: Enclosed; weatherproof. Two lamps standard at night.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Grand Coach (Four-Horse).** A larger, heavier enclosed coach drawn by four horses. The carriage of wealthy merchants, minor nobles, and traveling officials.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 1,200 lbs (6 persons) · Animals: 4 · Speed: 12 mph
Weight: 900 lbs · Durability: 200 · Crew: 1 driver + 1 footman
Cost: 800 gp
Traits: Comfortably upholstered. Internal storage compartments. Status symbol (Slight Presence bonus when arriving at functions).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**State Carriage.** An elaborate eight-horse ceremonial coach, gilded and often armored. Not a practical vehicle; a moving statement of power.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 2,000 lbs (6 persons in luxury) · Animals: 8 · Speed: 10 mph
Weight: 2,000 lbs · Durability: 300 · Crew: 2 drivers + 2 footmen + 2 guards
Cost: 5,000 gp
Traits: Moderate Presence bonus. Moderate Cover bonus (windows can be shuttered). Cannot navigate narrow roads or rough terrain. Slow to unhitch.
}}

\column

## Sleds and Snow Vehicles

{{CoreHanging
**Sled (Light).** A small runner-mounted vehicle drawn by a single horse, dog team, or character on foot. Essential in deep-snow cultures; useless outside winter.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 400 lbs · Animals: 1 · Speed: 8 mph (snow) / 0 (bare ground)
Weight: 80 lbs · Durability: 60 · Crew: 1
Cost: 50 gp
Traits: Snow-only. Cannot be used on unfrozen ground without severe damage to runners.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Sledge (Heavy).** A larger runner-mounted transport for heavy loads — logs, stone, siege material — dragged across snow, ice, or even grass in emergencies.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 2,500 lbs · Animals: 2–4 · Speed: 6 mph (snow) / 2 mph (grass)
Weight: 400 lbs · Durability: 180 · Crew: 1 + assistants
Cost: 180 gp
Traits: Functions on grass at reduced speed. Essential for winter logging and winter sieges.
}}

## Specialty Vehicles

{{CoreHanging
**Alchemist's Wagon.** A purpose-built freight wagon with racks, ventilated compartments, and padded storage for delicate and dangerous cargo. The mobile workshop of a traveling alchemist.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 2,000 lbs of alchemical goods · Animals: 2 · Speed: 12 mph
Weight: 700 lbs · Durability: 150 · Crew: 1 driver + 1 alchemist
Cost: 500 gp (plus outfitting)
Traits: Built-in safety features — ventilation, fireproof compartments, lockable cabinets. Moderate bonus to alchemical work performed inside.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Farrier's Wagon.** A portable smithy on wheels. Used by armies, large caravans, and traveling blacksmiths. Includes a small forge, anvil, tools, and work space.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 1,500 lbs + forge setup · Animals: 2 · Speed: 10 mph
Weight: 1,000 lbs · Durability: 160 · Crew: 1 driver + 1 smith
Cost: 600 gp
Traits: Field crafting facility. Acts as a smith's workshop per Chapter 3 when unhitched and set up (20 minutes). Good for horseshoe-work on the move.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Prison Wagon.** A freight wagon with an iron-barred cage body, used for transporting prisoners or captured animals. Heavy, slow, and uncomfortable for its occupants.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 6 humanoid prisoners (or 2 large) · Animals: 2 · Speed: 10 mph
Weight: 900 lbs · Durability: 200 · Crew: 1 driver + 2 guards
Cost: 350 gp
Traits: Reinforced cage (Heavy tier for escape attempts). Slow.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Mobile Kitchen (Field Kitchen).** A wagon fitted with stoves, water tanks, cutting surfaces, and food storage. Used by armies to feed troops on the move.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: Food for 200 for one day · Animals: 2 · Speed: 10 mph
Weight: 800 lbs · Durability: 150 · Crew: 1 driver + 2 cooks
Cost: 400 gp
Traits: Can prepare hot meals during a daily march halt. Moderate bonus to army's morale/Endurance checks.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Medical Wagon (Ambulance).** A covered wagon fitted for patient transport — padded interior, shelves for supplies, sometimes a small folding table for emergency care.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 4 wounded · Animals: 2 · Speed: 10 mph
Weight: 600 lbs · Durability: 140 · Crew: 1 driver + 1 medic
Cost: 300 gp
Traits: Slight bonus to medical checks for patients inside. Provides shelter, warmth, and basic care equipment.
}}

## War Vehicles

{{CoreHanging
**Chariot (Light).** A light, fast two-wheeled war vehicle drawn by two horses, carrying a driver and one to two fighters. Classical-era warfare; rare after the fall of chariot cultures.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 2 fighters + 1 driver · Animals: 2 · Speed: 20 mph
Weight: 200 lbs · Durability: 80 · Crew: 1 driver + 1–2 fighters
Cost: 500 gp
Traits: Unusable on rough terrain. Passengers gain mobility for ranged attacks (moving ranged without penalty). Easy to overturn (Challenging Agility check on sudden turns).
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**War Chariot (Heavy / Scythed).** A heavy chariot with scythe-blades extending from the axles, designed to cut through infantry formations at the charge.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 2 fighters + 1 driver · Animals: 4 · Speed: 15 mph
Weight: 500 lbs · Durability: 150 · Crew: 1 driver + 2 fighters
Cost: 1,200 gp
Traits: Scythe-blades deal Crushing damage to any infantry adjacent when the chariot passes at speed. Once engaged in melee, extremely vulnerable to being dismounted.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**War-Wagon (Tabor Wagon).** The Hussite-style armored freight wagon, with reinforced sides, hinged wooden flaps that can be raised for total cover, and a platform for crossbowmen or hand-gunners to fire from.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 10-man crew + crossbows/firearms + supplies · Animals: 4 · Speed: 8 mph
Weight: 2,000 lbs · Durability: 350 · Crew: 1 driver + 10 fighters
Cost: 1,800 gp
Traits: When "fortified" (raised flaps, 1 action), provides Major cover to all crew inside. Can be chained to other war-wagons to form a mobile fortress (see Chapter 8 / Wagon Fort tactical rules). A platform for small swivel-guns (see Chapter 13); a Tarnice (small breech-loading cannon) deals Solid × 10 damage.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Siege Tower.** A wheeled wooden tower taller than the wall it's attacking, with internal ladders or ramps for storming troops. Covered with hides or wet timber to resist fire.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 20 fighters · Animals: 12–20 (pushed, not pulled) · Speed: 0.5 mph
Weight: 12,000 lbs · Durability: 800 · Crew: crew + pushers
Cost: 5,000 gp
Traits: Height matching target wall (typical 20–30 ft). Fire-resistant covering (Major bonus against arrows and small fire). Vulnerable to ballista, trebuchet, or targeted fire attacks (Moderate resistance only). Requires level ground and reasonable approach.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Guliai-Gorod (Walking Walls).** A Russian / Eastern European system of pre-fabricated wall sections on wheels or sleds, pushed forward by infantry to create a mobile wall of defensive cover. Each section is one panel; armies might field fifty or more.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity (per section): n/a (pure cover) · Animals: 0 (pushed) · Speed: 1 mph
Weight (per section): 400 lbs · Durability: 180 · Crew: 4 pushers per section
Cost: 150 gp per section
Traits: Provides Major cover to any characters behind it. Sections lock together into a continuous wall. Can be used on flat terrain only. Winter sleds available (see sled-mounted variant).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Helepolis (Siege Fortress).** The Hellenistic siege tower-of-towers: a massive wheeled siege engine with multiple stories, ballista batteries, and crew quarters. Almost never seen outside classical settings; when seen, it usually wins.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 50+ fighters and several ballistae · Animals: 80+ (pushed) · Speed: 0.2 mph
Weight: 40,000+ lbs · Durability: 2,500 · Crew: large
Cost: 40,000+ gp
Traits: Unique siege weapon. Cannot be stopped by any ordinary battlefield force. Extremely vulnerable to specialized counter-weapons (Greek fire, massive rams). See Chapter 12 for full siege rules.
}}

## Vehicle Wear and Repair

{{CoreBody
Wagons and carts wear out. The rules in Chapter 3 apply, with specific notes:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Wheels.** The most common failure point. Expect a wheel replacement every 500–1,000 miles of road use, sooner on rough ground. Replacement is a Carpentry check at Routine difficulty and half a day of work, plus materials (5–15 gp for wagon-grade wheels).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Axles.** Heavy wagon axles last years but can snap under sudden stress. Replacement requires a day of work, a Smithy facility (for iron-reinforced axles), and 25–50 gp of materials.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Brakes.** Leather brake shoes wear with use. Budget one replacement per two or three months of active use. Minor cost (5 gp).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Canvas Covers.** Sun and weather degrade canvas. Expect a cover replacement every two to three years of continuous use, or sooner for heavy road work.
}}

\column

## Driving, Animal Handling, and Road Hazards

{{CoreBody
Piloting a wagon is not difficult for most ordinary travel. Fast driving, combat driving, or negotiating hazardous terrain is another matter.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Standard Driving.** No check required.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Fast Driving.** Challenging Animal Handling check. Success doubles speed for one hour; failure risks wheel damage or a turnover (Challenging Agility check or the vehicle crashes).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Combat Driving.** Challenging-to-Demanding Animal Handling check to maintain control while under attack, making tight turns, or dodging ranged fire. Failure can dismount passengers, flip the vehicle, or spook the animals.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Mud.** Heavy mud slows wagons by half and risks stuck wheels (Challenging Strength check to push free, often requiring the wagon to be partially unloaded).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Steep Slopes.** A laden wagon descending a steep slope must be braked constantly. Failure to brake: runaway (Demanding Agility check or the wagon smashes).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Banditry.** Bandits target wagons. An armed driver and guards are almost always necessary for freight wagons carrying value. See Appendix E for road-hazard tables.
}}

## Summary Table

{{TableTitle
Land Vehicles at a Glance
}}

| Vehicle | Cap. (lbs) | Animals | Speed | Dur. | Cost |

| Handcart | 200 | 0 | 2 | 30 | 15 |
| Pack cart | 500 | 1 | 12 | 50 | 40 |

\page

# Chapter 11: Ships & Nautical Warfare

{{Epigraph
*"A ship is a weapon only if its captain has remembered to bring one. Otherwise it is a wet coffin that floats."*
}}
{{EpigraphAuthor
— Admiral Pell of the Silver Strait
}}

## What This Chapter Covers

{{CoreBody
Sailing vessels, rowing vessels, river craft, and the rare specialty that combines the three. Crew requirements, cargo capacities, travel speeds, and the mechanics of naval combat — ramming, boarding, grappling, and the unpleasant alchemical weapons developed specifically for burning other ships.
}}

{{CoreBody
Shipbuilding as a crafting project is not fully treated here — it follows Chapter 3's framework but at seasonal timescales, with a dedicated shipyard facility and crews of skilled workers. A potential future expansion; see the project spec.
}}

## How Ships Are Stated

{{CoreHanging
**Cargo Capacity.** Tons of cargo the vessel can carry while remaining seaworthy. One ton equals 2,000 lbs.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Crew.** Minimum working crew / maximum battle crew. Larger vessels can function with a skeleton crew in calm weather but need full complements for combat or storm handling.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Speed.** Typical cruising speed in knots (nautical miles per hour) under favorable conditions. Reduce by half in headwinds; by three-quarters in poor conditions.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Durability.** The ship's structural hit points. Ships that take enough damage begin to founder.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Draft.** The minimum water depth the vessel needs to float. Deep-draft ships cannot enter shallow harbors or navigate rivers.
}}

## River and Coastal Craft

{{CoreHanging
**Rowboat.** A small wooden boat for four to eight passengers, propelled by oars. Used as ship's tender, river crossing, or shore-hop transport.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 600 lbs · Crew: 1–4 · Speed: 3 knots
Durability: 40 · Draft: 1 ft
Cost: 50 gp
Traits: Can be beached on any shore. Easily portaged overland by multiple people.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Coracle.** A round, hide-covered wicker boat used on rivers and coastal shallows in Celtic and similar cultures. Very light, very unstable, very maneuverable.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 200 lbs · Crew: 1–2 · Speed: 2 knots
Durability: 20 · Draft: 0.5 ft
Cost: 15 gp
Traits: Can be carried on one person's back. Capsizes easily in weather.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**River Barge.** A long, flat-bottomed craft poled or towed along calm rivers, carrying heavy cargo at slow speeds.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 20 tons · Crew: 3–6 · Speed: 2 knots (downstream) / 1 knot (poled upstream)
Durability: 120 · Draft: 2 ft
Cost: 300 gp
Traits: River-only. Grounds on any sandbar. Can be linked into a barge train for bulk trade.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Fishing Smack.** A small single-masted sailing vessel, twenty to thirty feet, used for coastal fishing and short-haul cargo.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 3 tons · Crew: 2–4 · Speed: 5 knots
Durability: 100 · Draft: 4 ft
Cost: 400 gp
Traits: Harbor-to-harbor only. Cannot venture out of sight of land without risk.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Coastal Trader (Knarr / Lugger).** A medium single-masted sailing vessel for near-shore trade and passenger transport.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 15 tons · Crew: 4–8 · Speed: 6 knots
Durability: 180 · Draft: 6 ft
Cost: 1,200 gp
Traits: Can handle open water briefly. Adequate storage; not a true deepwater vessel.
}}

## Sea-Going Sailing Vessels

{{CoreHanging
**Longship.** A long, shallow-draft rowing-and-sailing vessel, clinker-built and highly seaworthy for its era. The Norse exploration and raiding vessel.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 20 tons · Crew: 30–60 · Speed: 8 knots (sail) / 4 knots (rowing)
Durability: 250 · Draft: 3 ft
Cost: 3,500 gp
Traits: Shallow draft — can beach on sand or navigate rivers. Rows against the wind. Exposed deck (no below-decks shelter).
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Cog.** A single-masted, high-sided merchant ship characteristic of northern European trade through the late medieval period. Carries bulk cargo in a rounded hull.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 100 tons · Crew: 12–30 · Speed: 5 knots
Durability: 450 · Draft: 10 ft
Cost: 6,000 gp
Traits: High freeboard — difficult to board from a smaller ship. Slow; cannot outrun pursuit. Large cargo capacity relative to crew size.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Carrack.** A three- or four-masted deep-water merchant ship, the workhorse of the age of exploration.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 300 tons · Crew: 30–60 · Speed: 6 knots
Durability: 700 · Draft: 14 ft
Cost: 15,000 gp
Traits: Ocean-capable. Armed with small deck guns by standard; heavier armaments optional. Slow to turn.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Galleon.** A large warship-trader, evolution of the carrack, built for both cargo capacity and combat. The classic "treasure galleon" of age-of-exploration fiction.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 250 tons · Crew: 100–200 · Speed: 7 knots
Durability: 900 · Draft: 16 ft
Cost: 30,000 gp
Traits: Carries 20–40 cannon (see Chapter 13). Moderate Speed bonus vs. carrack. Significant crew requirement.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Caravel.** A light, fast, lateen-rigged exploration and trade ship. Maneuverable and capable of sailing closer to the wind than square-rigged vessels.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 50 tons · Crew: 15–25 · Speed: 8 knots
Durability: 280 · Draft: 7 ft
Cost: 4,500 gp
Traits: Slight Speed bonus. Excellent upwind performance. Minimal cargo relative to size.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Dhow.** A lateen-rigged single- or two-masted vessel native to Arabian and East African waters. Flexible, seaworthy, and capable of significant ocean voyages.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 50 tons · Crew: 10–20 · Speed: 7 knots
Durability: 260 · Draft: 6 ft
Cost: 3,800 gp
Traits: Slight Speed bonus upwind. Cargo versatility — trades in everything from spices to slaves historically.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Junk.** A Chinese merchant ship with distinctive battened sails and watertight compartments. Surprisingly seaworthy; the compartments made junks resistant to sinking from hull breaches.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 200 tons · Crew: 20–40 · Speed: 6 knots
Durability: 600 · Draft: 12 ft
Cost: 10,000 gp
Traits: Compartmentalized hull — a hull breach floods only the affected compartment, granting Moderate Durability bonus against ramming and ballistic damage.
}}

## War Galleys and Rowing Vessels

{{CoreHanging
**War Galley.** A long oar-and-sail warship, typical of Mediterranean naval warfare through the early modern period. Fast in calm water; vulnerable in open sea.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 30 tons · Crew: 150–200 (rowers + marines) · Speed: 9 knots (rowing) / 6 knots (sail)
Durability: 400 · Draft: 7 ft
Cost: 8,000 gp
Traits: Ram prow (see naval combat). Large marine complement for boarding. Slow to turn; vulnerable to larger ships in open water. Rowers are usually slaves or criminals; morale consideration in extended campaigns.
}]

{{CoreHanging
**Trireme (Classical War Galley).** A three-banked oar warship of antiquity. Faster and more maneuverable than later galleys; requires extreme crew coordination.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 15 tons · Crew: 170–200 · Speed: 10 knots (rowing)
Durability: 320 · Draft: 5 ft
Cost: 6,500 gp
Traits: Bronze ram. Exceptional turning ability. Cannot remain at sea overnight comfortably — beached each night for crew rest.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Turtle Ship (Geobukseon).** A Korean armored galley with a shell-like roof of iron plates and spikes, designed specifically to repel boarders and resist cannon fire. Rarely encountered outside specific historical settings.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 20 tons · Crew: 100–130 (rowers + gunners) · Speed: 7 knots
Durability: 600 · Draft: 6 ft
Cost: 12,000 gp
Traits: Armored shell (Major bonus to Durability against ranged and boarding). Protruding spikes prevent grappling. Multiple cannon (Chapter 13). Extremely expensive to maintain.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Dromon (Byzantine War Galley).** A medium-sized Byzantine galley carrying the dreaded Greek Fire siphon. Cannot be built outside settings where Greek fire is available.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 25 tons · Crew: 100–150 · Speed: 9 knots
Durability: 380 · Draft: 7 ft
Cost: 10,000 gp
Traits: Greek Fire projector (see naval combat). Ram prow.
}}

## Specialty Vessels

{{CoreHanging
**Slave Galley.** A variant of the war galley using large numbers of chained slave rowers rather than trained crew. Slower to deploy, cheaper to maintain, and a moral quagmire to every campaign.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 30 tons · Crew: 10 free + 150 chained rowers · Speed: 7 knots
Durability: 380 · Draft: 7 ft
Cost: 5,000 gp
Traits: Rowers bring catastrophic problems when taking damage or boarded (chaining below decks means fires or flooding kill the rowers first; morale of a boarding attempt collapses when the captured slaves are freed and turn on the capturers). Moderate Presence penalty in any civilized port.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Fireship.** A sacrificial vessel packed with pitch, oil, and alchemical incendiaries, sailed into an enemy fleet and set alight. An expensive one-shot weapon; the ship and its skeleton crew are both lost by design.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 0 (filled with incendiaries) · Crew: 2–5 (skeleton)
Durability: varies · Cost: 2,000 gp + incendiaries
Traits: On successful collision with enemy ship, deals Devastating fire damage scaling with loaded incendiaries. Crew must abandon ship immediately upon ignition.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Hospital Ship.** A converted merchant vessel used as a floating infirmary during naval campaigns. No combat value; transports wounded between engagements and port.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 100 wounded · Crew: 20 + 4 medics · Speed: 5 knots
Durability: 350 · Draft: 9 ft
Cost: 5,000 gp (converted merchant)
Traits: Flies a distinctive flag; traditional law of arms forbids attacking hospital ships. Traditional law of arms is not always honored.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Treasure Ship (Baochuan).** A massive Chinese oceangoing ship, largest wooden vessels ever built. Historical specimens reached over four hundred feet in length. Rare; encountered only in specific settings.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cargo: 2,000 tons · Crew: 300–500 · Speed: 6 knots
Durability: 2,500 · Draft: 20 ft
Cost: 80,000+ gp
Traits: Enormous cargo. Can carry smaller ships as boats. Deep draft restricts to major ports. Slow; cannot engage in maneuver combat.
}}

## Fantasy Variants

{{note
##### Elven Living-Wood Ship

A ship built from living wood (Chapter 2), self-repairing and resistant to rot. Cannot be set alight by mundane fire. Durability increases by a Major bonus; repair happens overnight as long as the ship is afloat in clean water. Cost: +Severe (×10) over the base ship's price. Elves rarely sell ships; acquiring one is a major story event.
}}

{{note
##### Sky-Ship (Aetheric Vessel)

A rare class of flying vessel, powered by bound elementals, enchanted lifting crystals, or similar exotic principles. Specific lore varies wildly by setting. Treat as a sailing ship that moves at standard naval speed but through air rather than water. Vulnerable to very different threats (air currents, aerial predators, altitude limits). Cost: 50,000+ gp and almost always uniquely commissioned.
}}

{{note
##### Ghost Ship

A cursed or haunted vessel, sailed by the dead or controlled by a bound spirit. Mechanics vary entirely by setting; some provide otherwise-impossible capabilities (sailing without wind, vanishing into fog) in exchange for supernatural risks (the captain's soul, sanity of the crew, attraction of certain undead). Plot device rather than purchasable gear.
}}

\page

## Naval Combat

{{CoreBody
Naval combat happens at three scales: long-range exchange (cannon, ballista, archery), ramming and boarding approach, and hand-to-hand boarding action. Each has its own mechanics.
}}

### Ranged Ship Combat

{{CoreHanging
**Ballista / Onager Exchange.** Medieval-era ship artillery. Ships equipped with deck-mounted ballistae exchange fire at ranges out to 600 feet. Individual shots rarely sink ships; cumulative damage whittles down rigging, sails, and crew.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Cannon Exchange.** Gunpowder-era ship-to-ship gunnery. Ranges out to 2,000 feet, though effective fire typically within 600. See Chapter 13 for cannon statistics. A single cannonball can punch through the hull of a smaller ship with one hit; a galleon's broadside can disable a carrack in a handful of exchanges.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Archery.** Used at short range (within 150 feet). Specialized fire arrows can ignite rigging and sails — Moderate risk of fire damage per ten arrow hits on sails.
}}

### Ramming

{{CoreHanging
**Requirements.** Ramming vessel (galley or other ramed ship). Target must be approachable at full speed in a relatively straight line. Ramming ship must have 100+ feet of running room.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Ram Attack.** A contested Sailing check between the two captains determines whether the ram connects. On success, deals Devastating damage to the target's hull (often crippling or sinking smaller ships) and Heavy damage to the rammer.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Snag.** On an uncleanly-delivered ram, the two ships become locked together (rammer's prow stuck in target's hull). Both ships are now effectively grappled; boarding action becomes inevitable.
}}

### Boarding

{{CoreBody
When two ships come alongside or are locked together, boarding is the standard resolution. Marines swing, climb, or leap from one ship to another, and the fight becomes hand-to-hand across the decks.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Grappling.** Hooked lines or grappling chains thrown across the gap. Contested Strength check between crews to lash or cut the lines.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Boarding the Target.** Once ships are grappled or alongside, boarders cross via planks, ropes, or leaping. Resistance is with boarding pikes, cutlasses, and missile fire.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Melee.** A boarding action is simply tight-quarters combat (see Chapter 8) across a ship's deck. Shields matter more than Reach. Naval officers and marines favor cutlasses, boarding axes, and short spears for this reason.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Victory Conditions.** Boarding combat ends when one side strikes its colors (surrenders), all of one side are dead or disabled, or one side breaks contact (cuts grapples and retreats under fire).
}}

\column

### Specialty Weapons

{{CoreHanging
**Greek Fire Siphon.** An alchemical incendiary pumped through a bronze nozzle, catching fire on contact with air. Adheres to surfaces, including ships' hulls, and cannot be extinguished by water (worsens, in some accounts). Range 60 feet; deals Devastating fire damage on impact plus continuing damage per round. Specifics of how it works were closely-guarded historical secret; in setting, may be similarly restricted.
}}

{{CoreMetadata
Range: 60 ft · Rate of Fire: 1/2 rounds
Damage: Devastating + Heavy continuing · Ammunition: 20 charges per barrel
Cost: 1,500 gp (weapon) + 200 gp per charge · Crew: 2
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Boarding Axe.** A short, one-handed axe purpose-built for breaking ship fittings — chopping rigging, smashing locks, cutting grappling lines, and as needed, opponents.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy · Weight: 3 lbs · Bulk: 1 · Cost: 35 gp
Traits: One-handed. Slight bonus to cutting rope and cord. Brutal.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Boarding Pike.** A medium-length spear designed for the specific use of repelling boarders from a ship's deck. Shorter than a land pike for indoor/shipboard use.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy · Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 3 · Cost: 45 gp
Traits: Two-Handed. Reach (Moderate). Set for Charge (against boarders crossing). Moderate Reach penalty in below-decks combat.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Cutlass.** A short, heavy, curved single-edged sword, specifically optimized for shipboard combat.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy · Weight: 2.5 lbs · Bulk: 1 · Cost: 90 gp
Traits: One-handed, Brutal. No tight-quarters penalty (designed for it). Slight bonus in any shipboard combat.
}}

\page

## Travel Times and Navigation

{{TableTitle
Daily Travel Distances by Vessel (24-hour period, favorable conditions)
}}

| Vessel | Daily Distance |
|:--|:-:|
| Rowboat | 30 mi (8 hrs active) |
| Coastal trader | 120 mi |
| Longship | 180 mi (sail) / 90 mi (rowing) |
| Cog | 110 mi |
| Caravel | 170 mi |
| Dhow | 150 mi |
| Carrack | 130 mi |
| Galleon | 150 mi |
| War galley | 120 mi (rowing, with rest) |
| Trireme | 100 mi (beached overnight) |

{{CoreBody
Weather, currents, and navigation errors can reduce all of the above by half or more. Heavy storms halt most vessels entirely or drive them off course. A ship in an unfamiliar archipelago, navigating only by sight of coastline, may make 40 miles a day rather than 150.
}}

## Crew, Rations, and Desertion

{{CoreHanging
**Daily Rations.** A sailor needs roughly two pounds of food and a half-gallon of water per day. Long voyages require salted or preserved food; see Chapter 15.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Scurvy and Malnutrition.** Extended voyages without fresh provisions produce cumulative health problems. Moderate Endurance penalty after four weeks without fresh food; Major after eight. Fresh citrus or cabbage can prevent this at a modest weekly cost.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Crew Pay.** A sailor earns 8–15 gp per month, paid at voyage's end. Officers earn multiples of this.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Desertion.** In foreign ports, some fraction of the crew may attempt to desert. A captain with good reputation loses 1%–5% per port; poor reputation, 20%+. Extended voyages without shore leave also prompt desertion and mutiny.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Press-Ganging.** Many naval forces in war conditions forcibly recruit sailors from civilian ships and taverns. Morally objectionable, operationally common, and a persistent adventure hook.
}}

\column

## Shipbuilding and Repair

{{CoreBody
Full shipbuilding rules are a future addition to the book. A summary:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Timescale.** A cog takes 3–6 months to build; a galleon, 9–18. Specialty vessels may take longer.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Workforce.** A shipyard needs shipwrights, carpenters, smiths, sailmakers, and rope-makers working in coordination. A vessel requires dozens of skilled workers at peak construction.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Facility.** A proper shipyard is an expensive facility — a dry dock, slipways, a timber yard, rigging lofts. Construction outside a shipyard is feasible for small vessels only.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Repair.** Damage to a ship requires port facilities for anything beyond field repairs. A hull breach can be temporarily patched at sea by a skilled carpenter but will require dry-dock repair eventually. Budget 10% of a ship's value per year for routine maintenance.
}}

}}
