# Chapter 12: Siege Engines & Fortifications

{{Epigraph
*"Any castle can fall. Most take months, bad weather, treason, a plague, or an engineer's clever idea. The good ones require all five."*
}}
{{EpigraphAuthor
— Engineer Delwyn of the Royal Sappers
}}

## What This Chapter Covers

{{CoreBody
This chapter covers the engines used to attack fortified positions — rams, towers, catapults, trebuchets, ballistae, and mines — and the walls, gates, and defenses built to resist them. Firearms and gunpowder artillery are treated in Chapter 13; this chapter focuses on mechanical siege engines and the structures they contest.
}}

## Structural Damage Scale

{{CoreBody
Siege engines and fortifications use the same Damage and Durability tiers as personal weapons and armor, but at much larger numbers. A catapult that deals "Crushing × 10" damage is applying the Crushing tier ten times over — appropriate against a castle wall, irrelevant against a single soldier.
}}

{{CoreBody
Most siege engines have a **Hardness** value: damage at or below this threshold is simply ignored, representing the fact that heavy timber and stone shrug off ordinary weapons. A crossbow bolt does not chip a castle wall. A warhammer does not dent a siege ram.
}}

## Battering Rams

{{CoreHanging
**Hand Ram.** A simple log with handles, carried by eight to twelve people and swung against a door. Crude and tiring, but effective against ordinary gates.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing (against structures only) · Crew: 8–12
Durability: 100 · Weight: 400 lbs · Cost: 40 gp
Traits: Useless against gates rated for actual siege resistance.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Covered Ram (Tortoise).** A large iron-headed ram suspended in a wheeled wooden shed, the shed covered in wet hides to resist fire. The shed provides cover for the ram crew.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing × 3 · Crew: 10–20 (ram) + 8 (push)
Durability: 450 (shed) / 200 (ram) · Weight: 2,500 lbs · Cost: 800 gp
Traits: Shed provides Major cover to crew. Wet-hide covering grants Moderate fire resistance. Slow (0.5 mph on flat ground). Cannot operate on rough terrain.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Siege Ram (Great Baran).** A massive iron-headed ram suspended from a heavy timber frame, rolled up to a gate and swung on chains. The size of a small building.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing × 6 · Crew: 20–30 + 12 pushers
Durability: 800 · Weight: 8,000 lbs · Cost: 2,500 gp
Traits: Requires level approach. Specifically designed to defeat castle gates. Hardness 15 — ignores most attacks that do not themselves come from siege weapons.
}}

## Throwing Engines

{{CoreHanging
**Light Mangonel.** A traction-driven catapult — a throwing arm pulled by ropes and men. The simplest of the throwing engines. Launches stones at medium range.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy × 5 · Crew: 8–12 (pullers)
Durability: 180 · Weight: 1,500 lbs · Cost: 400 gp
Range: Medium · Rate of Fire: 1/3 rounds
Traits: Requires 8+ pullers. Fire damage possible with flammable payloads.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Onager (Torsion Catapult).** A single-armed torsion-powered engine using twisted ropes of sinew or hair for its motive force. More powerful than a mangonel; sensitive to weather.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing × 3 · Crew: 4–6
Durability: 220 · Weight: 2,200 lbs · Cost: 700 gp
Range: Medium · Rate of Fire: 1/3 rounds
Traits: Torsion ropes suffer Moderate Durability penalty in wet or freezing conditions. Must be re-tensioned after each shot.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Trebuchet (Light).** A counterweight-driven engine — a great throwing arm with a heavy weight on the short end and a sling at the long end. Uses gravity rather than ropes or torsion.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing × 6 · Crew: 8
Durability: 400 · Weight: 4,000 lbs · Cost: 1,500 gp
Range: Long · Rate of Fire: 1/4 rounds
Traits: Exceptional accuracy for the era (Slight bonus to aimed shots). Unaffected by weather (unlike torsion engines). Slow to reset; requires dedicated crew.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Heavy Trebuchet.** A large counterweight trebuchet capable of hurling heavy stones against castle walls. The siege weapon of choice in the high medieval period.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Devastating × 4 · Crew: 12–16
Durability: 700 · Weight: 12,000 lbs · Cost: 4,500 gp
Range: Long · Rate of Fire: 1/4 rounds
Traits: Requires prepared platform (cannot be moved easily). Can be built on-site from local timber in 1–2 weeks (Carpentry + Siegecraft). Launches projectiles up to 300 lbs, including stones, fire-pots, and occasionally plague-corpses.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Great Trebuchet (Warwolf-Class).** Historical examples reached extraordinary sizes, launching projectiles of half a ton or more. Almost never fielded due to cost and construction time, but when fielded, decisive.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Devastating × 8 · Crew: 20+
Durability: 1,200 · Weight: 30,000 lbs · Cost: 15,000 gp + months of construction
Range: Extreme · Rate of Fire: 1/6 rounds
Traits: Must be built on-site. Legendary accuracy once sighted; a well-trained crew can repeatedly strike a specific wall section. Immobile once built.
}}

## Shooting Engines

{{CoreHanging
**Scorpion (Bolt Thrower).** A field-portable ballista-like engine firing large bolts. Crew-served; far more accurate than throwing engines but with far less damage per shot.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Devastating (single) · Crew: 2
Durability: 150 · Weight: 400 lbs · Cost: 600 gp
Range: Long · Rate of Fire: 1/2 rounds
Traits: High accuracy. Useful against individual targets (a specific siege tower, a particular captain on a wall). Can be repositioned by crew.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Ballista.** A larger, two-horse or eight-man ballista, mounted on a carriage and capable of breaking siege engines at range.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Devastating × 2 · Crew: 4–6
Durability: 280 · Weight: 1,800 lbs · Cost: 1,800 gp
Range: Extreme · Rate of Fire: 1/3 rounds
Traits: Precise at long range. Can be used as anti-siege-engine weapon; ballistae vs. trebuchets is a classic siege counter-battle.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Polybolos (Repeating Ballista).** A theoretical Hellenistic design: a chain-driven, magazine-fed ballista. Fires rapidly but at reduced per-shot damage. Rare in practice; encountered in specific historical settings.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy × 2 · Crew: 2
Durability: 200 · Weight: 500 lbs · Cost: 1,400 gp
Range: Medium · Rate of Fire: 3/round
Traits: Magazine holds 10 bolts; reloading the magazine takes 2 rounds.
}}

## Siege Towers and Bridges

{{CoreBody
Siege towers are treated in Chapter 10 (Land Vehicles). This chapter cross-references the standard siege tower; the special-purpose variants below are worth their own entries.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Belfry (Short Siege Tower).** A compact siege tower, suitable for lower walls or narrow approaches where the standard 30-foot siege tower cannot be deployed.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Height: 15 ft · Crew: 10–15 fighters · Durability: 400
Weight: 6,000 lbs · Cost: 2,200 gp
Traits: As siege tower but smaller. Suitable for town walls and smaller castle outworks.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Scaling Bridge (Corvus).** A boarding bridge mounted on a cart or ship, used to cross from attacker to wall-top or deck-to-deck. Classical-era equivalent of a drawbridge on wheels.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Span: 25 ft · Crew: 10 attackers + 2 operators · Durability: 200
Weight: 800 lbs · Cost: 600 gp
Traits: One-shot deployment; difficult to retract once lowered. If the bridge is broken while attackers are on it, falls are Crushing damage scaled to height.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Scaling Ladders.** Simple wooden ladders, bundled by the dozen for siege assaults. Not an engine per se but essential gear.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Height: 20 ft · Crew: 1–4 climbers per ladder · Cost: 15 gp each
Traits: Vulnerable to defenders pushing the ladder backward (Challenging Strength check from defender side). A ladder falling with climbers aboard deals Crushing × 2 damage to each climber.
}}

\page

## Mining and Sapping

{{CoreBody
Siege mining is the slow, unglamorous, and utterly effective approach to breaking a fortified position: dig a tunnel beneath the wall, shore it with timber, then burn the timbers to collapse the wall above.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Sapping Tools (Set).** Picks, shovels, timber props, and wheelbarrows for a mining crew. Essential for siege sapping operations.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 200 lbs total · Cost: 80 gp
Traits: Outfitting for a mining crew of 10. Consumed as the mine progresses; props are abandoned underground.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Mining Operation.** Not an object but a process. A mining crew of 10–20 sappers can advance 5–15 feet per day through earth, less through rock. A countermining defense — defenders digging their own tunnel to intercept — can turn a mine into a subterranean battle.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Controlled Collapse.** Once the mine is beneath the wall, the crew excavates a wider chamber, shores it with timber, fills the chamber with fuel (straw, tallow, pitch), then ignites and retreats. The collapse of the tunnel above brings down the section of wall above. Routine Siegecraft check to judge timing and placement; failure may collapse the wall on the mining crew.
}}

## Fortifications

{{CoreBody
Fortifications are the defender's reply to siege engines. The entries below summarize the statistics of typical defensive structures.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Timber Palisade.** A wooden wall of sharpened logs driven into the ground. Frontier and field-fortification grade; a good day's work to build, a good day's work to burn.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Height: 10 ft · Durability: 300 per 20-ft section · Hardness: 5
Cost: 15 gp per 20-ft section (labor) + local timber
Traits: Vulnerable to fire (Moderate Durability penalty from ongoing fire damage). Can be built in a day by a large crew.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Stone Wall (Standard).** A mortared stone wall, 15–25 feet tall and three to six feet thick. The baseline medieval castle wall.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Height: 20 ft (typical) · Durability: 1,500 per 20-ft section · Hardness: 15
Cost: 150 gp per 20-ft section (materials) plus major labor
Traits: Hardness 15 means ordinary weapons do nothing. Siege engines required for meaningful damage.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Stone Wall (Thick / Castle).** The heavy curtain wall of a proper castle — 8–15 feet thick at the base, with internal passages and defensive galleries.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Height: 30 ft · Durability: 3,500 per 20-ft section · Hardness: 20
Cost: 400 gp per 20-ft section + months of labor
Traits: Hardness 20 ignores most siege weapons below heavy trebuchet. Typically has battlements, arrow slits, and murder holes.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Portcullis.** A vertical iron grating that drops to seal a gate passage. Usually paired with a wooden door behind it, creating a killing-ground between.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Durability: 600 · Hardness: 15
Cost: 300 gp · Operation: 1 winch, 2 operators
Traits: Can be rapidly dropped (1 action). Lifting takes 1 minute with proper winch.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Gate (Heavy Oak, Iron-Banded).** The main entrance of a fortification. Standard design: heavy oak, banded with iron, with viewing slot and possibly a small postern (side door) beside.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Durability: 250 · Hardness: 10
Cost: 150 gp
Traits: Backed by a bar from inside (Moderate Durability bonus while barred). Can be reinforced with additional timber during siege.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Murder Holes.** Openings in a corridor ceiling, usually above a gatehouse passage, through which defenders drop rocks, boiling oil, or missile fire on attackers below.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Durability: n/a · Cost: n/a (integrated)
Traits: Defenders above the murder holes gain Major bonus on attacks targeting characters in the corridor.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Arrow Slits.** Narrow vertical openings in walls from which archers can fire while remaining almost entirely protected.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Traits: Defender behind arrow slit gains Major cover. Major penalty to the defender's targeting arc (can only fire in a narrow cone).
}}

\page

## Defensive Curiosities

{{note
##### The Fire-Wheel

A massive wooden wheel, filled with straw, pitch, and sulfur, lit on fire and rolled down a slope toward an attacking force or siege engine camp. Used mostly in Russian and mountain defensive traditions.

**Damage:** Crushing × 3 (impact) + Heavy (fire per round) · **Durability:** 100 · **Cost:** 80 gp

**Traits:** Must be deployed on a slope of at least 10°. 30% chance per 100 yards of rolling off-course (wind, terrain). Burns out in 5 rounds. Can hit friendly forces if terrain changes mid-roll.
}}

{{note
##### The Fire-Pig

An actual pig (or several) covered in pitch and set alight, driven toward enemy siege works. A desperate tactic historically associated with defenders facing imminent defeat. Unpredictable, unreliable, and occasionally effective.

**Damage:** 3d6 (crushing) + 3d6 (fire per round that contact is maintained)

**Traits:** Animals panic unpredictably. Roll 1d8 each round for direction. 30% chance per round of running the wrong way. If it reaches a wooden siege structure, deals 3d6 fire damage per round until the pig collapses (1d4 rounds). Also: you now have to live with the fact that you set a pig on fire.
}}

{{note
##### The Wall-Crane (Archimedes' Claw)

A large hooked crane mounted on a harbor wall, designed to snag attacking ships and capsize them. Theoretical in many settings; documented in specific classical histories (the defense of Syracuse).

**Range:** 60 ft from wall · **Durability:** 400 · **Cost:** 2,500 gp + permanent emplacement

**Traits:** Operator rolls Strength at Challenging difficulty. On success, ship is grappled and can be winched — Strength contest with Strength 50 (the crane) vs. the ship's Strength (varies, large ships are Strength 20+). Winning the contest pulls the ship toward the wall or capsizes it.
}}

\column

{{note
##### Greek Fire (Wall Emplacement)

The defensive variant of the Greek Fire siphon from Chapter 11, mounted on a fortification rather than a ship. Same mechanics; used against approaching siege engines, troops, or ships attacking a harbor fortification.

Damage and cost as per Chapter 11.
}}

## Siege Logistics

{{CoreBody
A siege is less a battle than a project. The attackers must maintain their force in the field for weeks or months; the defenders must hold out longer than the attackers can hold their position. Three factors dominate.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Supply.** An army in the field consumes food at rates the local countryside cannot sustain indefinitely. Sieges stripping a region bare are common; sieges collapsing because the attackers themselves starve are also common.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Disease.** Camps of thousands in unsanitary conditions breed disease. Dysentery has killed more besieging armies than arrows. Routine Endurance checks become Challenging, then Demanding, as a siege drags on.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Morale.** Both sides. Defenders lose hope as relief fails to arrive; attackers lose patience as weeks pass without results. Treachery, defection, and negotiated surrender are all common siege endings.
}}

{{CoreBody
A defender holding out for relief may endure any deprivation for weeks. A defender convinced relief is not coming may surrender in days. Information — and disinformation — is often the most powerful siege weapon.
}}

\page

}}

}}

\page

# Chapter 13: Firearms & Artillery

{{Epigraph
*"A good bow requires a lifetime of training. A matchlock requires a week. The consequences for the longbowman's profession were considerable."*
}}
{{EpigraphAuthor
— Colonel Thracel of the Grand Army of the West
}}

## Gunpowder and the Changing Battlefield

{{CoreBody
The introduction of gunpowder weapons fundamentally changed combat. The trained archer — a decade's investment — could be replaced by a conscript with a week's training on a matchlock. Heavy plate armor, which had been the culmination of a thousand years of metallurgy, became ballast against a cheap ball of lead. The curtain wall of a castle, impervious to all but the greatest trebuchet, was cracked open by a cannon in an afternoon.
}}

{{CoreBody
This chapter covers personal firearms (hand cannons, matchlocks, wheellocks, flintlocks), cannon and field artillery, ammunition, and the specific hazards of black powder — misfires, damp matches, powder fires, and the cost of a failed shot when a failed shot can kill the shooter.
}}

## The Misfire Problem

{{CoreBody
Every black-powder weapon has a chance of malfunction on firing. Weather, maintenance, and ammunition quality all affect the rate.
}}

{{TableTitle
Misfire Rates by Era and Weapon Class
}}

| Class | Dry Conditions | Damp | Rain/Snow |
|:--|:-:|:-:|:-:|
| Hand cannon (early) | 15% | 30% | 60% |
| Matchlock | 10% | 25% | 60% |
| Wheellock | 5% | 10% | 25% |
| Flintlock | 3% | 8% | 20% |

{{CoreBody
When a firearm misfires, roll on the appropriate table below for outcome. Most misfires are dud shots; a few are catastrophic.
}}

{{TableTitle
Misfire Results (d100)
}}

| Roll | Outcome |
|:--|:--|
| 1–70 | Dud. Weapon fails to fire. Reload required. |
| 71–90 | Pan-flash / smoke. No ball fired; shooter momentarily blind (Slight penalty, 1 round). |
| 91–97 | Slow fire. Shot fires on next round instead of this one. |
| 98–99 | Barrel fouling. Weapon cannot fire until cleaned (10 minutes). |
| 100 | Barrel burst. Weapon destroyed; shooter takes Heavy damage to firing hand. |

\column

## Personal Firearms

{{CoreHanging
**Hand Cannon.** The earliest portable firearm. A short iron barrel on a wooden stock, fired by a burning match applied to a touch-hole. Inaccurate, terrifying, and surprisingly effective against heavy armor at close range.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy (Armor-Crack)
Weight: 10 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 80 gp
Range: Close · Rate of Fire: 1/4 rounds · Ammo: Lead balls + powder
Traits: Two-Handed. Armor-Crack (Moderate). Requires lit match; cannot fire without matchcord preparation (one round to light before engagement). Misfire rate 15% dry.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Arquebus / Matchlock.** A refinement of the hand cannon: a mechanical matchcord lever drops a burning match onto the pan. Longer barrel, better accuracy, standardized loads.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy (Armor-Crack)
Weight: 12 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 150 gp
Range: Short · Rate of Fire: 1/3 rounds · Ammo: Lead balls + powder
Traits: Two-Handed. Armor-Crack (Moderate). Requires lit matchcord. Misfire rate 10% dry. Can be rested on a forked stand or bardiche for accuracy (Moderate bonus).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Musket.** A larger, longer-barreled successor to the arquebus. More powerful, heavier, and usually requiring a forked rest for accurate firing.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing (Armor-Crack)
Weight: 18 lbs · Bulk: 4
Cost: 250 gp
Range: Medium · Rate of Fire: 1/3 rounds · Ammo: Lead balls + powder
Traits: Two-Handed. Armor-Crack (Major). Requires forked rest for accurate fire (Moderate penalty without). Misfire rate 10% dry.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Wheellock Pistol.** A short firearm using a wheel-and-pyrite mechanism to spark the powder, eliminating the need for a burning matchcord. A gentleman's sidearm; reliable if well-maintained.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid (Armor-Crack)
Weight: 3 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 400 gp
Range: Close · Rate of Fire: 1/3 rounds · Ammo: Lead balls + powder
Traits: One-Handed. Armor-Crack (Slight). Concealable (under cloak). Misfire rate 5% dry. Wheel mechanism is fragile — Moderate Durability penalty against rough handling.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Wheellock Carbine.** A longer two-handed wheellock, mounted more like a musket than a pistol. Favored by cavalry for its ability to fire without requiring a lit match.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy (Armor-Crack)
Weight: 8 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 650 gp
Range: Short · Rate of Fire: 1/3 rounds · Ammo: Lead balls + powder
Traits: Two-Handed. Armor-Crack (Moderate). Usable from horseback. Misfire rate 5% dry. Expensive enough that only officers and elite cavalry typically carry them.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Flintlock Pistol.** A further evolution: a flint scraped against a steel frizzen produces sparks. Simpler than the wheellock, more reliable, and vastly cheaper.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid (Armor-Crack)
Weight: 3 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 200 gp
Range: Close · Rate of Fire: 1/3 rounds · Ammo: Lead balls + powder
Traits: One-Handed. Armor-Crack (Slight). Concealable. Misfire rate 3% dry. Flint wears after 20–30 shots; replacement flint 1 gp.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Flintlock Musket.** The standard long arm of infantry in later gunpowder settings. Rifled or smoothbore variants.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing (Armor-Crack)
Weight: 10 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 300 gp
Range: Medium · Rate of Fire: 1/2 rounds · Ammo: Ball and powder
Traits: Two-Handed. Armor-Crack (Major). Misfire rate 3% dry. Can be fitted with a bayonet (see below).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Rifled Flintlock.** A flintlock musket with grooves cut into the barrel to spin the ball in flight, producing dramatically improved accuracy and range. Slower to load than a smoothbore; favored by hunters and sharpshooters.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing (Armor-Crack)
Weight: 10 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 700 gp
Range: Long · Rate of Fire: 1/4 rounds · Ammo: Rifled ball and powder
Traits: As flintlock musket but Long range instead of Medium. Moderate bonus to Called Shot accuracy. Misfire rate 3% dry.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Blunderbuss.** A short-barreled, flared-muzzle firearm that scatters multiple small pellets rather than a single ball. The "shotgun" of the pre-industrial era. Devastating at close range, useless at any distance.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy (multiple targets possible)
Weight: 7 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 180 gp
Range: Close · Rate of Fire: 1/2 rounds · Ammo: Pellets or buckshot
Traits: One- or Two-Handed. Can target up to three adjacent targets with one shot; roll separately against each. No Armor-Crack (pellets are too light).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Bayonet.** A blade that fits to the muzzle of a musket, turning the musket into a spear. Early bayonets plugged the barrel; later ring-and-socket bayonets allowed firing with the bayonet fixed.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid (when converted)
Weight: 1 lb · Bulk: 0
Cost: 25 gp
Traits: Converts flintlock/matchlock musket into a Reach (Slight) thrusting weapon. Plug bayonets (early): cannot fire while fixed. Socket bayonets (later): can fire while fixed.
}}

## Artillery

{{CoreHanging
**Hand-Cannon Wall Gun.** A large-caliber hand cannon mounted on a ship's rail or castle wall. Used for defensive shot; too heavy to carry.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing
Weight: 40 lbs (mount) · Cost: 250 gp
Range: Medium · Rate of Fire: 1/4 rounds · Crew: 2
Traits: Fixed mount. Requires 2 crew for effective operation.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Falconet (Small Cannon).** A small wheeled cannon, firing shot of approximately one pound. Field artillery for infantry support.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing × 2
Weight: 500 lbs · Cost: 800 gp
Range: Medium · Rate of Fire: 1/4 rounds · Crew: 3
Traits: Can be moved by 2 horses. Anti-personnel at close range (grapeshot: Heavy × 2 damage to all targets in a 15-foot cone at Close range).
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Falcon (Light Field Cannon).** A medium cannon firing 3–4 pound shot. Standard field artillery piece for European armies from the 16th through 18th centuries.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing × 3
Weight: 900 lbs · Cost: 1,500 gp
Range: Long · Rate of Fire: 1/4 rounds · Crew: 4
Traits: Requires 4 horses to tow. Grapeshot option (Heavy × 3 to targets in 20-foot cone at Short range).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Culverin (Heavy Field Cannon).** A long-barreled cannon firing 8–12 pound shot. The standard siege or naval cannon.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Devastating × 2
Weight: 3,500 lbs · Cost: 4,000 gp
Range: Extreme · Rate of Fire: 1/5 rounds · Crew: 6
Traits: Requires 8 horses or a permanent emplacement. Effective against fortifications at Long range (Dev × 2 damage to stone walls per shot; several shots crack a standard wall section).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Bombard (Super-Heavy Cannon).** A massive short-barreled cannon firing stone or iron balls of 50–200 pounds. A siege specialist; almost never field-deployed.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Devastating × 6
Weight: 12,000 lbs · Cost: 12,000 gp
Range: Medium · Rate of Fire: 1/10 rounds · Crew: 8–12
Traits: Permanent emplacement only — cannot be moved under field conditions. Slight chance of barrel failure on each shot (Durability check at Routine difficulty; failure means barrel splits catastrophically).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Mortar.** A short, stubby artillery piece that lobs its shells in a high arc, dropping them on targets behind fortifications. The indirect-fire specialist.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Devastating (with explosive shell) or Crushing × 3 (solid)
Weight: 2,000 lbs · Cost: 3,500 gp
Range: Long · Rate of Fire: 1/5 rounds · Crew: 4
Traits: Indirect fire — can target areas behind walls or obstacles. Explosive shells require fused ammunition (see below).
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Organ Gun (Ribauldequin).** A multi-barrel firearm designed to fire a rapid volley, historically consisting of 10 to 30 small barrels mounted in parallel on a wheeled carriage. Fires once, then requires extensive reloading.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy per barrel (10–30 barrels)
Weight: 800 lbs · Cost: 2,500 gp
Range: Short · Rate of Fire: 1 volley per 5 rounds · Crew: 3
Traits: One-shot weapon — all barrels fire in sequence (treat as area attack; roll against all targets in a 30-foot cone). Reloading all barrels takes 5 rounds of concentrated work. Impressive deterrent; unreliable performance.
}}

## Ammunition and Powder

{{CoreHanging
**Black Powder (Barrel).** A 25-lb barrel of corned black powder — enough for roughly 100 musket shots or 20 cannon shots.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 25 lbs · Cost: 15 gp per barrel
Traits: Highly flammable. A spark can detonate a full barrel, dealing Devastating damage to all targets within 20 feet. Stored powder should never be near open flame.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Musket Ball (Cartridge).** A pre-measured paper cartridge containing ball and powder, used for rapid reloading. The innovation that doubled battlefield rate of fire.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 0.1 lb each · Cost: 0.3 gp each (bulk)
Traits: Slight bonus to reload time for weapons compatible with cartridges.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Cannon Ball (Solid Shot).** Standard cannon ammunition. Iron ball ranging from 3 to 200 lbs depending on the cannon size.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: varies · Cost: 2–30 gp each
Traits: Anti-structure damage. Less effective against scattered infantry than grapeshot.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Grapeshot.** A canvas bag filled with small balls, fired from a cannon as an area-effect anti-personnel shot. Lethal at close range; ineffective beyond.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: varies · Cost: 5–15 gp per shot
Traits: Area-of-effect cone at Close-to-Short range. Damage drops from the cannon's standard tier to the next lower; compensated by hitting multiple targets.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Chain Shot.** Two cannon balls connected by a chain. Devastating against ship rigging and sails; less useful against other targets.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 2× ball · Cost: 8–15 gp per shot
Traits: Moderate bonus to rigging and sail damage. Erratic flight path (Moderate penalty to hit point-targets).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Explosive Shell.** A hollow iron ball filled with gunpowder and fitted with a slow-burning fuse. Fired from a mortar; detonates after impact, causing damage to a wider area.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: varies · Cost: 20–50 gp per shell
Traits: Delayed detonation (1–3 rounds after firing, depending on fuse). Area damage on detonation (Devastating in a 15-foot radius). Fuse reliability: 10% chance of premature detonation.
}}

## Historical Curiosities

{{note
##### The Puckle Gun

An 18th-century experimental multi-shot firearm with a rotating cylinder magazine. The Puckle Gun famously included square bullets for shooting Muslims and round bullets for shooting Christians. Impressive as an engineering curiosity, unreliable in practice, and nearly never fielded.

**Damage:** Solid (one shot per cylinder chamber; 9 chambers) · **Cost:** 2,000 gp · **Weight:** 40 lbs

**Traits:** Mounted weapon. Rotating cylinder — fires 9 shots before reload (20 minutes). Misfire rate 15% per shot.
}}

{{note
##### The Flash Bomb

A small thrown clay pot filled with black powder, a short fuse, and small metal shards. A precursor to grenades, used by sappers and assassins.

**Damage:** Heavy · **Weight:** 1.5 lbs · **Cost:** 40 gp each · **Range:** 30 ft (thrown)

**Traits:** Explodes on 2-second fuse after thrown. Shrapnel deals damage in a 10-foot radius. Handle with care — premature fuse burns have killed many thrower.
}}

{{note
##### The Flame Projector (Cheng-Huang)

A Chinese experimental weapon: a portable pump-action incendiary sprayer, functionally similar to a small Greek Fire siphon but oil-based. Used by specialist military engineers; never widely deployed.

**Damage:** Heavy (fire) + continuing fire · **Range:** 30 ft · **Weight:** 20 lbs · **Cost:** 800 gp

**Traits:** Pump requires both hands. Fuel reservoir holds 10 shots. Reservoir can be set on fire if struck by fire attack (Major damage to wielder).
}}

\column

## Fantasy Variants

{{note
##### Rune-Lock Firearm

Any firearm with a rune-bound firing mechanism instead of powder-and-priming. The weapon fires a bolt of elemental energy rather than a lead ball; damage type varies with the bound rune. Never misfires regardless of weather. Does not require powder; charges are built into the runes and recharge over 24 hours with light/moon/sun exposure as appropriate.

**Cost:** +Severe (×10) over base firearm. The mechanism itself requires Chapter 19 enchantment.
}}

{{note
##### Dragon-Forged Cannon

A heavy artillery piece cast from dragon-bone and steel, typically a culverin or bombard. The dragon-bone imparts elemental qualities to the shot — fire-drake cannons fire incendiary shot, frost-drake cannons fire shot that freezes on impact, etc.

**Cost:** +Severe over base cannon.

**Traits:** The elemental damage is an additional Heavy amount applied to the cannon's normal damage.
}}

{{note
##### Powder-Warded Arsenal

A barrel or chest of black powder treated with alchemical wards against accidental ignition. Safe from sparks and ordinary fire; only direct enchantment or alchemy can set off warded powder.

**Cost:** +Major over unwarded powder.

**Traits:** Immune to misfire-related chain explosions. Valuable for shipboard magazines and field magazines in active combat zones.
}}

## Safety, Training, and the Gunpowder Economy

{{CoreBody
Gunpowder weapons are dangerous to the wielder as well as the target. Training is shorter than with a bow but more lethal in error. The book's recommended handling:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Training.** A character proficient with gunpowder weapons can fire with the standard rules. An untrained character firing a gunpowder weapon suffers a Moderate hit penalty, doubles misfire rates, and has a Slight chance per shot of ignite-the-wrong-thing accidents.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Storage.** A barrel of black powder near an open flame, a spark, or an incendiary attack explodes violently. Serious gunpowder users maintain dedicated magazines with stone floors, sealed doors, and no metal-on-metal contact.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Supply Chains.** Black powder is produced in dedicated mills — saltpeter refining, sulfur purification, charcoal grinding, and the careful mixing of the three. A kingdom's military capacity is directly limited by its powder supply.
}}

\page

# Chapter 14: Adventuring Gear & Sundries

{{Epigraph
*"Swords kill monsters. Rope, salt, and a ten-foot pole keep you alive long enough to meet them."*
}}
{{EpigraphAuthor
— Outfitter Margerie, Salt-and-Oil Emporium, Hollowford
}}

## The Most Important Chapter

{{CoreBody
Every player remembers their sword. Nobody remembers the lamp oil that kept the sword's wielder from walking into a pit. This chapter is for the gear that never gets a song written about it: the rope, the spikes, the buckets, the torches, the small iron nails and the smaller clay jars. It is the largest single chapter in the book for a reason.
}}

## Dungeoneering Essentials

{{CoreHanging
**10-Foot Pole (Collapsible).** A wooden or iron-banded pole, jointed into three sections for portability. Used to test for pressure plates, probe murky water, and physically separate a thing from a thing without getting within that thing's reach.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 3 lbs · Bulk: 1 (collapsed) / 3 (extended)
Cost: 5 gp
Traits: Does not function as a weapon. Collapsed: fits in a belt bag. Extended: has the bulk of a spear.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Rope, Hemp (50 ft).** The universal problem-solver. Climbs up cliffs, ties up prisoners, lowers packs, bridges gaps, hangs dinner. Always bring more than you think you need.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 10 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 2 gp
Traits: Standard breaking strength (400 lbs). Rots if left wet.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Rope, Silk (50 ft).** Lighter, stronger, and dramatically more expensive. Preferred by climbers, sailors, and anyone whose life depends on a rope not breaking.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 20 gp
Traits: Breaking strength 800 lbs. Resists rot. Slight bonus to climbing checks.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Grappling Hook.** A three- or four-pronged iron hook, used to anchor rope to walls, battlements, or trees.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 4 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 4 gp
Traits: Throw distance approximately 30 feet. Routine Athletics check to set reliably.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Iron Spikes (12).** Hardened iron spikes, six to eight inches long, for jamming doors shut, creating handholds in stone, and pinning opponents' cloaks to the floor.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 2 gp per dozen
Traits: Driven with a hammer. Can be retrieved with effort (Routine Strength check).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Crowbar.** An iron pry-bar, two to three feet long, for levering open doors, crates, or anything else the party is not legally entitled to open.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 2 gp
Traits: Slight bonus to Strength checks to pry. Functions as an improvised club (Solid damage).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Sledgehammer.** A heavy two-handed hammer, for driving spikes, breaking locks, and breaking things the locks were protecting.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 10 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 6 gp
Traits: Usable as a weapon (Heavy damage, Two-Handed, Brutal). Moderate bonus to forcing doors and breaking objects.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Caltrops (Bag of 20).** Small, four-pointed iron spikes arranged so one point is always up. Scattered in a corridor or behind a retreating party to hamper pursuit.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 15 gp
Traits: A 5-foot square covered in caltrops requires anyone passing through barefoot or in cloth shoes to make a Routine Agility check; failure causes Slight damage and Moderate movement penalty for one scene.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Chalk (5 sticks).** For marking passages, leaving signs, or improvising with a bit of magic. The dungeoneer's lifeline.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 0 lbs · Bulk: 0
Cost: 1 sp per stick
Traits: Washes away in water. Visible in darkness only with light source.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**String (Spool, 100 ft).** For marking, measuring, rigging, and general problem-solving.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 1 lb · Bulk: 0
Cost: 1 sp
Traits: Breaks under 10 lbs of strain. A spool lasts weeks of frugal use.
}}

## Climbing Gear

{{CoreHanging
**Climber's Kit.** A leather harness, steel pitons, a hammer, a carabiner-style loop, and a coiled 30-foot rope. Everything a climber needs for ordinary vertical work.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 8 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 35 gp
Traits: Moderate bonus to Climbing checks. Pitons can be set into cracks for secure rope anchors.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Climbing Irons (Crampons).** Spiked iron plates strapped to boots for ice and vertical climbing.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 3 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 15 gp
Traits: Slight bonus to ice or snow traversal. Cannot be worn indoors (damages floors and slips on smooth surfaces).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Pitons (10).** Steel spikes with eyelets, hammered into rock cracks to serve as rope anchors.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 0
Cost: 1 sp each
Traits: Single-use under load; retrievable with effort.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Grappling Arrow.** An arrow with a small folding hook at the head, designed to catch on a ledge or window. Fired from a heavy bow; returns with attached line.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 0.5 lb · Bulk: 0
Cost: 10 gp
Traits: Trailed line of 50 feet (standard). Requires a good set against an edge.
}}

\column

## Light Sources

{{CoreHanging
**Torch.** A pitch-soaked length of wood or bundled rushes. Burns for one hour, casting bright light in a 30-foot radius.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 1 lb · Bulk: 0
Cost: 1 cp
Traits: Requires one hand. Can function as an improvised club (Solid damage) or fire attack (Slight fire damage on touch). Extinguishes in water or strong wind.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Oil Lamp.** A small pottery or brass lamp burning lamp oil. 4-hour burn per flask of oil. Light radius: 15 feet bright, 15 feet dim.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 1 lb · Bulk: 0
Cost: 5 sp (lamp) + 5 sp per flask of oil
Traits: Extinguishes if knocked over. Fragile — drops may shatter.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Hooded Lantern.** A metal lantern with a hinged shutter, protecting the flame from wind and allowing it to be dimmed or directed. Standard dungeoneering light.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 7 gp
Traits: Burns 6 hours per flask of oil. Light radius: 30 feet bright. Shutter can reduce to 5-foot radius for stealth.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Bullseye Lantern.** A lantern with a mirrored reflector that focuses light into a 60-foot cone. Indispensable for dungeoneering; limited peripheral vision is a fair trade for the extended reach.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 3 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 12 gp
Traits: Cone of light 60 feet long, 20 feet wide. Shutter function present. 6 hours per flask.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Candle.** A wax or tallow candle. Short-burn, local light, no wind resistance.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 0 lbs · Bulk: 0
Cost: 1 cp (tallow) / 1 sp (wax)
Traits: 1 hour per candle. 5-foot bright radius. Fragile in any air movement.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Flask of Oil.** Refined lamp oil for lamps and lanterns. Can also be spilled and ignited as improvised incendiary.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 1 lb · Bulk: 0
Cost: 1 sp
Traits: When thrown at a target and ignited: Heavy fire damage at impact; floor catches fire in a 5-foot radius for 2 rounds.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Sun-Rod.** An alchemical glass tube that, when cracked, glows for 6 hours. Waterproof; usable underwater or in rain. See Chapter 16 for full alchemical entry.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 1 lb · Bulk: 0
Cost: 25 gp
Traits: 6-hour bright light, 30-foot radius. Immersion-safe. Cannot be extinguished once cracked; breaks down after its duration.
}}

## Containers

{{CoreHanging
**Belt Pouch.** A small leather pouch tied at the belt. Standard carrying for coins, small items, and everyday necessities.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: ~2 lbs · Bulk: 0
Cost: 5 sp
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Bandolier.** A cloth-and-leather strap worn across the chest, with pre-formed pockets for specific items — usually alchemical flasks, pistol charges, or throwing daggers.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 8 small items · Bulk: 0
Cost: 2 gp
Traits: Slight bonus to Quick Draw for items carried in the bandolier.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Backpack.** The adventurer's standard. Canvas-and-leather, shoulder-strapped, with one main compartment and typically two external pockets.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 40 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 2 gp
Traits: Wear reduces carrying capacity slightly. Cannot be quickly dropped (one action).
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Pack Frame (Reinforced).** A wooden-framed backpack for heavy loads — traveling merchants, teamsters, hunters bringing game home.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 80 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 15 gp
Traits: Distributes weight over shoulders and hips. Moderate penalty to Agility while worn fully loaded.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Saddlebags (Pair).** Two canvas bags that hang across a horse's back, for travel gear and mid-range cargo.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 30 lbs per side (60 total) · Bulk: 2
Cost: 4 gp
Traits: Does not add to rider's encumbrance; adds to the horse's.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Waterskin.** A sewn leather bladder holding 2 quarts of water or other liquid. The universal portable container.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 2 quarts · Weight: 4 lbs (full) / 0.5 lb (empty)
Cost: 2 sp
Traits: Leaks if pressure-pierced.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Water Flask.** A metal or ceramic flask for liquids, pinchable at the neck and stoppered. Smaller than a waterskin; sturdier.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 1 pint · Weight: 1 lb (full)
Cost: 2 sp
Traits: Rigid; does not collapse when drained.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Barrel (Small, 10 Gallons).** A wooden barrel for water, wine, salt pork, or anything else that travels well in bulk.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 10 gal (~85 lbs water) · Weight: 15 lbs (empty)
Cost: 3 gp
Traits: Must be properly sealed. Leaky barrels ruin their cargo.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Chest (Wooden, Locking).** A wood-and-iron chest with an attached lock. Standard storage for treasure, tools, or documents.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Capacity: 2 cubic feet · Weight: 25 lbs
Cost: 8 gp
Traits: Standard lock (Routine lockpick). Carried by two people.
}}

\page

## Tools

{{CoreHanging
**Lockpicks (Set).** A small leather roll containing 6–10 lockpicking tools.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 1 lb · Bulk: 0
Cost: 30 gp
Traits: Required for most lockpicking attempts. Slight bonus with masterwork picks (×3 cost).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Thieves' Tools (Full Kit).** A broader kit including lockpicks, trap-disarming tools, mirror on a rod, and small saws. The professional's loadout.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 75 gp
Traits: Slight bonus to any Lockpicking or trap-related check.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Smith's Tools (Portable).** Hammer, tongs, files, small anvil. Used for field repair of metal gear.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 12 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 50 gp
Traits: Enables field repairs per Chapter 3. Inadequate for building new items; portable only.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Carpentry Tools (Basic).** Saw, planes, chisels, hand-drill, hammer, measure.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 15 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 40 gp
Traits: Enables field woodworking. Suitable for repairs or small construction.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Herbalism Kit.** Pouches of herbs, small mortar, drying racks, measuring scoops.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 20 gp
Traits: Required for many Herbalism checks. Consumable components replenish as the character travels through wilderness.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Alchemy Kit (Basic).** A portable version of an alchemist's workspace — small vials, a tiny alembic, flasks, measures, a portable stove.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 8 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 75 gp
Traits: Enables basic alchemical work. See Chapter 16 for full rules.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Surveyor's Tools.** Chain, measuring rod, plumb, astrolabe or sextant for celestial navigation, notebook.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 10 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 120 gp
Traits: Moderate bonus to mapping and surveying checks. Essential for building projects.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Writing Kit.** Quills, ink, sealing wax, small knife, paper or parchment, blotter. A minor luxury for ordinary travelers, a necessity for scribes, priests, and scholars.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 15 gp
Traits: Ink and paper are consumables. Scroll tubes (separate) protect documents on the road.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Mason's Tools.** Chisels, hammer, line, trowel, measuring rods.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 12 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 35 gp
Traits: Enables stonework checks. Required for any serious construction involving mortared stone.
}}

## Fire and Cooking

{{CoreHanging
**Flint and Steel.** A steel striker and a flint. The standard fire-starter.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 0 lbs · Bulk: 0
Cost: 2 gp
Traits: Routine check to start a fire with dry tinder; Challenging in wet conditions.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Tinderbox.** A small box containing steel striker, flint, char cloth, and dry tinder. Easier than bare flint-and-steel; easier to keep dry.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 1 lb · Bulk: 0
Cost: 3 gp
Traits: Trivial check to start a fire in dry conditions; Routine in damp.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Cooking Pot.** A simple iron pot with a handle, suitable for boiling stews, soups, or water.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 4 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 1 gp
Traits: Standard camp-cook equipment. See Chapter 15 for cooking mechanics.
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Mess Kit.** A nested set of small pots, plates, and utensils for one traveler.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 2 lbs · Bulk: 1
Cost: 8 sp
Traits: Complete individual dining equipment. Rattles (Slight Stealth penalty when carried openly).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Portable Stove.** A small iron brazier or stove for controlled heating, especially useful for alchemy or fine cooking on the road.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 8 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 20 gp
Traits: Enables controlled-temperature work. Useful for Alchemy and Cooking skill checks requiring precision.
}}

## Specialty and Odd Gear

{{CoreHanging
**Whistle (Signal).** A small brass or bone whistle for battlefield signals, dog commands, or summoning aid.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 0 lbs · Bulk: 0
Cost: 5 sp
Traits: Audible 500 feet in still air.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Mirror (Small Polished).** A small steel or brass hand-mirror for signaling, checking around corners, or personal grooming.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 0 lbs · Bulk: 0
Cost: 5 gp
Traits: Can signal across several miles in direct sunlight.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Telescope (Early Form).** A hand-held telescope or "spyglass" — available in settings with glassworking sufficient for lenses.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Weight: 1 lb · Bulk: 0
Cost: 200 gp
Traits: Slight bonus to Perception at medium-to-long ranges. Fragile — Durability check on drops.
