# Part V: Contests & Enchantment

{{vignette
<!-- VIGNETTE GOES HERE -->
}}

\page

# Chapter 18: The Tournament

{{Epigraph
*"A tournament is a war with rules. A war is a tournament without them. A smart man does both for money, though he prefers the one with rules because the dead bodies afterward are less personal."*
}}
{{EpigraphAuthor
— Sir Havrick of the Fox Banner
}}

## The Tournament as Institution

{{CoreBody
Tournaments existed for four overlapping reasons: training for war, display of social rank, redistribution of wealth among the nobility, and entertainment. Most tournaments served all four. A serious tourney could make a landless knight wealthy in an afternoon — or ruin him for a decade. A ceremonial one could settle a marriage, a peace treaty, or a score between families.
}}

{{CoreBody
This chapter covers the formal events of the tournament (joust, melee, foot combat, archery, tilt), the special gear used at them, the ransom economics that drive the business, and the social and mechanical stakes for a player character who enters. Tournament plate armor is cross-referenced here from Chapter 4.
}}

## Tournament Events

### The Joust

{{CoreBody
Two mounted knights charge each other along a tilt-yard separated by a barrier. Each carries a lance. The goal is to unseat the other, break one's own lance cleanly on the other's shield or helm, or accumulate more points over three passes.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Pass.** Each pass is one charge at the opposing knight. The knights ride at full gallop, aiming their lances at each other's shields or helms. At the moment of contact, each makes a Riding check at Challenging difficulty, modified by armor fit, mount quality, and specific tournament rules.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Outcomes of a Pass.**
}}
{{HangingBullet
- **Clean Break.** One knight's lance breaks cleanly on the other's shield. 2 points.
- **Shattered Lance.** Lance splinters dramatically, often with crown-shaped tips. 3 points.
- **Unhorsed.** Target falls from the saddle. 5 points (and often ends the match).
- **Miss.** Both lances glance off; no score.
- **Foul Strike.** Hit to an illegal target (head when head-strikes forbidden; beneath the belt); judge's discretion; may cost points.
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Three Passes Standard.** Most jousts are three passes. The knight with the most points wins. Ties are rejoined or decided by judges.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Unhorsing.** A full unhorsing ends the match with the unhorsed knight disqualified, regardless of accumulated points. A knight who falls but lands standing may occasionally continue; this is a judge's call.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Injury.** Even with blunted lances, jousting is dangerous. Each pass with contact carries a Slight risk of injury to the recipient; a fall from a gallop carries Moderate damage risk. Serious injuries are common; deaths are rare but not unknown.
}}

### The Melee (Tournament Grand Melee)

{{CoreBody
A mass combat between two teams of knights, either mounted or on foot, with blunted weapons. The melee is closer to a controlled battle than a sport — it was the training ground that produced most of the great knights of medieval history.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Teams.** Usually 20-100 knights per side. Teams are often formed by region, liege-lord, or specific sponsorship.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Rules.** Blunted weapons only. Specific zones off-limits (no strikes to the horse; no killing blows). Prisoners are taken for ransom rather than killed.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Victory Conditions.** The team with more unhorsed or surrendered opponents after a set time wins. Individual glory is separately tracked — the knight with the most personal captures earns renown beyond the team result.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Ransom.** A knight captured in the melee becomes the captor's prisoner until ransomed. Ransom terms are negotiated either on the spot (a knight pays his own way out) or after the tourney (from his lord or family). See Ransom Economics below.
}}

\page

### Foot Combat

{{CoreHanging
**Single Combat Afoot.** Two knights, each in tournament plate (see Chapter 4), with blunted weapons, fight across a barrier or in an enclosed list. Usually a scheduled match with clear rules.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Weapons.** Traditionally poleaxe (the knight's tournament weapon par excellence), though arming swords, maces, and occasionally falchions are also used.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Rules.** Strike limits (e.g., "three clean blows"), no thrust to unarmored zones, specific stopping conditions.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Victory.** Most foot combats are scored — three clean blows, or the first knight to yield, or the judge's call at time-out.
}}

### Archery

{{CoreBody
Archery contests are commonly held at the same events as the knightly contests, drawing a separate class of competitors (yeoman archers, soldiers, mercenaries). They are rarely prestigious but often lucrative.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Standard Target Round.** Three arrows per round, at ranges of 30, 60, and 100 yards. Score based on distance from center.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Wand Round.** Arrows fired at a narrow willow wand at 30 yards. Cleaving the wand wins.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Clout Round.** Long-range shooting at a flag on the ground at 200-300 yards. Hits measured by closest arrow.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Prizes.** Money purses, fine bows, and occasionally the attention of noble patrons looking to hire good shots. An archer's victory rarely brings ransom-class wealth but can lead to long-term employment.
}}

### Swordplay and Fencing

{{CoreHanging
**Formal Fencing Tournament.** A lighter-armored event, common in courtly settings. Contestants use blunted swords and score by touches on specific target zones.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**School-Style Bouts.** Fencing schools compete formally. Sometimes an element of tournament days; sometimes a separate weekly event at fencing guildhalls.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Duels of Honor.** A specific kind of match held either at tournaments or in arranged settings. Rules vary by setting — some are to first blood, some to incapacitation, some to the death. Legal status varies widely.
}}

\column

## Tournament Gear

### Tournament Armor

{{CoreBody
See Chapter 4 for Tournament Plate. Key points:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Tournament Plate.** Heavier than war plate (75 lbs, Bulk 4). Reinforced against lance and mace impacts. Protection Extreme (with specific Devastating threshold against lance charges). Severe Agility penalty outside of mounted use. Cost: 6,000 gp.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Ceremonial Plate.** A lighter, more visually impressive variant of tournament plate, often gilded or engraved. Same Protection tier as standard tournament plate but with Moderate Presence bonus. Cost: 10,000+ gp.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Barding, Tournament.** Full articulated barding specific to the joust (see Chapter 9). Cost: 5,000 gp for standard; 15,000 gp for ornamental.
}}

### Tournament Weapons

{{CoreHanging
**Jousting Lance.** Blunted, crown-tipped, fragile by design. Fragments on impact to reduce damage to the recipient. See Chapter 9.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Solid for unseating purposes; no lethal damage
Cost: 90 gp (disposable)
Traits: Always breaks on impact. Single-use.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Blunted Weapons (Swords, Maces, Axes).** Any weapon can be fitted with a tournament-legal blunted version. Metal weapons are struck with rebated edges; blades have rounded tips.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: One tier below the normal weapon's damage tier (reducing lethal outcome)
Cost: ×1.5 of normal weapon cost (Slight cost bonus)
Traits: Not actually safe — still deals real damage, just much less. Retains all weapon traits (Reach, Balanced, etc.).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Poleaxe, Tournament.** The knight's signature tournament weapon. Blunted but otherwise identical to the war version.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Crushing (blunted version: Heavy)
Weight: 5 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 330 gp (+50% over war poleaxe)
Traits: Two-Handed, Reach (Slight), Balanced.
}}

\page

## Ransom Economics

{{CoreBody
The heart of the tournament economy. When a knight is captured or unhorsed and yields, he becomes the victor's ransom-prisoner. Ransom payment secures his release; the victor keeps the payment as the victory's substantive prize.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Standard Ransom.** By tradition, a captured knight's ransom equals one year's income from his lands. For landless knights-errant, ransom is typically 500–1,500 gp, paid by a sponsor if one is willing.
}}

{{TableTitle
Typical Ransom by Rank
}}

| Rank | Ransom |
|:--|:-:|
| Squire | 200 gp |
| Landless knight | 500–1,500 gp |
| Minor noble / knight-with-fief | 3,000–8,000 gp |
| Baron | 15,000–30,000 gp |
| Count / Count's heir | 50,000–100,000 gp |
| Duke / Royal | 100,000+ gp |

{{CoreHanging
**Ransom Payment.** Paid in coin, land, valuable goods, or — in extreme cases — hostages. Payment secures the prisoner's release.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Knight's Honor and Parole.** A captured knight traditionally pledges not to escape; in exchange, he is treated as an honored guest rather than a prisoner. Breaking this parole is a serious dishonor.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Tournament Circuit.** A knight who repeatedly captures others (and avoids capture himself) on the tournament circuit can accumulate substantial wealth. Historical knights who made careers of this were not uncommon.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Bankruptcy by Capture.** A knight captured without means to pay his own ransom becomes his captor's burden — housed, fed, possibly used for labor until ransom arrives. Extended captivity was a real consequence of losing at tournament.
}}

\column

## Social Stakes

{{CoreBody
A tournament is a public event. A knight who performs well gains fame, sponsorship offers, romantic attention, and sometimes lands. A knight who performs badly gains all the same things in their negative versions.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Fame and Renown.** Each victory at a notable tournament raises the knight's social standing by a Slight degree. Consistent victory over years can promote a landless knight into serious noble company.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Sponsors.** Nobles and wealthy merchants sponsor promising knights, funding their armor, horses, and entry fees in exchange for a share of ransom winnings. A knight with a sponsor has resources; a knight without one is funding his own career.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Favor of the Field.** A knight may wear the favor (a token — ribbon, scarf, glove) of a lady. Winning while wearing her favor is a socially significant outcome. Losing while wearing her favor is also socially significant, in the other direction.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The King's Pleasure.** A tournament held by a monarch may result in royal notice. Knights who perform well at royal tourneys gain opportunities not otherwise available — court positions, commissions, gifts of land.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Hard Feelings.** Tournaments sometimes produce lasting enmities. A knight humiliated in the lists may seek rematches, bring legal complaints, or simply avoid certain jousts. Player characters may use tournaments to pursue rivalries that have nothing to do with combat.
}}

\page

## Prize Structure

{{CoreBody
A tournament has multiple prize categories. The knightly events are the most prestigious and the most valuable; archery and fencing are profitable but lower-status.
}}

{{TableTitle
Typical Tournament Prizes
}}

| Event | Top Prize | Second | Third |
|:--|:-:|:-:|:-:|
| Grand Joust | 2,000 gp + accolade | 500 gp | 200 gp |
| Grand Melee (team) | 5,000 gp + shares of ransom | 1,500 gp (team) | 500 gp |
| Melee (individual glory) | Best knight: 500 gp | 200 gp | 100 gp |
| Foot Combat | 300 gp | 100 gp | 50 gp |
| Archery (combined) | 100 gp + quality bow | 40 gp | 15 gp |
| Fencing | 80 gp | 30 gp | 10 gp |
| Ladies' Champion (voted) | 200 gp + favor | 50 gp | — |

{{CoreBody
These are baseline prizes for a respectable regional tournament. Royal tournaments may offer 10× these amounts; minor local events, a quarter.
}}

## Entering a Tournament

{{CoreBody
Entry requirements vary by tournament and event.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Entry Fee.** Usually 10–50 gp for a knight entering the joust; higher for the grand melee. Archery and fencing entries are typically 1–5 gp.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Qualifications.** Most formal tourneys require knightly status for the lance events. Others are open to anyone who can demonstrate adequate gear and skill.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Gear Inspection.** All weapons and armor are inspected by tourney marshals. Weapons that are insufficiently blunted, armor that is inappropriately fitted, and equipment with hidden blades are confiscated and the bearer disqualified.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Heralds.** Each tournament has heralds who announce combatants, record outcomes, and adjudicate disputes. A herald's ruling is usually final.
}}

\column

## Specialty Events and Variants

{{CoreHanging
**The Behourd.** A practice tournament, lower-stakes, held frequently in training seasons. Typically features blunted weapons and low ransoms; more a training exercise than a prestige event.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Pas d'Armes.** A tournament where a knight (or small group) challenges all comers at a specific location over a set number of days. Often themed — "the Knight of the Fountain," "the Lady's Champion" — and with elaborate romantic trappings.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Trial by Combat.** A legal proceeding in which a dispute is settled by duel between the claimants or their champions. Not strictly a tournament but often held at tournament venues. See Chapter 8 for general dueling rules.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Grand Tournament of Nations.** A large-scale event drawing knights from multiple kingdoms. Rare; highly political; often marks major diplomatic occasions (coronations, peace treaties, royal marriages).
}}

## Tournament Injuries and Recovery

{{CoreBody
Even with blunted weapons and strict rules, tournaments are dangerous. Injuries are common; deaths occur. The book's guidance for player characters:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Blunted Damage.** Most tournament combat uses blunted weapons at one tier below lethal damage. A blunted greatsword deals Heavy damage instead of Crushing, which is survivable for most armored knights.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Fall Damage.** Falls from horseback at gallop deal Heavy damage scaled by armor weight. Tournament plate reduces this (cushioning, designed fall zones) but does not eliminate it.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Recovery.** Most tournament injuries are bruises, strains, and broken bones — painful but not lethal. Recovery takes days to weeks. Serious injuries (concussions, internal damage, spinal injuries) may take months or be permanent.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Tournament Medics.** A major tourney retains surgeons, bonesetters, and an apothecary. Their services are usually included in the event's common pool; a knight who needs serious care generally receives it.
}}

\page

## Summary Tables

{{TableTitle
Tournament Events
}}

| Event | Gear | Scoring | Stakes |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| Grand Joust | Tournament plate, jousting lance | Unseat / break lance / crown | Fame, ransom, prize |
| Grand Melee | Tournament plate, blunted weapons | Last team standing, individual glory | Ransom, team prize |
| Foot Combat | Tournament plate, poleaxe/sword | Clean blows, yield, time | Prize, reputation |
| Archery | Bow, standard arrows | Score vs. distance | Prize, patron eye |
| Fencing | Light armor, blunt blade | Touches on target zones | Prize, skill prestige |
| Duel of Honor | Agreed equipment | First blood / yield / death | Settles dispute |

{{TableTitle
Blunted Weapon Cost Modifier
}}

| Weapon | Standard Cost | Blunted |
|:--|:-:|:-:|
| Longsword | 90 gp | 135 gp (+Slight) |
| Greatsword | 200 gp | 300 gp |
| Mace | 35 gp | 55 gp |
| Poleaxe | 220 gp | 330 gp |
| Jousting lance | — | 90 gp (disposable) |

{{TableTitle
Ransom Reference
}}

| Captive | Typical Ransom |
|:--|:-:|
| Squire | 200 gp |
| Landless knight | 500–1,500 gp |
| Minor noble | 3,000–8,000 gp |
| Baron | 15,000–30,000 gp |
| Count | 50,000–100,000 gp |
| Duke / royal | 100,000+ gp |

{{pageNumber,auto}}
{{footnote
The Ultimate Provisioner's Handbook — Chapter 18: The Tournament

v0.1 working draft
}}

\page

# Chapter 19: Enchantment & Legendary Craft

{{Epigraph
*"A blade is not born legendary. It is made legendary by the hand that forges it, the rune that wakes it, and the hundred men who fall before it. Skip any step and the blade is just metal."*
}}
{{EpigraphAuthor
— Rune-Master Ingrid of the Deep Forge
}}

## A Framework, Not a Catalog

{{CoreBody
This chapter does not provide a list of named enchanted items. It provides the framework by which enchantment happens — how runes are bound, how infusions are worked, how legendary items are constructed — so that a GM can build unique named items that fit their setting rather than importing a generic catalog.
}}

{{CoreBody
The framework is deliberately modular. A GM running a low-magic campaign can use only rune-binding (which is itself a rare and difficult craft). A GM running high-fantasy can pile enchantments, masterwork status, and exotic materials into a single blade until it becomes the sort of thing songs are written about.
}}

## Three Layers of Enchantment

{{CoreBody
The book distinguishes three separate ways an item can become more than mundane. They stack; a single item can carry all three.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Material-Based.** Certain materials (Chapter 2) are inherently extraordinary. A dragon-bone sword carries the elemental affinity of its source without any further enchantment. The enchantment is the material itself, and it does not require a spellcaster to produce.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Infusion.** A process that modifies an item's properties through prolonged alchemical treatment and magical work. Weightless plate, everburning torches, self-cleaning robes — these are infusions. Infusions are permanent but non-specific: they change a property of the item rather than granting an active effect.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Rune-Binding.** The binding of a specific effect-carrying rune to an item. Rune-binding creates the characteristic "magic sword" effect — a blade that strikes fire, or glows in the dark, or drinks the blood of its victims. Runes are specific, named, and limited in number per item.
}}

\column

## Material-Based Enchantment

{{CoreBody
Chapter 2's fantasy materials already carry their effects; this section describes what happens when multiple fantasy materials are combined in a single item, and when a masterwork item is crafted from a fantasy material.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Combined Materials.** A weapon built with both a dragon-bone core and silver-inlaid edge gains the effects of both (elemental affinity from the bone; silver-tier supernatural bypass from the edge). Cost multiplies: a Severe material cost bonus + a Major material cost bonus stacks to approximately Severe+ (×15).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Masterwork + Fantasy.** A masterwork item (Chapter 3) forged from a fantasy material carries both sets of bonuses. Masterwork Dragon-Bone sword: Slight damage bonus (masterwork) + Slight damage bonus (dragon-bone) = Moderate damage bonus, plus elemental affinity, plus Durability shifts.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Sourcing.** Fantasy materials are not available at markets. Sourcing dragon-bone requires hunting a dragon, buying black-market relic bone, or inheriting it. Sourcing moon-silver requires elven contacts or ancient ruins. These are adventure hooks more than shopping trips.
}}

## Infusion

{{CoreBody
An infusion is a permanent modification to an item's basic properties, worked through weeks of alchemical and magical process. Infusions change what the item is without granting an active magical effect.
}}

### Common Infusions

{{CoreHanging
**Weightless Infusion.** The item weighs half its normal amount. Most commonly applied to heavy armor to reduce Agility penalties.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Time: 30 days
Cost: Major cost bonus (×3) over the base item
Requirement: Master-level alchemist + skilled enchanter
}}

\page

{{CoreHanging
**Everburning Infusion.** An item (typically a torch, lamp, or lantern) burns indefinitely without fuel. Cannot be quenched except by specific means (water, or a particular counter-magic).
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Time: 20 days
Cost: 500 gp
Requirement: Skilled enchanter
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Self-Repair Infusion.** The item mends minor damage over time, recovering Slight Durability per day at rest.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Time: 45 days
Cost: Major cost bonus over the base item
Requirement: Master-level enchanter
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Climate-Proof Infusion.** The item resists environmental wear — rust, cold brittleness, heat deformation. Suitable for sea-travel gear, arctic exploration, or volcanic expeditions.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Time: 14 days
Cost: 300 gp
Requirement: Skilled enchanter
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Self-Cleaning Infusion.** The item sheds blood, dirt, grease, and other stains as if freshly washed. Common on noble garments and ceremonial gear.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Time: 10 days
Cost: 200 gp
Requirement: Apprentice-level enchanter
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Silent Infusion.** The item makes no sound during normal use. Useful for armor (Moderate Stealth bonus), wagon wheels, horse barding.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Time: 21 days
Cost: Moderate cost bonus over the base item
Requirement: Skilled enchanter
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Waterproof Infusion.** The item repels water completely — rain slides off, submersion does not soak through. Useful for books, scrolls, clothing.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Time: 10 days
Cost: 150 gp
Requirement: Apprentice-level enchanter
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**Ageless Infusion.** The item does not wear with use or time. No rust, no rot, no fading. Common in religious and heirloom items.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Time: 90 days
Cost: Severe cost bonus (×10) over the base item
Requirement: Master-level enchanter
}}

### Infusion Rules

{{CoreHanging
**One Infusion Per Item.** An item can carry only one infusion. A weightless-infused sword cannot also be self-repairing.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Setting-Specific Requirements.** Infusions are specific to the magical traditions of a setting. Some settings require specific materials (moon-silver strands, dragon-blood, phoenix feather); others require specific practitioners (elven, dwarven, druidic). GMs should adapt freely.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Stacking with Masterwork.** Infusions stack freely with masterwork construction. A masterwork weightless breastplate is a 6,000 gp item (base full plate 3,000 gp × 3 for Major cost bonus = 9,000; minus masterwork adjustments; setting-specific refinement).
}}

## Rune-Binding

{{CoreBody
Runes are the most active form of enchantment. A bound rune grants a specific magical effect that triggers under defined conditions. Runes are setting-specific in their lore; this chapter provides the framework and sample effects.
}}

### How Rune-Binding Works

{{CoreBody
Rune-binding requires three things: a suitable item, a rune-stone or rune-formula, and a rune-binder with the appropriate skill.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Suitable Item.** The item must be of Standard quality or better. An ordinary mass-produced blade cannot hold a rune; a masterwork or fantasy-material blade easily can. Tier determines the number of runes the item can carry:
}}

\page

{{TableTitle
Rune Capacity by Item Tier
}}

| Item Tier | Rune Capacity |
|:--|:-:|
| Standard | 1 rune |
| Masterwork | 2 runes |
| Fantasy-Material (standard) | 2 runes |
| Fantasy-Material (masterwork) | 3 runes |
| Legendary (narrative) | Unlimited (GM's call) |

{{CoreHanging
**Rune-Stone or Formula.** Pre-carved stones that transfer their effect to the item; or formulas that must be worked directly onto the item. Rune-stones are a rare trade good; formulas are closely-guarded master-class knowledge.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Rune-Binder.** A character with Rune-Binding skill at Journeyman level or higher. Binding is a weeks-to-months process; attempts by unskilled practitioners typically fail and often destroy the rune-stone.
}}

### Rune-Binding as a Crafting Project

{{CoreBody
Rune-binding follows Chapter 3's crafting framework with specific adjustments:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Time.** Simple runes: 1 week. Complex runes: 1 month. Legendary runes: 3+ months.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Cost.** Rune-stones cost Major-to-Severe cost bonuses over the base item. The binding labor itself costs roughly 10-25% of the rune-stone value.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Instability Check.** After the binding labor, the binder makes an Enchantment check at difficulty matching the rune's complexity. Critical failure destroys both the rune-stone and potentially the item itself.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Fueling.** Some runes require ongoing "fuel" — a daily meditation, an occasional blood offering, exposure to moonlight. Others are self-sustaining.
}}

\column

### Sample Rune Effects

{{CoreBody
The sample effects below are meant as inspiration; GMs should adapt them and invent their own.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Rune of Sharpness.** Slight damage bonus to all strikes. Simple rune, common in weapon-heavy settings.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Rune of the True Path (Accuracy).** Slight bonus to hit on all attack rolls. Simple.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Rune of Light.** The item sheds a 20-foot dim light radius. Can be activated or suppressed by a command word. Simple.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Rune of Flame.** On a successful hit, weapon adds Slight fire damage. Medium complexity.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Rune of Frost.** On a successful hit, weapon adds Slight cold damage. Medium complexity.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Rune of Storm.** On a successful hit, weapon adds Slight lightning damage, with a chance to stun. Medium complexity.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Rune of Binding.** On a successful hit, target must save vs. Willpower or be slowed for one round. Complex.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Rune of the Undead Bane.** Slight additional damage to undead creatures; may repel specific undead types on critical. Complex.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Rune of the Demon Bane.** Slight additional damage to infernal creatures; similar special effects. Complex.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Rune of Armor-Rending.** Moderate Armor-Crack bonus in addition to the weapon's natural effects. Complex.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Rune of Swiftness.** Wearer or wielder gains Slight Initiative bonus while item is carried. Medium.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Rune of the Guardian.** Wearer gains Slight Protection bonus while wearing the item. Common on shields and amulets. Medium.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Rune of Healing.** Wearer recovers Slight Durability per day of wearing the item. Complex.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Rune of Invisibility.** Wielder can activate once per day for one minute of invisibility. Legendary.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Rune of the Slayer.** Massive damage against a specific creature type, specified at binding. Legendary; typically requires a blood-bond sacrifice.
}}

\page

## Constructing a Legendary Item

{{CoreBody
A legendary item in this framework is the intersection of material, masterwork, multiple runes, and story. The book does not provide a name for such items; the GM and players construct them together.
}}

### The Framework

{{CoreHanging
**Start with a base item.** A longsword, a breastplate, a shield — whatever form the legend takes.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Choose a material.** Dragon-bone, moon-silver, star-silver, dark-iron. The material defines the item's inherent properties and cultural associations.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Forge it masterwork.** This requires a master smith. The cost is substantial — 3× the fantasy-material cost, plus months of labor.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Apply infusions.** Usually one infusion that defines the item's character — weightless plate, ageless sword, silent armor.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Bind runes.** Usually two or three runes that give the item its combat properties. The specific runes tell the item's story: a weapon built to hunt demons carries Demon Bane + Flame + Accuracy; a guardian's blade carries Guardian + Healing + True Path.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Give it a history.** A legendary item has a name, a first wielder, a defining moment. These are narrative details, but they matter mechanically: legendary items often have story-driven properties (returns to its family, refuses a cruel master, appears only in certain phases of the moon) that exist outside the stat block.
}}

### A Worked Example: The Blade "Ashbane"

{{CoreBody
Illustrating the framework, here is a specific legendary sword built from first principles.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Base.** Longsword (Damage Heavy, 90 gp).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Material.** Dragon-bone from a fire-drake slain by the first wielder (gains elemental fire affinity, Slight damage bonus, Moderate weight reduction, Severe cost — +5,400 gp over base).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Masterwork.** Forged by a master smith of an elven clan over six months (gains Slight damage and Slight attack bonuses, Durability upgraded one tier — +2,700 gp for masterwork of fantasy material).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Infusion.** Silent infusion (sheathed, the blade makes no sound, even the sheath straps — Moderate cost bonus, 1,800 gp).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Runes.** Rune of Flame (bonded to the dragon-bone's natural fire affinity, amplifying it — adds Moderate fire damage on hit, 2,000 gp); Rune of the Demon Bane (forged to hunt the infernal entity that killed the smith's daughter — grants Slight bonus vs. demons, repels lesser demons on critical, 3,500 gp).
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**History.** The sword was first wielded by Sir Kael of the Red Moor, who used it to kill the demon Yarrowhold. It passed to his daughter, then his granddaughter, then vanished from the records during the sack of the Red Moor Keep. It appears once every two or three generations in the hands of a descendant of Kael; it has never stayed with any other wielder for long.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Total Stats.** Damage: Crushing (Heavy + 2 Slight bonuses from dragon-bone and masterwork) plus Moderate fire damage on hit; +Slight vs. demons; Armor-Crack (Slight from masterwork accuracy); silent when sheathed; weighs 2 lbs instead of 4.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Total Cost.** ~15,000 gp if purchased. In practice: not for sale; recovered, inherited, or won at great cost.
}}

## Commissioning Enchantment

{{CoreBody
A player character with coin and time can commission enchantment from an NPC specialist. The process follows Chapter 3's general framework but with specific enchantment considerations:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Finding a Specialist.** Major cities have one or two capable enchanters; capitals have perhaps a dozen. Master-level specialists are rare and often affiliated with specific institutions (guilds, temples, academies). Finding a free one takes a Routine Persuasion check in a capital; Challenging in a city; Near-Impossible elsewhere.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Time.** Weeks to months per working, depending on complexity. Enchanters typically have a queue; a new commission might wait 1–6 months before work begins.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Cost.** Per the cost tables above for infusions and runes, plus the enchanter's service fee (10–25% of the enchantment cost).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Complications.** Enchantment may require specific ingredients the commissioner must supply (a vial of their own blood, hair from a specific creature, a shard of a meteor). These often become adventure hooks.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Failure.** If the enchantment fails (instability check), the commissioner typically pays for materials but is refunded labor. A critically-failed enchantment may damage the item itself.
}}

\page

## Rune-Bound Ammunition and Consumables

{{CoreBody
Individual arrows, bolts, and specific alchemical consumables can carry minor runes. These are single-use variants of longer-lasting enchantments.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Rune-Bound Arrow/Bolt.** A single arrow carrying a minor rune — fire, ice, silver, armor-piercing, etc.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cost: 50 gp per arrow (simple rune) / 200 gp per arrow (complex rune)
Traits: Single-use. Discharges on impact; rune is expended.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Rune-Bound Consumable.** An alchemical item with a minor rune bound in. Example: a rune-bound healing potion that adds Slight extra healing over a standard one.
}}
{{CoreMetadata
Cost: +Moderate to +Major cost bonus over the base consumable
Traits: Single-use. Effects are permanent within the consumable's duration.
}}

## The Lore Problem

{{CoreBody
Every setting has its own theory of how magic works. Some settings treat enchantment as a learnable skill; others treat it as a rare gift held by specific bloodlines or patronized by specific deities. The framework in this chapter accommodates all of these.
}}

{{CoreBody
Where a setting has hard limits — "only dwarves can forge rune-bound weapons"; "enchantment requires a sacrifice of a year of the enchanter's life"; "infusions are forbidden by the Church and punished by burning" — the GM should adapt freely. The framework is a skeleton; the setting is the flesh.
}}

{{CoreBody
Where a setting has specific magical currencies — mana, spell slots, blood points — those should replace or supplement the gp costs listed here. The book uses gp as a proxy for "resources"; the specific resources vary.
}}

\column

## A Note on Named Items

{{CoreBody
A truly legendary item — one that has a name, a history, and a place in the world's story — is not a gear entry. It is a character in its own right, with preferences, consequences, and narrative weight.
}}

{{CoreBody
A GM building a named item should consider:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**What the item wants.** An aggressive weapon wants blood; a guardian wants someone worth guarding; a trickster wants to be wielded unpredictably. The item's preferences shape who it will work fully for.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**What the item costs its wielder.** Legendary items often exact a price — aging, obsession, moral drift, or simply attracting the attention of powerful enemies. The cost should match the item's power.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**How the item is lost.** Legendary items rarely stay with a single wielder forever. They are stolen, inherited, broken, lost in disasters, or reclaimed by ancient enemies. The loss is as much part of the story as the gain.
}}

\page

# Appendix A: System Conversion Guide

{{Epigraph
*"A blade is a blade in any tongue. The numbers change with the teller."*
}}

## How to Use This Appendix

{{CoreBody
Every item in this book is described using generic tiers — damage tiers for weapons, protection tiers for armor — and plain-English difficulty levels for skill checks. This appendix converts those generic values into the specific numbers your game system expects.
}}

{{CoreBody
Three broad families of rules are covered here, named by how each handles armor rather than by any particular product. Identify which family your system belongs to, and use its column on every table that follows. You may cross-reference columns freely; nothing in the book depends on picking one.
}}

## The Three Armor Families

{{CoreHanging
**Style A — Armor as Buffer.** Armor has its own pool of durability. When struck, the armor absorbs the damage until depleted, at which point the wearer begins taking personal damage. Weapons tend to deal larger damage numbers (2d6 or 3d6) to chip through the buffer. Used by Palladium-family systems and several other high-detail rule sets.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Style B — Armor as Class.** Armor changes how hard a character is to hit, not how much damage they take. A strike that connects does full weapon damage; armor's role is to make connections less common. Weapons tend to deal more modest damage (1d6 to 1d10). Used by the oldest school of fantasy systems and their retro-clones.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Style C — Armor as Points.** Every strike that lands has its damage reduced by a flat armor value. A character in heavy plate can still be killed by a single great blow, but the armor softens every hit. Weapons deal roughly the same damage as in Style B, but character hit points are lower, producing a more lethal feel. Used by percentile-based systems in the Basic Roleplaying lineage.
}}

{{note
##### Which Style Is Mine?

If your game uses an **Armor Rating** as a target number and tracks armor damage separately from personal damage, you are Style A.

If your game expresses armor as a descending or ascending **Armor Class** that affects to-hit rolls and has no armor-damage mechanic, you are Style B.

If your game subtracts **Armor Points** from every damage roll, you are Style C.
}}

\page

## The Quick-Reference Cheat Sheet

{{CoreBody
This single page contains everything most tables need during play. Keep it bookmarked.
}}

{{TableTitle
Damage Tier → System Dice
}}

| Tier | Style A | Style B | Style C |
|:--|:-:|:-:|:-:|
| Glancing | 1d6 | 1d4 | 1d4 |
| Solid | 1d8 (or 2d4) | 1d6 | 1d6 |
| Heavy | 2d6 | 1d8 | 1d8 |
| Crushing | 3d6 | 1d10 | 1d10+1 |
| Devastating | 4d6 | 1d12 (or 2d6) | 2d8 |

{{TableTitle
Protection Tier → Armor Value
}}

| Tier | Style A (AR / Durability) | Style B (Class) | Style C (Points) |
|:--|:-:|:-:|:-:|
| Minimal | 9 / 20 | 8 descending / 12 ascending | 1 |
| Light | 11 / 30 | 7 / 13 | 2 |
| Medium | 13 / 50 | 5 / 15 | 4 |
| Heavy | 14 / 70 | 4 / 16 | 6 |
| Extreme | 16 / 90+ | 3 / 17 | 8 |

{{TableTitle
Difficulty Word → System Target
}}

| Word | Style A (Skill %) | Style B (d20 Check) | Style C (Skill Multiplier) |
|:--|:-:|:-:|:-:|
| Trivial | +30% | Auto-success | Skill × 2 |
| Routine | +0% | Roll under attribute | Skill × 1 |
| Challenging | −10% | Roll under attribute, −2 | Skill × ½ |
| Demanding | −25% | Roll under attribute, −4 | Skill × ¼ |
| Near-Impossible | −50% | Roll under attribute, −8 | Skill × 1/10 |

{{TableTitle
Modifier Tier → Numeric Value
}}

| Tier | Percentile Roll | d20 / Integer Roll |
|:--|:-:|:-:|
| Slight | −5% / +5% | −1 / +1 |
| Moderate | −10% / +10% | −2 / +2 |
| Major | −25% / +25% | −4 / +4 |
| Severe | −50% / +50% | −6 / +6 |

\column

## Damage Conversion Details

{{CoreBody
The damage tiers in this book were calibrated against the "typical soldier" assumption: a trained adult in light armor, wielding a weapon of the relevant tier, should kill or disable most opponents in two to four solid hits. Each system achieves this feel differently.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Style A — Chipping Damage.** Characters have two health pools — personal hit points and a secondary durability pool. Weapons tend to empty the secondary pool across several rounds before threatening the character's life. Larger dice (2d6, 3d6) reflect the need to chew through both pools.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Style B — Clean Damage.** A single dice roll handles most weapons. If your ruleset has separate damage for small vs. large targets (such as older retro-clones), use the lower entry as Style B's default and add +2 to the die type for "vs. Large" variants.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Style C — Layered Damage.** Lower hit point totals make every strike matter. Armor Points are subtracted from each damage roll after dice are rolled. A longsword dealing 1d8 against a target with 4 Armor Points may do 1 to 4 damage per hit — or nothing, if the die comes up low.
}}

## Protection Conversion Details

{{CoreHanging
**Style A — Dual-Value Armor.** Each armor tier in this book lists two numbers in Style A's column: an Armor Rating (the target for bypass rolls) and a Durability pool (the armor's own hit points). When the durability reaches zero, the armor is destroyed, and the Armor Rating drops to the wearer's natural rating (typically 5).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Style B — Two AC Conventions.** Both descending AC (lower is better) and ascending AC (higher is better) are given, separated by a slash. Use whichever your ruleset expects. Conversion between the two uses the standard formula: 19 − descending AC = ascending AC.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Style C — Straight Subtraction.** The armor value is subtracted from every incoming damage roll before hit points are deducted. Armor does not degrade in Style C by default; see the optional hit-location rules in Chapter 4 if you want armor to suffer wear.
}}

\page

## Modifier Tiers

{{CoreBody
The modifier ladder appears everywhere in this book. It names bonuses and penalties on rolls, it scales costs and weights for materials and craftsmanship, and it provides a common vocabulary for every mechanic that asks "how much?" without wanting to commit to a specific number.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Slight.** A small nudge, often noticed only at the margin. Examples: a well-balanced weapon's parry bonus, a rain-soaked bowstring's accuracy loss, a boiled-leather jerkin's price over plain leather.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Moderate.** A clearly felt effect that changes decisions. Examples: the agility penalty for wearing chain mail, the extra weight of a stone mace over a wooden one, the cost of pattern-welded steel.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Major.** A significant handicap or edge. Examples: fighting blind, the price of a sky-glass weapon, the weight of a lead-filled sap.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Severe.** A crippling penalty or decisive advantage. Examples: stealth in full plate, the price of a dragon-bone blade, the weight of a solid gold helm that nobody asked for.
}}

{{CoreBody
The tables below convert each tier into numeric values for three common applications.
}}

{{TableTitle
Modifier Tier → Roll Adjustment
}}

| Tier | Percentile Rolls (d%) | Integer Rolls (d20, etc.) |
|:--|:-:|:-:|
| Slight | ±5% | ±1 |
| Moderate | ±10% | ±2 |
| Major | ±25% | ±4 |
| Severe | ±50% | ±6 |

{{CoreBody
Apply the percentage column to any system using percentile skill rolls (most Style A skill checks and all of Style C). Apply the integer column to any system using d20 combat rolls or similar integer-based resolution.
}}

{{TableTitle
Modifier Tier → Cost Multiplier
}}

| Tier | Cost Bonus | Cost Reduction |
|:--|:-:|:-:|
| Slight | ×1.25 | ×0.75 |
| Moderate | ×1.5 | ×0.5 |
| Major | ×3 | ×0.33 |
| Severe | ×10 | ×0.1 |

{{CoreBody
Used when a material, a quality grade, or a market region adjusts the book's default price. Multiply book-listed prices by the appropriate column. Multiple multipliers stack multiplicatively — a Major-bonus material in a Major-bonus region costs ×9 the listed price.
}}

{{TableTitle
Modifier Tier → Weight Multiplier
}}

| Tier | Weight Bonus | Weight Reduction |
|:--|:-:|:-:|
| Slight | ×1.1 | ×0.9 |
| Moderate | ×1.25 | ×0.75 |
| Major | ×1.5 | ×0.5 |
| Severe | ×2 | ×0.25 |

{{CoreBody
Used when a material makes an item heavier or lighter than its default construction. A weight change of a full tier (Major or Severe) may also shift the item's bulk rating by one step in the same direction; see Appendix D.
}}

{{CoreBody
Item entries will say "Moderate Agility penalty" or "Major cost bonus" — never a raw number. When an entry applies a tier to a mechanic, consult the table for that mechanic above.
}}

## Ability Name Translation

{{CoreBody
The six generic ability names used throughout this book map to the closest equivalent in each system family. Where a system groups concepts differently, the translation favors functional use (which ability is rolled against) rather than literal name match.
}}

{{TableTitle
Ability Name Mapping
}}

| This Book | Style A | Style B | Style C |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| Strength | Physical Strength | Strength | STR |
| Agility | Physical Prowess | Dexterity | DEX |
| Endurance | Physical Endurance | Constitution | CON |
| Intelligence | I.Q. | Intelligence | INT |
| Willpower | Mental Endurance | Wisdom | POW |
| Presence | Mental Affinity | Charisma | CHA |

{{CoreBody
When an entry calls for a check using a specific ability — "a Strength check to force the door" — substitute your system's equivalent from the row above. The difficulty word (trivial, routine, etc.) tells you how hard the check is; your system tells you how to roll it.
}}

## Skill Check Abstraction

{{CoreBody
The book names skills generically. Match each to your system's closest equivalent. Where your system has a broader or narrower skill, use the one that overlaps most.
}}

{{TableTitle
Generic Skill Names Used in This Book
}}

| Name | Typical Use |
|:--|:--|
| Acrobatics | Balance, tumbling, leaping |
| Animal Handling | Riding, training, calming |
| Athletics | Climb, swim, jump, run |
| Brewing | Beer, wine, mead, distilling |
| Cooking | Preservation, preparation, flavor |
| Crafting | General-purpose fabrication check |
| Forgery | Document and seal falsification |
| Herbalism | Plant identification, poultices |
| Lockpicking | Mechanical locks, simple puzzles |
| Medicine | First aid, long-term care |
| Navigation | Land or sea, map-reading |
| Perception | Sight, sound, smell |
| Persuasion | Social influence |
| Riding | Mounted combat and travel |
| Sailing | Ship handling, rigging |
| Siegecraft | Operating or directing engines |
| Smithing | Blacksmithing, armoring, weaponsmithing |
| Stealth | Hiding, moving silently |
| Survival | Foraging, tracking, shelter |
| Tanning | Hide preparation, leatherworking |

\column

{{CoreBody
Where a chapter calls for "a Smithing check," any system-specific skill that covers metal fabrication — Armorer, Weaponsmith, Blacksmith, Craft (weapons), Craft (armor) — is the correct choice. When in doubt, pick the narrower skill; GM rulings fill the gap.
}}

## Special Cases and Edge Rules

{{CoreHanging
**Armor Damage in Style B.** Style B does not, by default, track damage to armor. If your table wants that layer, import the Style A Durability numbers and use the optional rule in Chapter 4: when a character is struck for more damage than the armor's tier bonus in a single hit, reduce Durability by the excess.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Hit Locations in Style A and Style B.** Neither style requires hit locations. The piecemeal armor system in Chapter 4 provides location-specific Durability pools for either style when used. Style C's baseline rules already include hit locations in most implementations.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Damage Bonuses.** Style A weapons add a character's Physical Strength bonus on top of the listed dice. Style B weapons do likewise with Strength modifier. Style C weapons use a Damage Bonus tied to combined Strength and Size. In every case, the book's listed tier is the base; your system's character bonuses stack normally.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Critical Hits.** The book does not define critical hit rules. When an entry references a "critical," use whatever your system calls a natural high roll — a maximum weapon damage roll, a doubled damage total, or a called-shot bypass, as appropriate.
}}

\page

## Worked Example: The Longsword Across Systems

{{CoreBody
The longsword is the book's reference weapon. Here is its entry as it appears in Chapter 5, followed by the same weapon rendered into each system family.
}}

{{note
##### As Printed in Chapter 5

**Longsword** — *Heavy one-handed blade, 36" to 44" length*

{{CoreMetadata
Damage: Heavy
Weight: 4 lbs · Bulk: 2
Cost: 90 gp
Traits: Versatile, Balanced
}}
}}

{{CoreHanging
**In Style A.** The longsword deals 2d6 damage plus Physical Strength bonus. It is a one-handed weapon, but may be used two-handed for a Slight damage bonus. The Balanced trait grants a Slight bonus to parry once the wielder has measured their opponent — typically the second round of combat forward.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**In Style B.** The longsword deals 1d8 damage (1d12 versus Large targets, in systems that track that distinction). It is classed as a medium one-handed weapon. The Balanced trait grants a Slight bonus to hit in the first round of combat, representing superior weapon control before either side has gauged the other.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**In Style C.** The longsword deals 1d8 damage plus the wielder's Damage Bonus. It may be wielded one- or two-handed; two-handed use raises damage to 1d10 and disables shield use. The Balanced trait grants a Slight bonus to the Parry skill when using the weapon defensively.
}}

## Worked Example: Chain Mail Across Systems

{{note
##### As Printed in Chapter 4

**Chain Mail (Hauberk)** — *Interlinked steel rings, knee-length, sleeved*

{{CoreMetadata
Protection: Medium
Weight: 30 lbs · Bulk: 3
Cost: 250 gp
Coverage: Torso, upper arms, thighs
}}
}}

\column

{{CoreHanging
**In Style A.** Chain mail provides an Armor Rating of 13 and a Durability pool of 50. The mail protects the torso, upper arms, and thighs; other locations use natural Armor Rating unless piecemeal pieces are added. Imposes a Moderate penalty to Agility-based skills while worn (including stealth, acrobatics, and climbing).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**In Style B.** Chain mail gives AC 5 (descending) or AC 15 (ascending). It counts as medium armor for encumbrance and spellcasting purposes. Imposes a Moderate penalty to stealth-based checks.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**In Style C.** Chain mail grants 4 Armor Points to all covered locations. Covered hit locations: chest, abdomen, upper arms, upper legs. Uncovered locations use natural Armor Points. Imposes a Moderate penalty to Agility-based skills while worn.
}}

## Rescaling Prices

{{CoreBody
The book's default prices are calibrated for a gold-standard economy. Some systems assume much lower coin values. To rescale every price in the book for a low-coin setting — one where a longsword typically costs 15 gp rather than 90 — divide all gp values by 6. For a very high-coin setting, multiply by 2.
}}

{{CoreBody
Scaling does not need to be uniform. Some GMs prefer to keep weapon and armor prices at book default while rescaling lifestyle goods (food, lodging, clothing) independently. A full rescaling framework appears in Appendix B.
}}

## One-Page Summary

{{note
##### Quick Reference — Everything You Actually Need

**Damage:** Glancing / Solid / Heavy / Crushing / Devastating
**Protection:** Minimal / Light / Medium / Heavy / Extreme
**Difficulty:** Trivial / Routine / Challenging / Demanding / Near-Impossible
**Modifier:** Slight / Moderate / Major / Severe
**Abilities:** Strength / Agility / Endurance / Intelligence / Willpower / Presence
**Style A** = armor-buffer rules; use big dice and Durability pools.
**Style B** = armor-class rules; use small dice and AC values.
**Style C** = armor-points rules; use small dice and subtractive AP.
}}

{{pageNumber,auto}}
{{footnote
The Ultimate Provisioner's Handbook — Appendix A: System Conversion Guide

v0.1 working draft
}}

\page

# Appendix B: Master Price List

{{Epigraph
*"The price is the price. What it costs you is another matter entirely."*
}}

## How to Use This Appendix

{{CoreBody
Every priced item in this book appears below, sorted by category and alphabetized within each category. Costs are the book's default — a gold-standard economy calibrated so that a longsword costs 90 gp. See Appendix A for conversion to systems with different pricing conventions.
}}

{{CoreBody
Cross-references to the chapter where each item is fully described are given as (Ch##). This appendix is a lookup reference, not a substitute for the chapter entries — the prose, stat blocks, and traits live in the main text.
}}

## Rescaling Formula

{{CoreBody
To convert book prices to a non-default economy, apply a uniform multiplier. The book's default anchors at longsword = 90 gp.
}}

{{TableTitle
Common Rescaling Multipliers
}}

| Target Economy | Multiplier |
|:--|:-:|
| Very low-coin (longsword 15 gp) | × 0.17 (÷6) |
| Low-coin (longsword 30 gp) | × 0.33 |
| Default | × 1.0 |
| High-coin (longsword 150 gp) | × 1.67 |
| Very high-coin (longsword 300 gp) | × 3.33 |

{{CoreBody
Scaling does not need to be uniform. A GM may keep weapon and armor prices at book default while rescaling lifestyle goods (food, lodging, clothing) independently. Common pattern: raise lifestyle goods relative to combat equipment in a "gritty commoner" setting; the opposite in a "heroic knight" setting.
}}
