\page

# Appendix C: Scavenging & Battlefield Loot

{{Epigraph
*"The battle is lost or won by noon. The real work begins at dusk."*
}}

## How to Use This Appendix

{{CoreBody
The tables below are roll-when-you-need-them references for populating the aftermath of combat. Use them when the party loots bodies, raids a camp, or scavenges a battlefield. The results are deliberately varied — some rolls produce valuable finds, some produce nothing worth carrying, and some produce complications.
}}

{{CoreBody
Every result from these tables is assumed to be in some state of wear. Apply the Condition Table below to any equipment looted. A pristine find is rare; most battlefield loot has seen use.
}}

## The Condition Table

{{CoreBody
When a character recovers a piece of gear, roll d100 to determine its condition.
}}

{{TableTitle
Loot Condition (d100)
}}

| Roll | Condition | Durability | Resale Value |
|:-:|:--|:-:|:-:|
| 1–5 | Masterwork | +1 tier | 150% book |
| 6–30 | Pristine | Full | 100% book |
| 31–60 | Serviceable | ~75% | 60% book |
| 61–85 | Damaged | ~50% | 30% book |
| 86–97 | Badly Damaged | ~25% | 10% book |
| 98–100 | Ruined | 0% (scrap) | Material value only |

{{CoreBody
"Badly Damaged" and "Ruined" gear can often be reforged or scavenged for materials — see Chapter 3 for reforging rules. The scrap value of ruined gear is roughly 10% of the item's material cost.
}}

\column

## Skirmish Aftermath

{{CoreBody
Rolled after a small engagement (5–20 combatants), usually bandits or light-infantry skirmish. Per body defeated, roll once on the following table.
}}

{{TableTitle
Skirmish Bodies (d100 per body)
}}

| Roll | Result |
|:-:|:--|
| 1–20 | Nothing useful. Blood-soaked rags, broken gear. |
| 21–40 | 1d10 cp, common clothing in Damaged condition. |
| 41–55 | 1d6 sp, a belt pouch with flint & steel, a small knife. |
| 56–70 | A working weapon (roll Weapon Type table) in Serviceable condition. |
| 71–80 | Light armor (leather/hide/gambeson) in Serviceable condition. |
| 81–88 | A shield (any type) in Damaged condition. |
| 89–93 | 1d6 gp, a personal item (ring, medallion, letter). |
| 94–97 | A moderately valuable piece (weapon worth 100+ gp, or a quality item). |
| 98–99 | Something unusual (roll on Curiosity table or GM improvises). |
| 100 | Unique: a named item, a sealed letter, a hidden document. |

## Military Battlefield

{{CoreBody
A full battlefield is far richer than a skirmish site, but also far more picked-over by the victors before scavengers arrive. The party's place in the timeline matters.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Immediately After.** Roll on Skirmish Bodies; add +5 to the roll. Victorious soldiers are still looting; most bodies are being stripped by their own side's scavengers.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Hours After.** Roll on Skirmish Bodies normally. Most bodies have been stripped of coin; some have been stripped of armor.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Days After.** Roll on Skirmish Bodies with −20 modifier. Local peasants and camp-followers have been through.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Weeks or Later.** Roll on Skirmish Bodies with −40 modifier. Minimal loot; mostly weather-ruined gear.
}}

\page

## Cavalry Clash Aftermath

{{CoreBody
Rolled after a cavalry engagement. Mounts are the big prize; armor is often damaged by the violence of cavalry combat.
}}

{{TableTitle
Cavalry Clash (d100 per rider + per horse)
}}

| Roll | Per Rider |
|:-:|:--|
| 1–30 | Nothing useful. |
| 31–50 | Basic gear in Damaged condition (weapon or piece of armor). |
| 51–70 | Light-medium armor in Serviceable condition. |
| 71–85 | A sword or lance in Serviceable condition. Knight-quality gear. |
| 86–92 | Knight's purse (2d10 gp) + quality sword or shield. |
| 93–97 | Medium-heavy armor + quality weapon + 5d10 gp. |
| 98–99 | Masterwork weapon or piece of armor; heraldic marking (identifiable). |
| 100 | Unique: a signet ring, a sealed letter, a legendary piece. |

{{TableTitle
Horses Found (d100 per horse)
}}

| Roll | Result |
|:-:|:--|
| 1–30 | Dead or too badly wounded. |
| 31–50 | Alive but wounded (care will restore in weeks). |
| 51–70 | Alive and healthy. A rouncey or palfrey. |
| 71–85 | Alive and healthy. A courser. |
| 86–95 | Alive and healthy. A war-trained horse. |
| 96–98 | Destrier (still skittish; needs handler). |
| 99–100 | Destrier with intact barding. |

## Siege Aftermath

{{CoreBody
A fallen castle or fortified camp contains layered loot: the defender's supplies, the attacker's abandoned gear, the accumulated goods of whoever held the position.
}}

{{TableTitle
Siege Site (d100 per structure searched)
}}

| Roll | Result |
|:-:|:--|
| 1–20 | Nothing useful — already scavenged. |
| 21–40 | Food stores: 3d6 person-days of rations (Damaged, partially spoiled). |
| 41–55 | Weapons rack: 1d4 assorted polearms or swords, Serviceable. |
| 56–65 | Armor store: 1d3 complete sets of light/medium armor. |
| 66–75 | Siege engine intact (roll Ch 12 table) — Damaged but repairable. |
| 76–83 | War chest: 1d6 × 50 gp in mixed coin. |
| 84–90 | Stables with 1d4 unclaimed horses. |
| 91–95 | Armory with masterwork weapon (roll Ch 5 table). |
| 96–98 | Officer's quarters: personal kit (masterwork weapon, quality armor, 100–500 gp, journal or letters). |
| 99 | Hidden cache: 1,000–5,000 gp in silver/gold/jewels. |
| 100 | Unique: a relic, a sealed document of political import, a legendary artifact. |

\column

## Bandit Camp

{{TableTitle
Bandit Camp Inventory (d100)
}}

| Roll | Result |
|:-:|:--|
| 1–15 | Empty. The bandits took everything. |
| 16–30 | Rotted food, bloodstained rags, broken gear. |
| 31–50 | Small stash: 1d6 × 10 gp in mixed coin, 1d4 items from Chapter 14. |
| 51–70 | Working camp supplies: tent, cooking gear, 2d6 days rations, 1d4 weapons. |
| 71–85 | Leader's stash: 1d6 × 50 gp, quality weapon, valuables worth 50-200 gp. |
| 86–93 | Stolen goods: 1d6 × 100 gp worth of trade goods (wool, salt, etc.). |
| 94–97 | Prisoner(s) or kidnapping victim. GM implications. |
| 98–99 | A sealed document implicating a local noble or official. |
| 100 | A legendary piece (often stolen from a recent campaign). |

## Monster Slain

{{CoreBody
Creatures sometimes carry loot; sometimes are the loot. Roll based on the creature type.
}}

{{TableTitle
Mundane Beast (d100)
}}

| Roll | Result |
|:-:|:--|
| 1–60 | Harvestable materials only (see Ch 3 harvesting). |
| 61–80 | Prior victim's gear partially intact nearby. |
| 81–90 | Accumulated shiny objects (raven-type hoard): 1d4 × 20 gp of small valuables. |
| 91–97 | Significant kill's gear: full kit of 1 adventurer (weapons, armor, gold). |
| 98–100 | Unique item attached to the creature's lair. |

{{TableTitle
Intelligent Monster (d100)
}}

| Roll | Result |
|:-:|:--|
| 1–20 | Personal gear and trinkets (50-100 gp worth). |
| 21–50 | Crude weapons + 1d6 × 10 gp + food stores. |
| 51–75 | Quality weapons and armor (light-medium) + 1d6 × 50 gp. |
| 76–90 | Tribal wealth: 1d6 × 200 gp + group valuables. |
| 91–97 | Chieftain's hoard: 1d6 × 500 gp + masterwork item + slaves or prisoners. |
| 98–100 | Unique: ancestral relic, captured legendary piece. |

{{TableTitle
Dragon / Ancient Creature
}}

| Roll | Result |
|:-:|:--|
| — | GM-designed hoard. Use Ch 19 framework. A dragon's hoard is a campaign event, not a random roll. |

\page

## The Weapon Type Table

{{CoreBody
When a result says "roll Weapon Type," use the following quick table.
}}

{{TableTitle
Weapon Found (d20)
}}

| Roll | Weapon |
|:-:|:--|
| 1 | Dagger |
| 2 | Club / mace |
| 3 | Hand axe |
| 4 | Shortsword |
| 5 | Short spear |
| 6 | Hunting bow + arrows |
| 7 | Sling + bullets |
| 8 | War spear |
| 9 | Arming sword |
| 10 | Shortsword or seax |
| 11 | Battle axe |
| 12 | Warhammer |
| 13 | Mace / flanged mace |
| 14 | Longbow + arrows |
| 15 | Light crossbow + bolts |
| 16 | Halberd |
| 17 | Longsword |
| 18 | Heavy polearm |
| 19 | Masterwork version of 17-18 |
| 20 | Exotic weapon (GM picks) |

## The Armor Type Table

{{TableTitle
Armor Found (d20)
}}

| Roll | Armor |
|:-:|:--|
| 1–3 | Padded gambeson |
| 4–6 | Leather armor |
| 7–8 | Studded leather |
| 9–10 | Ring mail |
| 11–13 | Chain shirt |
| 14–15 | Scale or lamellar |
| 16–17 | Light brigandine |
| 18 | Plated mail |
| 19 | Heavy brigandine or partial plate |
| 20 | Full plate (piecemeal — roll hit locations for which pieces survive) |

\column

## Piecemeal Armor Finds

{{CoreBody
A piece of armor scavenged from a body rarely comes as a complete set. Roll d8 for each armor piece to determine which zone.
}}

{{TableTitle
Armor Piece Found (d8)
}}

| Roll | Zone |
|:-:|:--|
| 1 | Helmet |
| 2 | Cuirass (torso front + back) |
| 3 | Left arm / pauldron |
| 4 | Right arm / pauldron |
| 5 | Left vambrace |
| 6 | Right vambrace |
| 7 | Left greave |
| 8 | Right greave |

{{CoreBody
A character looking for complete set matching will typically need to loot multiple engagements. See Chapter 4's piecemeal armor system for the rules on mixing and matching.
}}

## Special Circumstances

{{CoreHanging
**Recent Battle (< 1 hour).** Roll with +10 modifier; bodies still have their coin and personal items. But: surviving combatants may still be nearby.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Old Battle (> 1 week).** Roll with −20 modifier. Weather, local scavengers, and camp-followers have done their work.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Magically Preserved (Tomb, Frozen, Vacuum-Sealed).** Roll with +15 modifier. Conditions preserve gear that would normally have degraded.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Booby-Trapped.** Modifier-based: small chance each roll that a finding triggers a trap. GM discretion.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Cursed.** On a result of 98-100, there is a small chance the item is cursed — valuable but carrying a negative consequence. Legendary items in particular often carry story weight.
}}

\page

## Scavenging Harvestable Materials

{{CoreBody
See Chapter 3's harvesting rules for recovering raw materials from creatures and battlefield wrecks. Abbreviated quick reference:
}}

{{TableTitle
Harvest Yields Quick Reference
}}

| Source | Primary | Secondary | Time |
|:--|:--|:--|:-:|
| Common game (deer, boar) | 1 leather | Meat | Hours |
| Wolf / big cat | 1 pelt | Teeth, sinew | Hours |
| Bear | 2 hide | Meat, claws | Hours |
| Bandit / soldier | Scrap metal, cloth | Boots, tools | Hours |
| Giant snake / lizard | 1 scale-armor | Venom, meat | Hours |
| Wyvern / drake | Scales, claws | Trace dragon-bone | Day |
| True dragon | Multiple dragon-bone items, full scale armor | Various | Days |
| Siege wreckage | Scrap metal, timber, cloth | Occasional finished goods | Days |

{{CoreBody
Self-harvested materials can reduce crafting material costs by Moderate-to-Major amount (see Ch 3). A party planning around this should prepare tools (skinning knives, salt for preservation, coopering gear) in advance.
}}

## A Note on Morality and Legality

{{CoreBody
Looting bodies is almost always legal in the sense that no authority will punish scavengers on a battlefield. It is almost always immoral in the sense that the families of the dead may wish otherwise, that the religious traditions of most settings condemn it, and that the dead had friends who may identify their gear later.
}}

{{CoreBody
Common-sense GM rules: looting identifiable officers may bring reprisal; looting in the presence of grieving survivors is a social offense; looting religious figures is often explicitly illegal. The book provides tables; the GM provides the consequences.
}}

\column

## The "Nothing Useful" Problem

{{CoreBody
Many of the tables above have a significant chance of returning "Nothing useful." This is not a failure of the design; it is by design. Not every body on a battlefield has a treasure. A party that loots methodically will find valuable things; a party that expects every corpse to be worthwhile will be disappointed.
}}

{{CoreBody
GMs who want to avoid excessive "nothing useful" results can add a flat +10 or +20 modifier to all loot rolls — this shifts the distribution toward more finds without eliminating the possibility of empty bodies. A campaign that explicitly uses the tables with a +15 floor has a much more generous treasure economy than the default.
}}

## Sample Loot Rolls

{{CoreBody
Quick examples of how the tables play out.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**A party defeats 5 bandits (skirmish). They roll 5 d100s.**

Rolls: 22, 58, 41, 14, 71.

Results: 22 (common clothing, 1d10 cp), 58 (working weapon: d20=7, sling + bullets), 41 (small pouch with flint & steel, 1d6 sp), 14 (nothing useful), 71 (personal item: a ring worth ~30 gp).

Total haul: 5 cp + 3 sp + 1 sling + 1 ring + some flint & steel.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**A party finds a fallen knight after a cavalry clash. They roll once per rider.**

Roll: 89 — Knight's purse (2d10 gp: 14 gp) + quality sword or shield. GM rules sword (longsword, Serviceable condition — the knight fell and the sword was bent but repairable).

Rolling horse: 68 — alive and healthy courser. The rouncey is recoverable.

The knight had a signet ring (noted on the body, Ch 17) that the party chose to return to the family.
}}

{{pageNumber,auto}}
{{footnote
The Ultimate Provisioner's Handbook — Appendix C: Scavenging & Battlefield Loot

v0.1 working draft
}}

\page

# Appendix D: Encumbrance & Bulk

{{Epigraph
*"Your pack weighs what it weighs. Your fitness decides how much of that weight is a problem."*
}}

## Two Axes: Weight and Bulk

{{CoreBody
The book tracks physical load on two axes: weight and bulk. Weight is literal — how many pounds a character is carrying. Bulk is practical — how much space the things take up, how awkwardly they hang, how much they hamper the wielder in tight quarters.
}}

{{CoreBody
A character carrying heavy gear that travels compactly (a bag of gold coins, a coil of thin rope) has high weight and low bulk. A character carrying light gear that is awkward to maneuver (a ten-foot pole, a pike, a spare shield) has low weight and high bulk. Both can be problems, but they are different problems.
}}

## Weight: Carrying Capacity

{{CoreBody
Carrying capacity scales with a character's Strength. The tables below map Strength scores (using the book's Generic Ability convention; see Appendix A for system mappings) to capacity in pounds.
}}

{{TableTitle
Carrying Capacity by Strength
}}

| Strength | Unencumbered | Light Load | Medium Load | Heavy Load | Maximum |
|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|
| 8 (weak) | 20 lbs | 40 lbs | 60 lbs | 90 lbs | 120 lbs |
| 10 (average) | 30 lbs | 60 lbs | 90 lbs | 120 lbs | 150 lbs |
| 13 (strong) | 40 lbs | 80 lbs | 120 lbs | 180 lbs | 240 lbs |
| 16 (very strong) | 50 lbs | 100 lbs | 150 lbs | 225 lbs | 300 lbs |
| 18 (powerful) | 60 lbs | 120 lbs | 180 lbs | 270 lbs | 360 lbs |
| 20+ (legendary) | 80 lbs | 160 lbs | 240 lbs | 360 lbs | 480+ lbs |

### Load Effects

{{CoreHanging
**Unencumbered.** No effect. Character moves and fights normally.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Light Load.** No effect for short-duration activity. Over long travel days (8+ hours), Slight Endurance penalty.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Medium Load.** Slight Agility penalty on all physical rolls. Moderate Endurance penalty on long travel.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Heavy Load.** Moderate Agility penalty; Moderate Endurance penalty; Slight Speed penalty.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Maximum Load.** Major Agility penalty; Major Endurance penalty; Moderate Speed penalty. Cannot run; cannot charge.
}}

\column

### What Weighs What

{{CoreBody
Common equipment weight cross-reference for quick estimation.
}}

{{TableTitle
Quick Weight Reference
}}

| Category | Typical Weight |
|:--|:-:|
| Coin (100 coins of any type) | 2 lbs |
| Torch | 1 lb |
| Rope (50 ft hemp) | 10 lbs |
| Rope (50 ft silk) | 5 lbs |
| Waterskin (full) | 4 lbs |
| Rations (1 day) | 2 lbs |
| Dagger | 1 lb |
| Shortsword | 2 lbs |
| Longsword | 4 lbs |
| Greatsword | 6 lbs |
| Heavy crossbow | 8 lbs |
| Padded gambeson | 8 lbs |
| Leather armor | 10 lbs |
| Chain shirt | 30 lbs |
| Full chain hauberk | 45 lbs |
| Full plate | 55 lbs |
| Backpack with standard gear | ~40 lbs |
| Pack frame with heavy load | ~80 lbs |

{{CoreBody
A heavily-armored warrior in full plate with sword, shield, and pack easily runs 75-90 lbs. At Strength 13, that's a Heavy Load — enough to matter but manageable. At Strength 10, it's Maximum — the warrior must adjust or suffer significantly.
}}

## Bulk: Physical Awkwardness

{{CoreBody
Bulk measures how much room a piece of gear takes up in practical use — whether it fits through doors, whether it swings in corridors, whether it hangs on the back without flopping. Bulk runs from 0 (imperceptible) to 5 (borderline unmanageable).
}}

\page

{{TableTitle
Bulk Ratings at a Glance
}}

| Bulk | Description | Example |
|:-:|:--|:--|
| 0 | Pocket-sized or trivial | Dagger, ring, scroll |
| 1 | Held in one hand easily | Shortsword, lantern, belt pouch |
| 2 | Takes up space; two-handed or shoulder-slung | Longsword, backpack, chain shirt |
| 3 | Substantial; requires dedicated carry | Greatsword, full plate, pike |
| 4 | Large; awkward in civilian settings | Zweihänder, pavise, barrel |
| 5 | Unwieldy; requires vehicle or mount | Sarissa, siege tower segment |

### Bulk and Tight Quarters

{{CoreBody
Bulk interacts with tight-quarters combat (Chapter 8). The core rule:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Bulk ≤ Corridor Width ÷ 3 feet.** The weapon fits and can be used normally.

**Bulk > Corridor Width ÷ 3 feet.** Moderate penalty to all actions with the item.

**Bulk > Corridor Width ÷ 2 feet.** Major penalty; the item cannot be effectively swung, thrust, or fired.

**Bulk > Corridor Width.** The item cannot enter the space at all.
}}

{{CoreBody
Example: A 5-foot corridor. Weapons up to Bulk 1 (shortsword, cutlass) work fine. Bulk 2 weapons (longsword) suffer a Moderate penalty. Bulk 3 weapons (halberd, pike) cannot be effectively used. Bulk 4+ weapons cannot be swung at all.
}}

### Bulk and Carrying

{{CoreBody
Total bulk carried also matters. A character can openly carry bulk equal to twice their Strength score without appearing "loaded up." Above that, the character looks visibly burdened; Stealth checks suffer penalties; social perception shifts.
}}

\column

## Specific Situations

### Drawing a Weapon

{{CoreHanging
**Bulk 0-1.** One action or part of an attack (no separate draw needed).
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Bulk 2.** One action to draw and ready.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Bulk 3.** One action to draw, one action to ready. (Or one action if held sheathed properly.)
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Bulk 4-5.** Typically carried at the ready already; otherwise two full actions to deploy.
}}

### Swimming

{{CoreHanging
**Light Load or less.** Swim normally.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Medium Load.** Moderate Swim penalty.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Heavy Load.** Major Swim penalty.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Maximum Load.** Cannot swim; will drown unless gear is dropped.
}}

### Running

{{CoreHanging
**Up to Medium Load.** Run at normal speed.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Heavy Load.** Slight Speed reduction; cannot sprint.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Maximum Load.** Moderate Speed reduction; cannot sprint or charge.
}}

### Long-Distance Travel

{{CoreBody
For extended travel (miles per day, Chapter 9), carrying capacity produces cumulative fatigue.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Light Load.** Full daily distance.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Medium Load.** Slight reduction to daily distance. One Endurance check per day; failure imposes additional Slight penalty.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Heavy Load.** Moderate reduction. Endurance checks become Challenging; failure imposes significant penalties.
}}
{{CoreHanging
**Maximum Load.** Major reduction. Most characters cannot sustain Maximum Load for more than a day.
}}

\page

## Hardness (Structural Resistance)

{{CoreBody
Some objects — primarily siege works, ships, fortifications — have Hardness. Damage at or below the Hardness rating is simply ignored. Damage above Hardness applies normally (subtracting Hardness from the damage, in most systems).
}}

{{CoreBody
Hardness is a structural property; it applies to objects, not people. Characters do not have Hardness; they have Durability or Hit Points.
}}

{{TableTitle
Common Hardness Values
}}

| Object | Hardness |
|:-:|:--|
| Glass, pottery | 0 |
| Leather, cloth | 0 |
| Wood (thin) | 3 |
| Wood (heavy, door, shield) | 5 |
| Stone (soft, softwood) | 8 |
| Iron (item-scale) | 10 |
| Steel (item-scale) | 10 |
| Hard wood + iron banding | 10 |
| Heavy iron bars | 15 |
| Stone wall (standard) | 15 |
| Stone wall (castle) | 20 |
| Dragon-bone | 20 |
| Dark-iron | 25 |
| Enchanted materials | varies by setting |

{{CoreBody
Chapter 14's "Breaking Things" table and Chapter 12's fortification table reference these values directly. A character swinging a warhammer (Heavy damage) against an iron-banded oak door (Hardness 10, Durability 80) will chip away at it slowly — most of each strike absorbs into the hardness, and what's left takes a dozen blows to destroy the door.
}}

{{CoreBody
Siege engines, with their × N damage multipliers, are specifically designed to overcome hardness. A trebuchet dealing Crushing × 6 easily breaches a Hardness 15 wall with each shot, even after subtracting hardness.
}}

\column

## Encumbrance Simplifications

{{CoreBody
Many groups prefer not to track encumbrance by the pound. Two simplified approaches:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The "Slots" Method.** Each character has slots equal to their Strength. Any item of significant weight or bulk (Bulk 1+) takes one slot. Some items take multiple slots (e.g., Bulk 3 items take 3 slots). A character carrying more slots than their Strength is Encumbered.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The "Fit or Not" Method.** The GM rules by description. If the character's carry load seems reasonable, it's fine. If not, apply Moderate-to-Major penalties. This method works well in narrative-heavy games where precise tracking detracts from play.
}}

{{CoreBody
Both methods lose precision that the weight-and-bulk system captures, but they run faster at the table. The book's advice: use the full system if gear-management is a focus of your campaign, and use one of the simpler methods otherwise.
}}

## The Mule Problem

{{CoreBody
A fact of low-magic campaigns: adventurers quickly acquire more gear than they can carry. The traditional solution is a pack animal.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Pack Animal Capacities.** A mule carries 250 lbs comfortably; a pony, 200 lbs; a draft horse ridden, 250 lbs; a draft horse pulling a wagon, 2,000 lbs of cargo. See Chapter 9.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Cost of a Mule.** 120 gp plus feed (1-2 sp/day) and stabling. The investment pays for itself quickly if the party is accumulating loot.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Mules in Combat.** A mule in the middle of combat is either fleeing or dead, usually the second. Travel gear on a pack animal is gear the party cannot use in combat without advance preparation.
}}

{{pageNumber,auto}}
{{footnote
The Ultimate Provisioner's Handbook — Appendix D: Encumbrance & Bulk

v0.1 working draft
}}

\page

# Appendix E: The Market — Settlement Tables

{{Epigraph
*"Every village has everything. It just takes longer to find and costs more than you think."*
}}

## Settlement Tiers

{{CoreBody
A settlement's size determines what's reliably available, what can be special-ordered, and what will simply never appear in its markets. The six tiers below form the basis of the availability tables in this appendix.
}}

{{TableTitle
Settlement Tiers (from Ch 3)
}}

| Tier | Population | Description |
|:-:|:-:|:--|
| Thorpe | < 100 | A handful of farms, no real market |
| Hamlet | 100-500 | A small chapel, no shops, occasional traveling merchants |
| Village | 500-1,500 | A weekly market, one smith, an inn |
| Town | 1,500-6,000 | Weekday market, multiple smiths, specialized shops |
| City | 6,000-25,000 | Daily markets, all trades, foreign goods |
| Capital | 25,000+ | Everything, eventually |

## Stock Availability by Tier

{{TableTitle
What's Always Available
}}

| Item Category | Thorpe | Hamlet | Village | Town | City | Capital |
|:--|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|
| Food, local | Always | Always | Always | Always | Always | Always |
| Basic tools | — | — | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Rope, spikes, lanterns | — | — | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Simple clothing | — | — | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Common weapons (<50 gp) | — | Rarely | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Medium weapons (50-200 gp) | — | — | Sometimes | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Heavy weapons (200-500 gp) | — | — | — | Sometimes | Yes | Yes |
| Exceptional weapons (>500 gp) | — | — | — | Rarely | Yes | Yes |
| Leather armor | — | Rarely | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Medium armor | — | — | Sometimes | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Heavy armor | — | — | — | Rarely | Yes | Yes |
| Extreme armor | — | — | — | — | Rarely | Yes |
| Horses (pony, rouncey) | — | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Destriers / war horses | — | — | — | Rarely | Yes | Yes |
| Exotic mounts | — | — | — | — | Rarely | Yes |
| Alchemy (basic) | — | — | Rarely | Sometimes | Yes | Yes |
| Alchemy (advanced) | — | — | — | — | Sometimes | Yes |
| Enchantment services | — | — | — | — | Rarely | Yes |
| Ships (small) | Coast only | Coast only | Coast | Coast | Yes | Yes |
| Ships (ocean-going) | — | — | — | Port cities | Yes | Yes |

{{CoreBody
"Rarely" means on 1d6 = 1 the item is present that day; "Sometimes" means 1d6 = 1-3; "Yes" means reliably available. "—" means effectively never.
}}

\page

## Availability Rolls

{{CoreBody
For items at the margins of a settlement's range, an Availability Roll determines whether the specific item is in stock on the day the party asks.
}}

{{CoreBody
Roll d6 when the table entry says "Rarely" or "Sometimes." The result determines whether the item is currently in stock, whether it can be ordered, and how long an order takes.
}}

{{TableTitle
Availability Roll (d6)
}}

| Roll | Result |
|:-:|:--|
| 1 | In stock now. Full price. |
| 2 | Sold out, but the shop has one on order; 1-2 weeks |
| 3 | Not in town; one of the neighboring towns has it; 2-4 weeks or travel |
| 4 | Would have to be made / commissioned; see crafting (Ch 3) |
| 5 | Not available without connections; GM determines |
| 6 | Can be found through a collector or black market; premium price (Moderate or Major cost bonus) |

## Price Variance by Region

{{CoreBody
Prices vary geographically. The book's default price assumes a neutral, economically ordinary region. The same items may cost significantly more or less in specific regional contexts.
}}

{{TableTitle
Regional Price Modifiers
}}

| Region Type | Cost Modifier | Notes |
|:--|:-:|:--|
| Frontier / poor | −Moderate | Remote, low coin circulation |
| Urban capital | +Slight | Demand exceeds supply |
| War-torn | +Major (military gear); −Slight (everything else) | Local disruptions |
| Trade hub (port, caravan junction) | −Slight | Goods flow through; competition |
| Monopoly town (guild-controlled) | +Moderate | Guild-mandated prices |
| Enchanter's enclave | −Moderate (enchanted); +Slight (mundane) | Abundant magic, scarce mundane |
| Religious pilgrimage site | +Moderate (religious goods); neutral (others) | Demand from visitors |
| Underground / black market | Varies Wildly | ±Severe on specific goods |

{{CoreBody
These modifiers apply to base book prices. A longsword listed at 90 gp would run 135 gp in an urban capital (+Slight), 60 gp on a remote frontier (−Moderate), or 270 gp in a war-torn region (+Major for military gear).
}}

\column

## Caravan Economics (Quick Reference)

{{CoreBody
For campaigns involving trading caravans, the book provides a quick reference for caravan profits, risks, and typical margins.
}}

{{TableTitle
Caravan Trade Margins
}}

| Trade Route | Typical Margin |
|:-:|:--|
| Adjacent towns (same region) | 10-20% |
| Same country, different regions | 25-50% |
| Between kingdoms (diplomatic peace) | 50-150% |
| Between kingdoms (hostile) | 100-500% if deliverable |
| Exotic continent | 200-1000% |

{{CoreBody
Margins are gross; subtract caravan costs (wages, feed, wagon maintenance, tolls, guards). A typical merchant caravan nets 20-40% of gross as actual profit.
}}

### Caravan Costs

{{TableTitle
Monthly Costs for a Mid-Sized Caravan (4 wagons)
}}

| Item | Cost |
|:-:|:--|
| Teamsters (4 × 25 gp) | 100 gp |
| Guards (8 × 30 gp) | 240 gp |
| Cook + laborer | 40 gp |
| Draft animal feed | 80 gp |
| Wagon maintenance | 50 gp |
| Tolls (per region) | 20-100 gp |
| Bribes / tips / unexpected | 50-200 gp |
| **Total monthly operation** | **~600-800 gp** |

{{CoreBody
A caravan with 4 freight wagons carrying 12,000 lbs of trade goods. If the goods cost 5,000 gp at origin and sell for 8,000 gp at destination (60% margin), the trip is profitable — 3,000 gp gross minus 800 gp operation = 2,200 gp net, minus the party's own lifestyle and wagon wear.
}}

{{CoreBody
A caravan catastrophically robbed by bandits loses everything — goods, wagons, sometimes crew. Insurance does not exist in most settings, or exists only among very wealthy merchants and their dedicated brokers.
}}

\page

## Road Hazards

{{CoreBody
Every caravan or overland trade journey faces hazards. The book rolls for encounters at GM discretion, but the tables below provide a reference.
}}

{{TableTitle
Road Encounters (d100 per day of travel)
}}

| Roll | Encounter |
|:-:|:--|
| 1-60 | Nothing significant. |
| 61-70 | Another traveler or small caravan. Typically friendly; may share news. |
| 71-80 | Weather event (heavy rain, storm, blizzard) — slows travel. |
| 81-88 | Bandit scouts observing from cover. Attack on a future day unless driven off. |
| 89-93 | Bandit attack. Strength scaled to caravan size. |
| 94-96 | Toll or checkpoint (legal or illegal). Requires payment or parley. |
| 97 | Broken bridge or washed-out road; detour required. |
| 98 | Sick or dying traveler requests help. |
| 99 | Lost child or lost wagon of another caravan seeking help. |
| 100 | Something truly strange (GM discretion — unusual creature, lost ruin, magical event). |

### Bandit Attack Strength

{{TableTitle
Bandit Attack Scale
}}

| Caravan Size | Typical Bandit Group |
|:-:|:-:|
| Single traveler | 1-2 bandits |
| Single wagon | 3-5 bandits |
| 2-4 wagons | 6-12 bandits |
| 5-10 wagons | 10-20 bandits |
| 10+ wagons | 20+ bandits; organized raiders |

{{CoreBody
Well-defended caravans (with a competent guard complement) usually deter attack — bandits target the weak. A single merchant with one guard is far more attractive prey than a four-wagon caravan with eight guards.
}}

## Finding Specific Items

{{CoreBody
When a player character seeks a specific non-standard item, the GM should consult the Availability table and adjust for circumstance.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Standard Items.** No check required; the item is in stock per the table.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Non-Standard Items.** Availability Roll (d6) plus a Persuasion or Streetwise check at Routine difficulty to find the right shop.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Exotic Items.** Availability Roll plus a Challenging check to identify the specialty shop; plus a Presence or Persuasion check to convince the shopkeep to sell.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Illegal Items.** Successful check locates a black market contact; then a separate Persuasion check to secure the transaction. Risk of entrapment, betrayal, or follow-up crimes.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Unique Items.** GM story-dependent. Sometimes an item can be hunted down through a series of leads; sometimes it is simply unavailable and the party must do without.
}}

\column

## Selling Non-Standard Items

{{CoreBody
The flip side of availability: finding a buyer for something unusual. See Chapter 3's Find-a-Buyer rules. Short version:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Common Items.** Sell readily at 40-60% of retail price (shop margins).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Valuable Standard Items.** Find-a-Buyer check per Chapter 3. Success in a town or city within days; unlikely in a village.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Exotic or Rare Items.** Find-a-Buyer check with Moderate-to-Major modifiers against. Usually requires a city or capital. Specialized collectors may pay premium for specific pieces.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Legendary Items.** Cannot be sold through ordinary channels. Buyers are kings, wizards, guilds, temples — negotiation, not shopping.
}}

## Trade Goods as Wealth

{{CoreBody
In settings where coin is rare or where large sums are impractical to carry, trade goods are often used as currency substitutes. Key categories:
}}

{{TableTitle
Common Trade Goods as Currency Equivalents
}}

| Good | Typical Unit | Approximate Value |
|:--|:-:|:-:|
| Salt | 1 lb | 1-5 gp (highly regional) |
| Wool | bolt (50 yds) | 30-60 gp |
| Fine cloth | bolt | 80-200 gp |
| Silk | bolt | 200-500 gp |
| Grain | bushel | 5 sp |
| Iron | 10 lb ingot | 5 gp |
| Copper | 10 lb ingot | 15 gp |
| Silver | 1 lb bar | 100 gp |
| Gold | 1 lb bar | 1,500 gp |
| Spices (common) | 1 lb | 20 gp |
| Spices (exotic) | 1 lb | 200+ gp |

{{CoreBody
In barter-heavy economies, a party's wealth is often better expressed in trade-goods than coin. A wagonload of fine wool is equivalent to several thousand gp; transporting, selling, and converting it is its own small story.
}}

\page

## Inn and Lodging Prices

{{CoreBody
Often overlooked but essential: where does the party sleep tonight, and what does it cost?
}}

{{TableTitle
Lodging Per Night
}}

| Type | Cost | Notes |
|:--|:-:|:--|
| Hayloft / floor | 1 cp | Rural inn; bring your own blanket |
| Common room bunk | 5 cp | Shared space, meal included |
| Private room (simple) | 3 sp | Bed, basin; meal extra |
| Private room (good inn) | 1 gp | Bed, fire, good meal |
| Private room (fine inn) | 3 gp | Bed, fire, proper linens, multiple meals |
| Suite (merchant quality) | 8 gp | Multiple rooms, service staff |
| Castle guest quarters | 0 (or Favor) | If sponsored by a noble |
| Hostel, monastery | 1-5 sp (donation) | Austere; sometimes offered free |

### Meals at Inns

{{TableTitle
Tavern Meal Pricing
}}

| Meal | Cost |
|:--|:-:|
| Peasant fare (bread, porridge, small ale) | 2 cp |
| Common meal (stew, bread, ale) | 5 cp |
| Hearty meal (meat, vegetables, bread, beer) | 2 sp |
| Good meal (two courses, wine) | 5 sp |
| Fine meal (multiple courses, spirits) | 1 gp |
| Feast (multiple courses, wine, entertainment) | 5+ gp |

### Stabling

{{TableTitle
Horse Care Per Night
}}

| Service | Cost |
|:--|:-:|
| Standing stall (bring your own feed) | 5 cp |
| Full stable (feed included) | 3 sp |
| Covered paddock (several horses) | 2 sp per horse |
| Farrier service | 2 gp |
| Groom service | 2 sp |

\column

## Hired Help and Services

{{TableTitle
Daily Wages
}}

| Worker | Daily Wage |
|:--|:-:|
| Unskilled laborer | 1 sp |
| Domestic servant | 2 sp |
| Porter / torchbearer | 2 sp |
| Teamster | 5 sp |
| Artisan (skilled) | 1-3 gp |
| Sergeant / guard (experienced) | 5 sp |
| Mercenary (common) | 5 sp - 1 gp |
| Mercenary (veteran) | 2-5 gp |
| Scribe / clerk | 3-10 sp |
| Master artisan | 3-10 gp |
| Scholar / specialist | 5-20 gp |
| Sellsword (skilled) | 3-10 gp |
| Bodyguard (specialist) | 5-20 gp |

### Specific Professional Services

{{TableTitle
Professional Services
}}

| Service | Typical Cost |
|:--|:-:|
| Letter writing (professional scribe) | 5 sp per letter |
| Translation (common languages) | 2 gp per page |
| Translation (exotic languages) | 10+ gp per page |
| Legal consultation | 1-5 gp per hour |
| Legal representation (case) | 20-100+ gp |
| Medical care (injury) | 1-20 gp depending on severity |
| Major surgery | 50-500 gp |
| Funeral and burial | 5-50 gp |
| Noble funeral | 100-1,000+ gp |
| Navigational guide (local) | 5 sp/day |
| Navigational guide (distant regions) | 1-3 gp/day |
| Horse and cart hire | 5 sp/day |
| Ship's passage (common) | 5-20 gp per traveler |
| Ship's passage (fine cabin) | 50-200 gp per traveler |

# Appendix F: Curiosities Index

{{Epigraph
*"Most experiments fail. A few fail spectacularly enough to become legends. A smaller few succeed, and those we lose to the fire when the next war comes."*
}}

## How to Use This Appendix

{{CoreBody
This appendix indexes every "curiosity" — experimental, failed, or culturally unique item — scattered throughout the book. These are the strange, the almost-worked, the once-fielded, the frequently-mocked: the equipment that lives in footnotes rather than manuals.
}}

{{CoreBody
The entries below cross-reference the full description in the source chapter. Think of this appendix as a quick-lookup for GMs seeking unusual gear to equip a specific enemy, reward a specific encounter, or flavor a specific culture.
}}

## Armor & Shield Curiosities (Chapter 4)

{{CoreHanging
**The Kalkan (Reed Shield).** Crimean Tatar light cavalry shield of woven willow reeds and silk. Light, fast, exceptional against arrows.

**Stats:** Parry Moderate, Durability 40, 3 lbs, 90 gp. Moderate bonus vs. arrows. Slight Durability penalty vs. heavy edged weapons.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Hungarian Tartsche (Winged Shield).** Asymmetric shield with raised "wing" protecting the rider's neck and shoulder.

**Stats:** Parry Moderate, Durability 70, 6 lbs, 140 gp. Moderate bonus vs. head/neck strikes.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Lantern-Shield.** Italian experimental buckler with shuttered lantern, sword-breaker rim, and disarm hook.

**Stats:** Parry Slight, Durability 40, 4 lbs, 250 gp. Dazzle effect when lantern unshuttered. Moderate Disarm bonus.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Zertsalo (Mirror-Plate).** Russian polished steel chest-plate worn over existing armor.

**Stats:** +20 Torso Durability, Slight Protection upgrade, 4 lbs, 140 gp. Dazzle effect in sunlight.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Zertsalo-Shield (Mirror Array).** Defensive shield covered in polished steel plates for sun-reflection.

**Stats:** Parry Slight, Durability 90, 14 lbs, 300 gp. Moderate penalty to enemy attacks from front in bright sun. Wielder highly visible.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Tarkh (Gun-Shield).** Shield with built-in matchlock pistol barrel.

**Stats:** Parry Slight, Durability 50, 10 lbs, 400 gp. Solid damage on single shot at Close range. 3 actions to reload.
}}

\column

## Melee Weapon Curiosities (Chapter 5)

{{CoreHanging
**The Sword-Breaker.** Dagger-length parrying weapon with deeply-notched edges for catching blades.

**Stats:** Glancing (−Slight) damage, 1.5 lbs, 55 gp. Parrying and Entangling. Moderate Disarm bonus.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Cinquedea.** Five-finger-wide Italian dagger, approaching shortsword length. Designed for thrusts in tight quarters.

**Stats:** Solid damage, 2 lbs, 40 gp. Vitals trait, Quick. No tight-quarters penalty.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Tsar's Gauntlet.** Armored gauntlet with spring-loaded stiletto concealed in forearm casing.

**Stats:** Glancing damage on trigger, +2 lbs on gauntlet, 300 gp. Moderate Strike bonus on first-round surprise. Jam risk on failed Agility check.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Hook-Sword (Shuang Gou).** Chinese paired weapon with hooks and crescent guards.

**Stats:** Heavy, 3 lbs each, 250 gp for pair. Finesse, Hooking, Entangling. When linked: Reach Moderate.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Kukri.** Forward-angled Himalayan tool-weapon with characteristic belly-heavy cut.

**Stats:** Solid (+Slight vs. unarmored), 1.5 lbs, 35 gp. Quick, Brutal. Thrown Range 15 ft.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Macuahuitl.** Mesoamerican sword-club of wood inlaid with obsidian edges.

**Stats:** Heavy (Crushing on first strike), 4 lbs, 60 gp. First strike ignores one Protection tier; edges require re-knapping after use.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Atgeir.** Norse hybrid polearm-sword of uncertain historical form.

**Stats:** Heavy damage, 5 lbs, 120 gp. Two-Handed, Reach Slight, Versatile between thrust and cut.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Flamberge.** Wavy-bladed two-handed sword producing prolonged-bleeding wounds.

**Stats:** Crushing, 7 lbs, 400 gp. Slight Endurance penalty per wound for one scene.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Urumi.** Flexible whip-sword of coiled steel; Indian origin.

**Stats:** Solid, 1.5 lbs, 200 gp. Finesse, Entangling, Reach Moderate. Requires specialized training (Major penalty otherwise). Can strike around corners.
}}

\page

## Polearm Curiosities (Chapter 6)

{{CoreHanging
**The Flamberge Pike.** Wavy-bladed pike producing bleed-wounds on withdrawal.

**Stats:** Pike base + Slight bleed per wound for one scene. +Major cost bonus.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Triple-Head Pike.** Experimental pike with three parallel pike-heads on one shaft.

**Stats:** Pike base; +Slight to-hit on braced charge but Moderate Agility penalty otherwise. +Moderate cost bonus.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Gun-Spear.** Short spear with built-in matchlock pistol.

**Stats:** As short spear in melee; Solid single shot at 30 ft range. 350 gp, +3 lbs over base spear.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Man-Catcher.** Spring-loaded U-shaped head on pole, for non-lethal capture.

**Stats:** Glancing contact only, 8 lbs, 60 gp. Grapple rather than strike. Target held against Demanding Strength escape.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Couteau de Brèche.** French short-polearm with wide cleaving blade, designed for storming breaches.

**Stats:** Crushing, 5 lbs, 110 gp. Two-Handed, Brutal. No tight-quarters penalty.
}}

## Ranged Weapon Curiosities (Chapter 7)

{{CoreHanging
**The Slur (Bullet-Bow).** Crossbow with split string for firing pellets.

**Stats:** Solid, 4 lbs, 110 gp. Silent release. Slight Stun bonus. Cannot fire bolts.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Multi-Bolt Organ Crossbow.** Theoretical siege defense with multiple parallel prods.

**Stats:** Solid per bolt; 1d4 bolts per shot; 40 lbs mounted; 500 gp. Fails after 3 uses historically.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Spring-Flinger (Hand Ballista).** Portable torsion-powered launcher.

**Stats:** Crushing (+alchemical), 35 lbs, 450 gp. Two-person crew. Torsion failures in cold.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Blowpipe.** Silent dart-launcher; ideal poison delivery.

**Stats:** Glancing dart, 1 lb, 25 gp. Silent; darts hold one contact-poison dose each.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Yumi (Asymmetric Bow).** Japanese longbow with grip below center for mounted/kneeling use.

**Stats:** Heavy, 2.5 lbs, 200 gp. No mounted penalty. Moderate penalty for standard archery stance.
}}

\column

## Naval Curiosities (Chapter 11)

{{CoreHanging
**Greek Fire Siphon.** Alchemical incendiary projector; adheres on contact, cannot be extinguished by water.

**Stats:** Devastating + Heavy continuing fire, Range 60 ft, 1,500 gp weapon + 200 gp per charge. Crew of 2.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Turtle Ship (Geobukseon).** Korean armored galley with iron shell and spikes preventing boarding.

**Stats:** 100-130 crew, Durability 600, 12,000 gp. Major bonus vs. ranged and boarding. Multiple cannon.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Treasure Ship (Baochuan).** Massive oceangoing wooden ship, historical largest wooden vessels.

**Stats:** 2,000 tons, 300-500 crew, Durability 2,500, 80,000+ gp. Deep draft.
}}

## Siege Curiosities (Chapter 12)

{{CoreHanging
**The Fire-Wheel.** Massive burning wheel rolled downhill.

**Stats:** Crushing × 3 + Heavy fire, Durability 100, 80 gp. Requires 10°+ slope. 30% chance of straying off-course per 100 yards.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Fire-Pig.** Pitch-coated pig set alight and driven toward siege works.

**Stats:** 3d6 crushing + 3d6 fire per round contact. 30% chance per round of running the wrong way.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Wall-Crane (Archimedes' Claw).** Harbor-wall crane for grappling attacking ships.

**Stats:** Range 60 ft, Durability 400, 2,500 gp. Grappled ships subject to winch capsize (Strength 50).
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Great Trebuchet (Warwolf-Class).** Historical mega-trebuchet launching half-ton projectiles.

**Stats:** Devastating × 8, Range Extreme, 30,000 lbs, 15,000+ gp + months of construction.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Polybolos (Repeating Ballista).** Hellenistic chain-driven magazine ballista.

**Stats:** Heavy × 2 per shot, Range Medium, 500 lbs, 1,400 gp. 3 shots per round; 10-bolt magazine.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Helepolis.** Hellenistic siege tower-of-towers with multi-story ballista batteries.

**Stats:** 50+ fighters, Durability 2,500, 40,000+ gp. Needs 80+ pushers.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Guliai-Gorod.** Eastern European mobile wall sections, pushed forward to create mobile cover.

**Stats:** Per-section 400 lbs, Durability 180, 150 gp. Major cover to those behind.
}}

\page

## Firearm Curiosities (Chapter 13)

{{CoreHanging
**The Puckle Gun.** 18th-century experimental multi-shot firearm with rotating cylinder.

**Stats:** Solid per shot; 9-chamber cylinder; 40 lbs mount; 2,000 gp. 15% misfire per shot.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Flash Bomb.** Clay-pot grenade with black powder and shrapnel.

**Stats:** Heavy damage, 1.5 lbs, 40 gp. Range 30 ft. 2-second fuse; area radius 10 ft.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Flame Projector (Cheng-Huang).** Chinese experimental pump-action oil sprayer.

**Stats:** Heavy fire + continuing, Range 30 ft, 20 lbs, 800 gp. 10-shot reservoir; catches fire if struck.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Organ Gun (Ribauldequin).** Multi-barrel volley weapon on wheels.

**Stats:** Heavy per barrel, 10-30 barrels total. Fires once, reloads 5 rounds. 800 lbs, 2,500 gp.
}}

## Vehicle Curiosities (Chapter 10)

{{CoreHanging
**The Scythed Chariot.** Heavy war chariot with axle-mounted scythe blades.

**Stats:** 4 horses, Durability 150, 1,200 gp. Scythes deal Crushing to adjacent infantry on charge. Easily overturned.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The War-Wagon (Hussite Tabor).** Armored freight wagon with raised flaps and platform for shooters.

**Stats:** 10-man crew, Durability 350, 1,800 gp. Major cover when fortified. Linkable into mobile fortress.
}}

## Sidelong Mentions

{{CoreBody
Some curiosities appear as flavor in the book's prose rather than as stat-blocked items. They deserve mention here for completeness:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Fire-Pig (variant).** A defensive siege tactic where multiple pigs were coated in pitch, lit on fire, and driven toward attacking siege works. Unpredictable and cruel; effective when lucky; remembered mostly for the unnatural screams.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Plague Corpses in Trebuchets.** The fully-historical tactic of hurling infected bodies into besieged cities. An early form of biological warfare; ethically disturbing; effective at undermining morale and sometimes at transmitting disease.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**The Mongol Silk Undershirt.** Not a curiosity exactly, but a surprising real practice: Mongol light cavalry wore silk shirts under their armor. Silk stopped no arrows, but when an arrow penetrated, the silk wrapped around the arrowhead and allowed cleaner extraction.
}}

\column

## Fantasy Material Curiosities

{{CoreBody
The fantasy materials of Chapter 2 are themselves worth listing as curiosities, since each produces an item class unique to its tradition.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Dark-Iron Items.** Heavy, fire-absorbing, unreforgeable. Used for heirloom plate and blades that never tarnish. Cannot be worked after initial forging.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Star-Silver Items.** Lightweight, silver-propertied, glows in moonlight. Particularly prized for swords, jewelry, and ceremonial gear.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Sky-Glass Items.** First-strike armor-piercing; fragile. Often used for a single, dramatic engagement.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Dragon-Bone Items.** Elemental affinity from source; politically and spiritually charged. Carrying a fire-drake's bone in fire-drake territory is itself a provocation.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Deep-Salt Crystal Items.** Dehydration effect on wounds; dissolves in water; salt-desert-origin exotics.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Moon-Silver Items.** Elven-reforged silver with hardness matching steel. Glows in moonlight; requires elven smiths for repair.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**Living-Wood Items.** Druidically shaped from sacred trees without killing the parent. Self-repairs; fireproof; refuses to harm druids.
}}

## Use-Case Summary

{{CoreBody
A GM looking to outfit a unique encounter can browse this appendix for instant flavor. Some suggestions:
}}

{{CoreHanging
**An Eastern European enemy.** Kalkan, Hungarian tartsche, Zertsalo, bardiche, Guliai-Gorod. Specific regional arsenal.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**A renaissance-era duel.** Cinquedea, sword-breaker, lantern-shield, rapier. Dueling arsenal with experimental flair.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**A technologically-inclined villain.** Puckle gun, gun-shield, gun-spear, organ gun, multi-bolt crossbow. The failed-experiment collection.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**An ancient-Greek-flavored setting.** Sarissa, Polybolos, Helepolis, Dromon, trireme. Classical arsenal.
}}

{{CoreHanging
**An exotic eastern visitor.** Urumi, shotel, kriss, khopesh, chakram, blowpipe. Global weapons tour.
}}
